# Decisions and open questions

Everything settled in the first two reviews, in one place. **Decided** entries are not up for re-litigation without a new reason; **open** entries still need a call.

---

## 1. Write_On feature set — decided

The full list is preserved in [WRITEON.md](WRITEON.md). Manual alternatives, ghosting, and the stash are [Phase 3.5](ROADMAP.md#phase-35-v035-alternatives); AI alternatives and the Lab are [Phase 4](ROADMAP.md#phase-4-v04-writing-partner). Features are catalogued in [FEATURES § U](FEATURES.md#u-alternatives-ghosting-stash-and-the-lab). No one-click posting to X or LinkedIn.

---

## 2. Who the deployment is for — decided

One instance per household or small team. Many users, projects private by default, no tenancy boundary beyond project membership. A non-member sees nothing about a project.

---

## 3. Editing surface — decided

**A rich text editor, with the Markdown always available.**

- The writing surface is rich text: headings, emphasis, lists, quotes, links, and images are rendered as you type.
- A **Source** view shows the exact Markdown that will be stored and exported, and can be edited directly.
- Markdown remains the storage and interchange format. The rich surface is a view of it, never a separate document model.
- The editor is **ProseMirror** with a Markdown parser/serializer, chosen for its schema and decoration APIs (needed for alternatives, ghosting, and comments). It is bundled once in CI; there is no npm at runtime.
- Every rich-text construct has a Markdown representation. Unknown Markdown is preserved as source text, never dropped.

---

## 4. Source of truth — decided

The database is canonical. The on-disk Markdown mirror is a generated projection. Git pushes the mirror. The only path from a remote back into Scríobh is an explicit import.

---

## 5. OpenCode API — decided

The provider is the [OpenCode API](https://opencode.ai/docs/api), not a model gateway.

- Base URL is the user's OpenCode instance.
- Models come from `GET /api/model`.
- One-shot actions use `POST /api/experimental/generate/text` (stateless, no tools).
- Chat uses the session routes, not the agent's shell, PTY, filesystem, VCS, or MCP routes.

---

## 6. Local runtime — decided

Ollama is the recommended local default. Any OpenAI-compatible endpoint also works.

---

## 7. Collaboration depth in v1 — decided

Comments, suggestions, roles, versions, and share links. No real-time co-editing.

---

## 8. Export engine — decided

Native Markdown, HTML, DOCX, and EPUB. Optional Pandoc for exotic formats. Print PDF through paged HTML and headless Chromium, with a browser-print fallback.

---

## 9. What "blog" means — decided

Static export: HTML and RSS into a folder the writer serves. Not a hosted CMS and not a public comment surface.

---

## 10. Mobile and desktop — decided

A PWA only. No separate mobile apps. The single exception is the future Tauri desktop shell around the same binary.

---

## 11. Auth — decided

OIDC only, with the existing group mapping retained: `scriobh-admins` (instance admin), `scriobh-writers` (create projects), `scriobh-readers` (only invited projects). No local passwords, TOTP, or reset flow. The first login may claim admin only when no admin group is configured, and that claim closes after first use.

---

## 12. Repository and publishing — decided

A private Forgejo repository to start. A **publish to GitHub/Forgejo** flow comes later, in the spirit of Weave's publishing: create or select a repository, choose visibility, push the mirror. It is not part of the first release.

---

## 13. Name — decided

**Scríobh** with the accent where the name is displayed. The ASCII slug **scriobh** is used for URLs, the binary, package names, and the data directory. Both are final.

---

## 14. Default AI posture — decided

AI is invisible until a provider is configured. A project can be `local-only` before any provider exists. No AI button on a fresh install.

---

## 15. Encryption — decided

**The app does not encrypt manuscript data, exports, the mirror, the database, or backups.** No encryption is implied anywhere in the interface or the docs.

- Provider credentials and Git keys are the only encrypted values, because storing them in plaintext is worse. That is an implementation detail, not a feature promise.
- Operators protect the volume and the backups (LUKS, ZFS, host disk encryption, or whatever they already run).
- A future **password-protect viewing a project** feature is an application-level gate, not encryption. It is planned as a later addition.

---

## 16. v0.1 scope — decided

Phase 1 as written: a usable single-project editor with the tree, search, export, themes, and mobile. AI and comments wait.

---

## 17. Hosting and TLS — decided

No built-in ACME, no built-in TLS. The reverse proxy and certificates are the administrator's responsibility. `SCRIOBH_BASE_URL` is configurable in the application, with a sane default (`http://localhost:8080`), and is used for cookies, links, and OIDC callbacks.

---

## 18. Visual direction — decided

**A. Paper** is the baseline: warm paper, moss accent, manuscript tree, 316 px inspector, and a shelf of typographic covers. The other four directions are archived in git history. The current sheets are in [wireframes/](../wireframes/README.md).

---

## 19. Write_On PDF — resolved

Received as text and preserved in [WRITEON.md](WRITEON.md). No further action.

---

## 20. Git commit identity — decided

`Scríobh <username@scriobh>` by default, where `username` is the project owner's local username. If the project sets an author name and email, those are used instead. No hostname in the message.

---

## 21. Git importer defaults — decided

Sane defaults, documented: a Scríobh mirror via `scriobh.json`, or a generic Markdown folder (folders → parts, `NN-title.md` → chapters, headings → scenes). The mapping is previewed before anything is written. No other layout detection.

---

## 22. EPUB import — decided

Reflowable EPUB 2 and 3, spine order, metadata, cover, images, footnotes, and links. No DRM, no fixed-layout, no CSS reproduction. The original file is kept and hashed.

---

## 23. Desktop wrapper — decided

Tauri 2 first, Electron only as a documented fallback. Linux and macOS ship first; Windows is required before v2.0 is done. Embedded OIDC issuer on loopback with a single local profile.

---

## 24. Push-only — decided

Scríobh never pulls. If the remote changed, the choices are force-push (explicit UI action, typed confirmation, local bundle) or a new import. There is no merge path.

---

## 25. Alternatives in Markdown — decided

The chosen text is canonical Markdown. Alternatives are written as one trailing HTML comment per document:

```markdown
<!-- scriobh:alts {"version":1,...} -->
```

Inline `[a|b]` syntax is rejected: it is unreadable outside the app and fragile in prose. A sidecar `.alts.json` is the fallback for very large sets.

---

## 26. Ghosted words in the count — decided

**Ghosted words do not count as words.** They remain in the document and are exported by default, but the word count excludes them. A separate raw count can be shown for reference. Ghosting is a visual state, not a deletion, and reviving restores the words to the count.

Ghosting itself is provisional: it ships in Phase 3.5, and it can be removed later without touching the document model, because it is only an annotation.

---

## 27. Cross-project entities — decided

**Decided:** one name, **Compendium**, everywhere. No World/Compendium switching.

- A Compendium holds entities, research, clippings, timeline, glossary, and rules.
- A Compendium is a first-class library item and can exist **without any project** — built for future books, shared as reference, or used on its own.
- A project has **at most one primary Compendium** and may have none.
- A project can **reference** material from other Compendiums (entities, research, timeline) read-only, or copy it into the primary.
- Entities are Compendium-level by default, with optional project-local entities.
- A project can override fields of a shared entity (age at this point, role, appearance) without changing the Compendium record.
- Compendium membership is explicit and separate from project membership, so a beta reader on one book cannot read the whole bible.
- An entity shows "appears in" across every project, with first appearance per project.

**Implementation note:** the database uses `compendium_id` throughout. Projects carry a nullable `compendium_id` for the primary, and `compendium_refs` records references to others.

---

## 28. Fiction DOCX presets — confirmed

Ship two presets: **US Standard Manuscript (Shunn)** and a **UK manuscript** variant. Times New Roman or Courier New, 12 pt, double-spaced, 1-inch margins, title page with contact details and word count, running header with author/title/page, chapters on new pages, `#` scene breaks, 0.5-inch first-line indent, no extra paragraph spacing. Use real Word styles and header fields, not direct formatting. A compile preview comes before the file.

The preset options remain editable: font, size, spacing, margins, header format, scene-break style, chapter page breaks, indent.

---

## 29. SQLite at manuscript scale — confirmed

**Decision:** keep SQLite. A 1M-word manuscript is only a few megabytes of text; the database stays in the hundreds of megabytes with pruned history. The real risks are single oversized documents, revision growth, and huge exports, and those are handled with guardrails and benchmarks.

`scriobh bench` generates 150k, 500k, and 1M-word projects and gates releases on chapter open, save, search, compile, and database size. If the pure-Go driver ever misses the budgets by a wide margin, the documented escape hatch is CGO SQLite; if only FTS is the limit, an external index behind the search interface. Postgres remains out of scope unless the product grows multiple writers or real multi-tenancy.

---

## 30. Agent CLI and `--skill` — decided

**Decided:** agents use the same op registry through a CLI, with no separate agent API.

- `scriobh --skill` (alias for `scriobh skill show`) prints the agent skill: purpose, object model, safe workflow, recipes, error codes, and the schema hash.
- `scriobh skill install` writes it into a detected agent's instruction file inside marked blocks; `scriobh skill check` reports drift against the server.
- The skill is generated from the op registry plus hand-written guidance. `make skill` regenerates `skills/scriobh/**` and `internal/cli/snapshot.json`; a CI test fails if the committed output is stale.
- The CLI is name-based (`--compendium`, `--project`, `--entity`), returns JSON on request, uses stable exit codes, supports `--dry-run`, `--yes`, and idempotency keys.
- Keys are scoped to read/write/admin and can be restricted to one Compendium or one project.

**Also decided:** `scriobh --skill` prints the full skill, and `scriobh --skill <topic>` prints one recipe (`compendium`, `story`, `review`). The full skill remains the default.


---

## 31. Writing analytics and goals — planned for v1.2

**Decided:** the goal, streak, and analytics layer ships as **Phase 7.5 — v1.2 "Writing Studio"**, not mixed into the earlier editorial phases.

- Daily, weekly, and monthly goals; project and custom goals.
- Streaks and a 24-week calendar; no red missed days.
- Basic counts plus words over time, pace, productive days and times.
- Project goal dashboard, pace forecast, sprint stats, milestones.
- Optional SMTP progress email, off by default.
- All analytics local; no third-party tracker.
- Social sharing of snippets is not included.

The reference is the set of screenshots supplied by the maintainer and stored in [`docs/reference/novlr-writing-studio/`](reference/novlr-writing-studio/), not the current live site. The details and boundaries are in [RESEARCH.md § 5](RESEARCH.md#5-novlr-writing-studio-earlier-product).


---

## 32. Phase 0 identity — decided

**Decided:** build against a **stub OIDC provider**, deploy against **Passage**.

- The stub is a real OIDC issuer (discovery, authorization, token, JWKS) used in development and CI. It exercises the same client code path as production; there is no auth bypass.
- It is dev-only: enabled by a flag, refused when the base URL is public, and omitted from production builds.
- Production registers Scríobh as an OAuth/OIDC application in **Passage** (`https://github.com/crueber/passage`), with callback `${SCRIOBH_BASE_URL}/auth/oidc/callback`, scopes `openid profile email`, and the `groups` claim mapped to `scriobh-admins`, `scriobh-writers`, and `scriobh-readers`.

---

## 33. Goals and streaks — decided

**Decided:** goals count **words added**, not net words. Cutting 500 words is work; the net change is shown separately, never used to judge the day.

- A **streak day** is a day the daily goal was met (words added at or above the target), not merely a day with any writing.
- The day boundary is the user's timezone.
- No red days, no streak-shaming, no "productivity decreased" warning.

---

## 34. Compendium access on collaborator add — decided

**Decided:** when someone is added to a project that has a primary Compendium, Scríobh **prompts** to add them to that Compendium too.

- Default **yes** for editors.
- Default **no** for reviewers and readers.
- Nothing is granted silently. Declining leaves them with project-local entities only; the manuscript stays editable.


---

## 35. Phase 0 build decisions — decided

Settled before the first line of code, so the plan and the repository agree.

- **HTTP package is `internal/server`.** `AGENTS.md` already verifies `./internal/server -run Authz`; `internal/web` is dropped from the layout. The top-level `web/` directory is itself a Go package (`web/embed.go`) because `//go:embed` cannot reach a parent directory.
- **One issuer package, two shapes.** `internal/oidcstub` is mounted in-process at `/dev-idp/` (same origin as the app) and wrapped by `cmd/scriobh-dev-idp`. It is wired into the server only in files without the `prod` build tag; `make build` and the image use `-tags prod`. The dev compose stack builds without the tag.
- **Lazy discovery.** The OIDC client resolves the issuer on first use and retries; startup never depends on the network. The base URL is derived from the bound listener when the port is ephemeral.
- **Listen default is loopback.** The binary defaults to `127.0.0.1:8080`; the container image sets `SCRIOBH_LISTEN=:8080`. `127.0.0.1:0` picks an ephemeral port and prints it (`LISTEN http://127.0.0.1:<port>`), optionally also to `SCRIOBH_PORT_FILE`.
- **Roles live on the user.** `users.role` (`admin`, `writer`, `reader`) replaces `is_admin`; it is recomputed from groups at every login, except that a first-run admin claim persists while no admin group is configured.
- **CSRF** uses the standard library's cross-origin protection (`Sec-Fetch-Site` and `Origin`) on every mutation, plus a per-session token on cookie-authenticated UI routes.
- **`make skill` exists from Phase 0.** `cmd/scriobh-skillgen` writes `internal/cli/snapshot.json` and `skills/scriobh/SKILL.md` from the registry; a test fails when they drift. The recipes arrive with the CLI in Phase 3.
- **The editor round trip** is a Go test that drives the committed JS bundle with Node over a shared golden corpus. It fails, never skips, when Node is absent in CI.
- **CI** workflows are in the repository. `make ci` runs the same steps locally.

---

## 36. Decisions from the Phase 0 review — decided

- **Development identity is port-independent.** Development users are stored under the fixed issuer `urn:scriobh:dev-idp`, not the issuer URL, because the URL carries the bound port. A production issuer URL is kept exactly as configured (no trimming); a mismatch with what the provider reports is a configuration error, not "unreachable".
- **The development issuer needs both a loopback base URL and a loopback listen address.** It has no credentials, so anyone who can reach the port can sign in as an admin. The dev compose stack listens on `:8080` inside the container and sets `SCRIOBH_DEV_ALLOW_ANY_LISTEN=1`, with the host port published to `127.0.0.1` only.
- **Project slugs are unique per owner.** A page route resolves a slug among the projects the caller can open, so choosing a title never reveals another person's project.
- **Removed from every IdP group means sessions end at the next sign-in attempt**, not at cookie expiry; a removed account is refused, never silently recreated. While no admin group is configured, an admitted user keeps their role, so the first user who skips the claim screen can come back and still claim.
- **Ops carry an API-key scope** (`read`, `write`, `admin`; defaulted from the method), and keys pinned to a project or Compendium can only call ops that name a project. The registry enforces both, so a Phase 3 key cannot be forgotten at an individual op.
- **Ops whose input names a child object** (a document, a comment) declare `RequireProjectVia` with a resolver that looks up the owning project. Handlers must still scope their queries by the resolved project.
- **Events and audit rows join the content transaction** through `Ctx.FlushTo(tx)`, so a crash cannot commit a change without its event. Ops that do not need atomicity may simply `Emit`.
- **API shape:** `413 too_large` for oversize bodies, `405 method_not_allowed` with `Allow`, JSON for cross-site refusals, trailing data after a JSON body is refused, and `X-Forwarded-For` uses the rightmost entry when the proxy is trusted. Rate-limit buckets are per signed-in user, falling back to the client address.
- **Files are private by default:** the process umask is `077` and the data directory is forced to `0700`.
- **Paper `--muted` is `#726D63`** (the documented `#7A756B` measured 4.39:1 on `--bg` and 4.09:1 on `--surface-2`, below the 4.5:1 rule).
- **Known and deferred:** nested `WithTx` on the single writer connection would deadlock (no code nests today; guard when Phase 1 adds multi-step ops); `project.list` has a 500-row cap and no cursor yet; the authz sweep covers REST ops, and UI routes are covered by the end-to-end and smoke tests.

---

## 37. Editor spike decisions — decided

Full detail in [EDITOR-SPIKE.md](EDITOR-SPIKE.md). The ones that shape later phases:

- **Canonical Markdown:** `*em*`, `**strong**`, `~~strike~~`, `-` bullets, ATX headings, fenced code, a backslash hard break, LF only, one trailing newline. A scene break is `* * *` (every thematic-break spelling is read as one); the `#` spelling belongs to the export presets, not the stored text.
- **Preserved, never dropped:** raw HTML and comments (including the alts comment), tables, front matter, `:::` blocks and link reference definitions come back byte for byte as a visible source block. Tables are source-only until a later phase.
- **First rich edit normalises** setext headings, `*` and `+` bullets, reference-style links, indented code and entities; soft line breaks and empty paragraphs are not preserved. Literal punctuation in prose is written with backslash escapes (`\[sic\]`), which also shows in the mirror.
- **Emphasis hugging punctuation inside a word** (`a*(b)*`) cannot round-trip under CommonMark; those marks come back as literal asterisks.
- **Security under the app CSP:** pasted `style=""` attributes are rewritten to data tokens before any DOM parse (Chromium refuses them under `style-src 'self'`); remote images are drawn as alt text and never given a `src` by the editor; obfuscated `javascript:` link schemes are refused.
- **Loading:** `editor.js` (135.7 KB gzip against a 180 KB budget) and `editor.css` load only on document routes. The host must call `markSaved(version)` after a save or the editor stays dirty and refuses `setMarkdown`.
- **Not verified:** real devices (iOS and Android IME, dictation, selection handles), Safari and Firefox. `EDITOR-SPIKE.md` has a 12-step phone checklist to run before the Phase 1 exit.

---

## 38. Phase 1 build decisions — decided

- **Save loop:** `base_revision` is the concurrency token; a 409 carries `data.reason` (`revision` or `lease`) and `data.current_revision`. The per-tab lease is in memory (90 s, renewed by saves) and is released by `doc.lease` with `release: true` when a tab closes. A response lost in flight is recognised by comparing the server text with every unconfirmed snapshot, so it never becomes a false conflict. Text that loses a conflict is kept in `data/conflicts/<project>/` before anyone chooses.
- **Drafts live in the browser** (`localStorage`), are offered whenever they differ from the server, are held under a separate key until the person answers, and are cleared at sign-out (privacy beats convenience on a shared machine; unanswered drafts go too).
- **Long documents:** warn at 15,000 words, read-only at 25,000. A read-only chapter can still be cut with Split at cursor, which is how it gets back under the limit.
- **`doc.split`** cuts at a UTF-16 offset in the stored text (CRLF counts as two), keeps the compile and AI-exclusion flags, honours the tab lease, and refuses a cut inside a fenced code block, an HTML comment or front matter, and any document that carries alternative wordings (resolve them first). Known gap: `:::` blocks, tables and multi-line HTML blocks are not yet detected as preserved blocks.
- **Search** is FTS5 over title, synopsis and body (`unicode61 remove_diacritics 2`). User text never reaches FTS5 syntax: it is cut into words, each is quoted, the last is a prefix match. More than twelve words is refused rather than truncated. Highlight markers are random private-use characters per request, so nothing in a document can forge a `<mark>`.
- **Export is a POST** (`export.markdown`) because each export records a manifest row; a GET that writes breaks the "no state-changing GETs" rule. Layout: a document with children becomes `NN-slug/` holding its text as `00-slug.md`; notes go flat under `notes/` (a final `# Notes` section in single mode); bundle files are the stored text byte for byte, titles live in the manifest; the zip uses Store so it is byte-stable. Caps: 2,000,000 words, 5,000 documents, 64 MB of text. Private `%%notes%%` are exported verbatim until F-079 changes that.
- **The counter** is a goldmark-based Go port of the editor's counter and agrees with it on every round-trip corpus input (a short exceptions list is documented in its test). A linear sanitiser bounds goldmark's quadratic cases; it does not touch fenced code.
- **Palette:** `Mod+K` stays Link inside the editor (F-063), so the palette there is `Mod+Shift+P` or `Mod+/`; everywhere else `Mod+K`. The quick switcher is `Mod+O`.
- **Italics are synthesised:** Newsreader ships no italic face and the font budget is four files. It reads acceptably in body text and would not pass a typographic review; adding a real italic is a budget decision for a later release.
- **Editor focus** is a 2 px accent rule in the left margin, off the text, so nothing moves when it appears.
- **JS budgets:** the writing glue (`doc.js` + `tree.js`) is 21 KB gzip (it was 20 KB; the second review round's save-path and draft fixes took the extra kilobyte) and is at its limit; new behaviour goes in lazily loaded files (`palette.js` 12 KB budget, small helpers a few hundred bytes to a few KB each).
- **Not verified:** Safari, Firefox, real phones and on-screen keyboards, a screen reader, the Docker image and Forgejo CI.

---

## 39. Phase 2 goals and import decisions — decided

### Goals

- **Words added are measured against a baseline.** For each person, session and document, credit is the number of words the current text holds beyond what the text had when the session first saved it (a multiset difference: each word counts once per extra occurrence), added to the credit banked so far, and never reduced. When credited words leave the text (a cut or a rewrite) the credit is banked and the baseline moves to the text as it stood just before that save, so new writing adds to what was earned instead of competing with it. A word typed letter by letter across autosaves counts once; a cut pasted back, in any number of saves (even of text typed earlier in the session), earns nothing new; a deletion never subtracts; writing 300 words and deleting them keeps the 300; deleting 500 old words and writing 500 new ones earns 500; writing 300, discarding them and writing 300 different ones earns 600. Net words are separate and exact (new word count minus old). Rejected: summed positive deltas (credits a paste-back and every typed letter), and a pool of removed words (makes net fail a day).
- **Other people's words are not yours.** Each session remembers the hash of the text it last left in a document. If the text under the next save is different (another member saved, or restored, in between), the session banks its credit and measures from the text as it now stands, so a collaborator's 500 words earn the saver nothing and a restore by someone else earns nothing. The same hash check covers two tabs of one person: their saves chain, and a save that lost a conflict never reaches the recorder.
- **Word counts are re-derived when they matter.** A document's stored `word_count` can come from an older counter. The first save of a document in a session, and every rebase, count the old text itself instead of trusting the stored number, for both the net change and the "never credit less than the growth" guard. Later saves in the session trust the stored count.
- **Baselines are copies of manuscript text and are kept short.** `writing_session_docs` has a foreign key to the document (a purged document takes its baselines), a trigger removes a person's baselines when they leave a project, any save by anyone prunes baselines untouched for an hour, and a person's next session deletes the previous session's. What remains is at most one session's baselines for people who still have access, until the next save anywhere. A pruned baseline costs no credit already earned.
- **Documented approximations.** Words are whitespace-delimited runs with a letter or digit, punctuation trimmed from the edges and case folded, counted on the raw Markdown (so a pasted code block counts although the document word count ignores it). Replacing a word by the same word earns nothing. Select-all and paste-over in one save is credited as the larger of the two texts' new words, not their sum (two saves are credited in full). Text cut before a rebase and pasted back after it is credited as new. A move between two documents credits the pasted words in the second one. A baseline lasts one session, so a cut in one session pasted back in a later one counts as new. Only the region where baseline and text differ is tokenised, so an ordinary edit of a 25,000-word document costs well under a millisecond.
- **Which writes count.** Content saves through `doc.update` only. Opening a document, creating one with text, splitting, importing and trashing earn nothing. `goals.RecordSave` takes `NoCredit` for writes that are not typing: it records the net change and rebases the baseline so the restored text is not credited when the person types on. The revision restore must call it with `NoCredit` (it does not exist yet in this tree).
- **Sessions and minutes.** A session continues while saves arrive within 30 minutes and the local day and time zone are the same; minutes are the sum of the gaps between saves, each capped at 5 minutes, not first save to last (a 30-minute pause is not 30 minutes of writing). This replaces the "first and last save, capped" idea: it needs no cap per session and cannot be inflated by one long gap.
- **Days are local at write time.** Each session stores the local date and zone it was written in, so a change of profile time zone never moves past words to another day, and `daily_stats` is a plain sum of sessions. DST needs nothing special: the date comes from the zone's calendar, and streaks step by calendar date, never by 24 hours. `goals.Recompute` (and `scriobh stats recompute`) rebuilds the totals exactly and keeps each day's target and hit.
- **A finished day stays finished.** The day's target follows the current daily goal until the day is met, then freezes. Raising the goal later does not take the day away; lowering it below today's words meets today; deleting the goal leaves met days met.
- **Streak.** Consecutive local days on which the daily goal was met. Today does not break a streak until it ends; a streak only grows when today's goal is met. `stats.today` also returns the longest streak. A day with no goal is not a hit, and nothing marks a quiet day: `stats.history` returns dense days with zeros and no "missed" field.
- **Project target has two writers, each with one meaning.** `project.update` sets the project's shared `word_goal` and `deadline` (owner only). `goal.set` with `kind=project` sets the caller's own total-word target and optional deadline (editor and above). `stats.project` uses the caller's own goal if there is one, else the shared one; the other members' goals are never visible.
- **Projection is withheld rather than guessed.** A projected finish date needs at least 5 days with words added in the last 28 and a positive net pace; the pace for the total is net words (the total moves by net), while the daily goal and the habit numbers use words added. Words per day needed for a deadline is shown whenever a future deadline and remaining words exist. No field says "behind".
- **Failure isolation.** Recording runs in a savepoint inside the `doc.update` transaction. A statistics fault rolls back only the statistics, is logged, and the save succeeds.
- **Per person, always.** No stats or goal op takes a user id; all read the caller's rows. Readers and reviewers cannot save, so write no stats. Sessions are kept indefinitely (a few rows a day); only baselines are pruned.

### Import

- **Stateless.** `import.preview` writes and keeps nothing. `import.apply` takes the same files again plus the preview's token (a hash over every accepted file's path and content) and the writer's edits, rebuilds the plan, refuses with 409 if the files differ, and creates everything in one transaction. Nothing partial can exist. No job table and no stored copy of the source; `import_jobs` waits for the Git import.
- **The importer is a library over `[]File`.** The CLI, the zip upload and the future Git import all feed `imports.Preview` and the same plan builder. A file's bytes go in `data` (base64) so invalid UTF-8 is detectable; `content` is a text-only convenience. `imports.ReadZip` reads an uploaded zip with bounded entries and bounded inflated size, never trusting declared sizes. A zip's single wrapper folder is dropped (a zipped folder always has one); a file listing is taken as already relative to the folder, so a folder holding one subfolder keeps that part. Preview accepts the same edits and reordering as apply, so what it shows is what apply creates.
- **Caps.** 10,000 listed entries; 2,000 Markdown files; 2 MiB per file; 32 MiB of Markdown in all (not 64: the JSON body carries base64 and the server holds both forms); 512-byte paths; 10 directories deep; 5,000 documents after splitting. A file whose headings would take the plan past 5,000 documents is kept whole, and listed as skipped with the reason "too many headings to split into scenes" and a warning (the plan is never built that large; a 2 MiB file of headings would otherwise be 400,000 scenes). The preview returns at most 5,000 nodes, edits are limited to 10,000 and reorder keys to 20,000, and a zip's central directory is walked (header by header, from every place the library could start) and refused above 10,000 entries before the archive is parsed; the declared count and size are not trusted, and zip64 archives are refused. Documents are parsed for their counts and search text under a budget of 2 MiB of Markdown in flight, so one very large document is parsed alone (a 2 MiB file of headings peaks at a few hundred MB; eight at once peaked at 2 GB). An over-cap folder is refused; an over-cap file is skipped and listed. The import ops declare a 48 MiB request body (`Op.MaxBody`, applied by the server before routing; the caller is authorized before the body is read); every other route keeps 2 MiB.
- **What is skipped, and reported.** Non-Markdown files, hidden files and folders (and `__MACOSX`), over-size files, text that is not valid UTF-8 or holds NUL, symbolic links, duplicate paths, unsafe paths (absolute, `..`, drive letters, control characters, backslash tricks) and too-long or too-deep paths. The preview lists them with a reason and counts by reason.
- **Generic Markdown folder.** Folders become parts (nested folders nest). `NN-title.md` files become chapters; siblings sort by number, then name the way a person counts (`ch2` before `ch10`); unnumbered front matter first and unnumbered back matter last. The title is the front matter `title:`, else a leading `# H1` (which then leaves the body, because it is the title now), else the file name made readable (`the-salt-road` becomes The Salt Road; a name with capitals is left alone). Front matter stays in the text exactly. Front and back matter are recognised by exact name only: `front-matter, title-page, copyright, dedication, epigraph, foreword, preface` and `back-matter, acknowledgements, acknowledgments, appendix, about-the-author, afterword`. A root `README.md` becomes a note. Headings do not split a file unless asked (`split_scenes=h2`, or per file with an edit): then each `##` outside code fences becomes a scene, the heading line becomes its title, and the text before the first one stays in the chapter. The preview always reports how many `##` headings each file has.
- **Scríobh layout.** Recognised by `scriobh.json` (export bundle, Git layout) or `project.json` (mirror); both names are read, the one that lists `documents` wins. The `project.json` shape is read tolerantly (a `project` block or top-level fields, optional `documents`) and is unverified against the mirror writer, which is built elsewhere. Structure comes from paths: a folder's own text is its zero-numbered file named like the folder (`02-chapter-two/00-chapter-two.md`), the rest of the folder are its children, and kinds, titles, statuses and (from a version 2 `project.json`) the outline metadata come from the manifest because file names are lossy; see §40. Text is kept verbatim. `notes/` becomes top-level notes after the manuscript (an export puts notes in their own section, so a note that sat among chapters returns at the end). `README.md` and anything else outside `manuscript/` and `notes/` are skipped and listed; entities and the Compendium projection import in Phase 3.
- **The manifest is untrusted.** Documents always get new ids; kinds are checked against the document kinds and statuses against the status list (falling back to chapter and draft); titles are cut at 300 characters (project fields at 200); manifest paths outside the supplied files are ignored with a warning; files the manifest does not list are placed by name with a warning.
- **Provenance.** `project_sources` (kind `markdown`, folder name, token, layout and counts) and one `document_sources` row per file-backed document with the sha256 of the file's bytes as read. Documents get revision 1, word counts, hashes and search text through the same insert the document ops use, parsed before the writer is taken. Importing earns no words toward a goal. If revisions later record a baseline when a document is created, the import insert must do the same.
- **Edits.** Rename, kind change, skip (a node and its subtree), per-file split, and reordering one parent's children. Edits are keyed by source path (a folder's key ends in a slash, so a file `a.md` and a folder `a.md/` never share one); they cannot move a node to another parent.
- **Into an existing project.** `import.append` (editor and above) adds the documents after everything already in the target folder (top level, or `parent_id`), never touching existing ones.
- **CLI.** `scriobh import <folder>` previews; `--apply` creates; `--into`/`--parent` append; `--split-h2`, `--title`, `--kind`, `--owner EMAIL` (needed when more than one person has signed in). It opens the database directly under the data lock, so the server must be stopped, and writes events like the server does. Hidden folders are not read (the count is printed).
- **Not done.** Re-import diff, Git and zip-from-URL sources, DOCX, EPUB, Obsidian and every other layout; the screens.

---

## Phase 2 backend decisions — decided

Revisions, links, scene metadata and the outline. Ops live in `internal/domain/revisions`, `links` and `docs`; storage in migrations 0310, 0320, 0330 and 0400.

**Revisions**

- **What an automatic revision is.** On a save that changes the body, the *previous* head is recorded if it differs from the newest revision's text and that revision was written at least 5 minutes ago. Recording the incoming text instead would lose the end of yesterday's session when today's first save lands. The newest text is the document itself; history lists it as the current version (`head` in `revision.list`).
- **"Active editing" has no idle timer.** The clock is the injectable `db.Now`, measured from the newest revision row's *write* time. A save after a long pause always qualifies; a burst writes at most one revision per window. Two timestamps follow: `created_at` is when the text became the head (shown, filtered by day, used for retention) and `recorded_at` is when the row was written (the throttle). Gating on the shown time would make a late capture be followed by another a minute later.
- **`documents.content_at` and `content_by`** (0330) say when and by whom the body last changed, because `updated_at` also moves on a rename or move. Rows written without them fall back to `updated_at` and no author, so importers that insert rows directly keep working.
- **Storage.** Text lives in `revision_blobs`, one row per distinct text per document, referenced by `(document_id, content_hash)`. It is per document so it cascades with the document and a purge leaves nothing; cross-document dedupe is not worth a leak. gzip above 8 KB, stored plain when gzip would be larger, verified against its hash on read. `zstd` is not offered: no encoder in the standard library and no new dependencies.
- **Restore and split keep the head first.** If no revision holds the exact current text, a `snapshot` revision (`Before restore`, `Before split`) is written, ignoring the throttle. A restore then writes the new text through the save path's rules (base revision, lease, counts, search text, links) and a `restore` revision. It emits the same `doc.updated` event with `content: true` that a save does, plus `restored: true`; goals counting words added may want to ignore events carrying `restored`. Restoring the text the document already has writes nothing (`unchanged`).
- **Retention.** Pinned forever: reason `manual` and any revision with a label (labelling an automatic version pins it; clearing the label unpins it). The newest revision of a document is never pruned. Everything else (autosave, restore, snapshot, import, alternative) is kept in full for `revisions.autosave_days` (default 30), then the newest of each UTC day when `revisions.keep_daily` is true (default), otherwise dropped. An hourly sweep runs it; `revision.prune` (owner, with `dry_run`) runs it for one project. `revision.delete` removes only `manual` revisions.
- **Revision ids are never in the path.** The authorization sweep fills every `{param}` with one id, so ops take the document in the path and the revision in the query or body (`revision_id`); required fields are checked before any lookup, and every revision lookup is scoped by the document.
- **Diff.** Prefix and suffix lines are set aside, the rest is diffed by line with Myers' algorithm (capped at 1,000 edits), and words are diffed only inside changed hunks (a hunk capped at 30,000 tokens and 1,000 edits, and 30M comparisons overall). Whatever a cap stops is returned as a delete then an insert with `coarse: true`. Deterministic by construction. The revision reasons DATA-MODEL lists for later phases (Phase 3.5 and Phase 4) are accepted by the table so it is never rebuilt; nothing writes them yet.

**Wiki links**

- **Syntax and scope.** `[[Title]]` and `[[Title|text]]`, read by the same parser as the word counter (`text.WikiLinks`), so the Go side matches the editor. Links inside code (fenced, indented, inline), source blocks (raw HTML, front matter, tables) and `%%private notes%%` are not links: a private note never becomes a backlink snippet or an auto-created page.
- **Resolution.** By title within the project, ignoring case and runs of whitespace, folded in Go (Unicode lower case) because SQLite's `lower()` is ASCII-only; trashed documents are never targets. A title with more than one live document resolves to the first in reading order and is flagged `ambiguous`. Create, rename (old and new title), trash, restore, split and move (which changes "first") re-resolve the affected links in the same transaction. Writers outside the docs ops must call `links.Sync` and `links.Reresolve`, or run `links.Rebuild`.
- **Uniqueness.** `(from_id, kind, link_text)`, not DATA-MODEL's `(from_id, to_id, kind, link_text)`: the same key (`to_id` follows from the text), and it covers unresolved links, which a UNIQUE index over a NULL does not.
- **Backlinks** join both ends back to the project and to live documents, exclude self-links, and come in reading order with the linker's breadcrumb. One entry per linking document (counts summed). The snippet is the paragraph around the first link, taken from the search text, so private notes are out.
- **`doc.resolve_link` is an editor op** (it can create, and POST carries the write scope); readers follow links through `doc.links`, which already carries the target. Auto-create makes a `note` (or the requested kind) titled by the link text at the end of the first top-level document titled `Notes` (any case), else at the end of the top level. It never creates the folder.

**Scene metadata and outline**

- **`pov` and `location` are free text** (≤ 200 characters) in 0320, next to the existing `story_date` and `story_date_end` (≤ 64, ISO 8601 recommended, not enforced). `pov_entity_id` and `location_entity_id` stay NULL. **Phase 3 plan:** when entities arrive, a migration creates a character or place entity for each distinct non-empty value per project, sets the FK, and keeps the text as the display name until the entity is renamed; odd values are listed for review rather than guessed.
- **`outline.get`** is one query without bodies, in reading order, with `depth`, `child_count` and a roll-up on every item (documents, words, target, progress, status counts). A document's roll-up target is its own target when it has one, otherwise the sum of its children's roll-up targets; words and status counts always add up the whole subtree. Progress is words over target, to three decimals, 0 without a target. There is no bulk status op: `doc.update` already sets status and target.
- **Reordering a part** is `doc.move`; a test moves a part with its chapters and asserts that no word, parent or content changes.

---

## 40. Phase 2 mirror and backup decisions — decided

**The mirror**

- **The folder is `data/mirror/<slug>--<last six characters of the project id>/`, not `<slug>/`.** Slugs are unique per owner, not per instance (§36), so two owners can both have `the-salt-road`; a bare slug would put two projects in one folder. The id suffix is the random end of the ULID, so the name is unambiguous and the folder can be found from the project id alone (the mirror records it in `data/mirror/.state/<project id>.json`, and falls back to scanning for a folder whose `project.json` carries the id). The slug comes first so a folder is still readable. `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` shows `<project-slug>/`; read it as this name.
- **The manifest is `project.json` in the mirror; the push and the import read `scriobh.json`.** `ARCHITECTURE.md` uses both names. The mirror writes `project.json` (the roadmap's name) and the Git push should publish it as `scriobh.json`, the name the import flow recognizes, unless the two are reconciled in one place later. It carries the schema version, the project's id, slug, title, subtitle, author, kind, language, genre and status, a `state` of `active`, `archived` or `trashed`, whether notes are included, and every document's id, path, kind, title, status, outline metadata (below), word count and SHA-256. It holds no time of writing, so the same project is always the same bytes. Goals are not in it: they are per person, not part of the book.
- **`project.json` version 2 carries the outline.** Version 1 listed only id, path, kind, title and status, so a clone from Git or a re-import lost every synopsis, scene detail and target. Each document entry now also has, in this order, `synopsis_short`, `synopsis_long`, `pov`, `location`, `story_date`, `story_date_end`, `target_words` and `include_in_compile`, then `word_count` and `sha256`. All are always written, with their defaults (empty, 0, true) when unset, so a reader never has to guess; the file is not HTML-escaped, so a synopsis with `&` or `<` reads as written; a golden test pins the bytes. The metadata is also in the start-up fingerprint (and the layout version is 2), so every existing mirror is found stale at the first start after the upgrade and has its `project.json` rewritten once; the Markdown files are untouched (their bytes do not change), and a second start writes nothing. Bump `schema_version` and the layout version together for any further change.
- **The importer reads those fields.** When a Scríobh mirror or the Git layout (`project.json` or `scriobh.json`) is imported, each listed document's synopses, point of view, location, story dates, target and compile flag are stored with it, cleaned to the limits `doc.update` enforces (one-line fields at 300, 200 and 64 characters, the long synopsis at 20,000 characters with its line breaks and no control characters, a target between 1 and 10,000,000, anything else ignored), because the manifest is untrusted. The fields are decoded leniently: a value of the wrong type loses that field only, not the manifest. The long synopsis is kept exactly as written (no trimming, no collapsing of spaces, line endings as they were); a value cut to a limit is reported as a preview warning. A manifest without them (an export bundle, a version 1 mirror) imports as before: no synopsis, no scene metadata, no target, compiled. A manifest may be up to 8 MiB (a document body is limited to 2 MiB, but a mirror's manifest lists every document with its synopses, and a project near the importer's 2,000-file limit passes 1 MiB before any synopsis is written); a larger one is not read, and the preview says so with a warning and a line in the skipped list instead of quietly reading the folder as a generic one. `docs.InsertTx` takes the same fields so the importer writes them in the one transaction. A test takes a project through mirror, folder and import (under both manifest names) and compares status, synopses, target, point of view, location, story dates and compile flag in tree order. The export bundle's own `scriobh.json` is unchanged and still does not carry them.
- **Nothing but the projection lives in a project folder.** Temporary files are written to `data/mirror/.tmp/` on the same file system and renamed in, so a snapshot of a folder never sees one. The record of what was written (its fingerprint, the hash of each file, the time of the last change) is in `data/mirror/.state/`. The folder holds `project.json`, `README.md`, `manuscript/` and `notes/`, and nothing else the mirror creates.
- **Atomic and quiet by per-file diffing, not a directory swap.** Each file is written to a temporary file, synced and renamed, so a reader sees the old file or the new one. A file whose bytes are unchanged is not touched, so mtimes and Git diffs stay quiet. A directory swap would rewrite every file's mtime on every save and make a 60-document project cost 60 writes. The cost: across files the tree can be between two states for a few milliseconds. A reader that needs one state (the Git push) uses `Mirror.Hold`, which blocks writes to that project while it copies.
- **Trigger: the events table, not a wrapped sink.** The mirror reads the events the ops already write, from a cursor, whenever the event store wakes it, and marks any project an event names. It does not look at event types, so an op added later is covered as soon as it emits an event; a test lists every mutating `doc.` and `project.` op and fails until a new one is classified. Events are written inside the content transaction, so a committed change cannot lose its trigger. A 30-second poll covers a missed wake-up.
- **Debounce 2 s, at most 15 s.** A project is rewritten two seconds after its last change, but never more than fifteen seconds after its first unwritten one, so steady autosave still reaches the disk. `SCRIOBH_MIRROR_DEBOUNCE_MS` changes the two seconds.
- **Never blocks a save.** One background worker writes projects one at a time. A failure is logged and retried with backoff (5 s doubling to 5 min); `mirror.status` reports the error, the retry count and when the next attempt runs. On shutdown the server writes what is pending (within ten seconds) before it closes the database.
- **Self-healing at start.** Each project's fingerprint (its fields and, per live document, id, place, title, status and stored content hash; no text is read) is compared with the one recorded when it was last written. A missing or stale mirror is rewritten; a current one costs two queries and no writes. `mirror.rebuild` compares every file with the bytes on disk and repairs the ones that differ. A rebuild removes Markdown files that no document accounts for under `manuscript/` and `notes/` (they can only be leftovers); anything else in a project folder, and any folder the mirror did not record, is never removed.
- **Notes are always included; research and Compendium documents are not.** There is no per-project switch yet. A trashed or archived project stays mirrored and says so in `project.json`; purging it removes the folder and its record (through the purge hook), and a project that vanished while the server was down is found and removed at the next start.
- **On or off is an environment switch only.** `SCRIOBH_MIRROR=off` stops all writing; the default is on. There is no admin-console setting because the settings table has no editor yet (the admin console is later). When one exists, the switch moves there and the environment keeps winning.
- **The compendium projection** is written from Phase 3: see [Phase 3 Compendium decisions](#phase-3-compendium-decisions).

**Backups**

- **A backup is a zip with a manifest of hashes.** `scriobh.db` (a `VACUUM INTO` copy), `files/`, `mirror/` (without its scratch directories), `conflicts/`, and `manifest.json` last. The manifest lists every other entry with its size and SHA-256, the migrations applied in the copy, and project and document counts. The zip uses an allow-list of those roots; it never holds `secret.key`, the lock file, other backups, `backups/migrate/` or `backups/git/`.
- **The database copy runs on its own connection.** `db.Backup` holds the single writer connection for the whole copy, and a save waits at most five seconds for it; a large database could fail saves. `db.Snapshot` opens a short-lived connection and copies a consistent read snapshot, so saves go on, and it works from a second process, which is how `scriobh backup` runs beside a server. A test saves continuously during backups of an 8 MB database.
- **The secret key is backed up separately, on purpose.** An archive that held the key would put every credential and the means to read it in one file. Back up `secret.key` (or `SCRIOBH_SECRET_KEY`) in a different place. Restoring never creates, copies or replaces a key. A restore without the original key leaves sessions alone (they are stored hashed) but any encrypted remote credential cannot be decrypted: **no such credentials exist yet**, so today this is documentation and an empty hook (`backup.PostRestore`); the Git push phase adds the step that clears the ones the key cannot decrypt and tells the person to enter them again. Not invented before the table exists.
- **Restore order keeps the live directory safe.** It takes the data-directory lock (refused while a server holds it), validates the manifest in both directions (every listed file present, every file listed), extracts into `.restore-<time>/` beside the data while checking each size and hash as the bytes are read (limit counted on bytes actually read; names that are absolute, contain `..` or a backslash, sit outside `scriobh.db` and the three roots, or are not regular files are refused), migrates and integrity-checks the staged database (a backup from a newer build is refused here), takes a pre-restore backup of what is there, moves the old pieces aside and swaps the new ones in, undoing the move if a step fails. Deviation: migrations run on the staged copy before the swap rather than after, so a failure leaves the live directory untouched; the server migrates again at start if needed.
- **An empty directory, or `--force`.** "Empty" ignores `secret.key`, `backups/` and the lock file. `--force` takes `backups/pre-restore-<time>.zip` first and refuses if it cannot. With no database to archive, the replaced files are kept in `.restore-old-<time>/`. The cap on what restore writes is checked three ways: the manifest may not declare more than the cap, each file is read only up to its declared size and what is left of the cap, and the total is counted as bytes are read.
- **A written archive always restores.** `backup` leaves out, and counts, any file whose name restore would refuse (a colon or a backslash, legal on Linux), instead of writing an archive that cannot be restored.
- **Scheduled backups arrive in Phase 2, not Phase 5.** The roadmap lists them under Phase 5 and the catalogue as F-324 (P2, Phase 4); the local ticker is small and a restorable backup is part of a v0.2 that holds a book. Default **on**, daily (`SCRIOBH_BACKUP_INTERVAL_HOURS=24`, `0` turns it off), keeping the newest seven scheduled archives (`SCRIOBH_BACKUP_KEEP`). The first runs a minute after start, or when due after the newest scheduled one, so a restart does not take another. Only scheduled archives (`auto-<time>.zip`) are pruned; manual (`<time>.zip`) and pre-restore (`pre-restore-<time>.zip`) archives are never deleted by the application.
- **Backup ops return ids, never paths.** `backup.list` lists only top-level zips with a generated name; `backup.download` takes one of those ids and refuses anything else with a 422, so no path is reachable. An op's file is held in memory, so downloads are capped at 256 MB; larger archives are copied from `data/backups/`.
- **Backups are plaintext.** Unchanged from §15: the operator protects the volume and the archive.

---

## Phase 3 Compendium decisions

The Compendium and the entity bible: ops in `internal/domain/compendiums`, `entities`, `projects` (members) and `compaccess`; storage in migrations 0500, 0910, 1500 and 1510. Mentions, first appearance, the in-this-scene sidebar, the timeline and research are the second round.

**Access**

- **Three roles: owner, editor, reader.** DATA-MODEL listed `reviewer` for a Compendium too; it is not built, because nothing comments on a bible entry yet and a reviewer would only be a reader. An instance admin acts as owner of every Compendium (the rule for projects); lists show only the caller's own memberships. A trashed or archived Compendium still opens for its members (to restore it); the trash list is the owner's alone.
- **Project access never grants the bible.** `project.entity.list` and `project.entity.get` return only entities of Compendiums the caller is a member of; a project reader who is not on the Compendium gets an empty list (with `hidden: true`) and 404 on a name, and `project.compendium.get` counts what they cannot see without naming it. Writing an override needs project editor *and* membership of the entity's Compendium. A reader of a Compendium that another Compendium or a project references cannot write through the reference: `entity.*` ops authorize by the entity's own Compendium. Reading through a Compendium's own references (`entity.referenced.list`) follows the same rule as a project's view: a reader of A can read B's entities through A's reference only if they are also a member of B, and are told only that something was left out.
- **The mirror is the one place the bible crosses that line.** A project's `compendium/` folder holds everything the project uses, whoever may read each file in Scríobh, because it is a projection of what the project's owner wired in and is what a Git push sends. It is documented in OPERATIONS. Referencing a Compendium needs read access to it (an unseen one is the same 404 as a missing one); setting a primary needs editor access to it; linking an entity needs read access.
- **Project members.** `project.member.list/add/update/remove`, owner only, scope `admin` for a key. People are found by exact email (case-insensitive) among live, human accounts; there is no search or autocomplete. An address that is unknown, removed, an API-only account, or an instance reader being made an editor all answer with the same 404 body, so adding people is not a way to list who is on the instance. What it cannot hide: a successful add tells the owner the address belongs to someone, and an already-added person is a 409. Audit lines carry user ids and roles, never addresses.
- **Compendium prompt (§34), exactly.** `add_to_compendium` defaults to true for an editor and false for a reviewer or reader. The result's `compendium` object says `requested`, `granted`, the role held afterwards and a `reason` when nothing was granted: `not_requested`, `no_primary_compendium` (skipped, never an error), `already_member` (never downgraded or re-granted), `not_compendium_owner` or `restricted_key`. Only the Compendium's owner can grant. If the caller cannot and the request was only the default, the person is added to the project and the reason says so; if the request was explicit, it is refused with a 403 *before anything is written*. An editor gets editor access, a reviewer or reader gets reader access. `project.member.remove` says which role they still hold (to a caller who may see it) and never removes them from the Compendium.
- **Key pins (the final model).** A key carries one pin, never two. **A key pinned to one Compendium** (`key.create` with `compendium_id`; the creator must be able to read it, and the key acts as the creator, so it can never do more there than they can; the scope narrows it further) reaches that Compendium's ops and nothing else: no other Compendium, no project of its owner (a project id is a 404 for it), nothing instance-wide. **A key pinned to one project** reaches that project and that project's *primary* Compendium (looked up at each call, so it follows a change of primary) and no other Compendium. It may fill the bible (`entity.*`, custom fields, `search.compendium`, `compendium.export`) and set overrides, but the Compendium may be shared with other books, so it cannot rename, archive, trash, delete or share it (`compendium.update`, the state ops, `purge` and `compendium.member.*` are refused for a project key; a key pinned to that Compendium itself may do the first ones), cannot change which Compendiums the project uses (`project.compendium.set`, references, links), cannot grant Compendium access (`add_to_compendium` is refused when explicit, skipped with `restricted_key` otherwise), cannot read other Compendiums through a reference (`entity.referenced.list` is refused for any pinned key), and cannot reference one Compendium from another. `whoami` names the Compendium only if the key's owner is a member of it. The registry enforces this for every op through its declared auth (`authorizeInput`); a Compendium-only pin used to pass straight through to the owner's projects before. The key store is `api_keys` (migration 1610), and `keys.CompendiumAccess` is installed by the Compendium domain. The two-way tests run through real keys (`key.create`, then Bearer over REST). **The roadmap's exit criterion** ("an agent with a compendium-scoped key creates a compendium, adds twenty entities, and drafts a chapter") is met by a project-pinned key: `project.compendium.create` makes a Compendium and attaches it as the pinned project's primary (the caller must own the project; a project with a primary already is a 409), then the key adds entities to it and drafts chapters of the book, all by name through the CLI. Creating a Compendium that belongs to no project (`compendium.create`) needs an unrestricted key, so the agent-with-a-pin story is: a person mints the project-pinned key, the agent does the rest. `whoami` names the Compendium a pinned key works in, and the CLI uses it for names (a pinned key cannot list Compendiums, and entity lookups need no `--compendium`).

**Entities**

- **Five kinds, fixed.** `character`, `place`, `item`, `org`, `concept` are a CHECK constraint, and an entity's kind cannot change. The extension path is custom fields per kind (typed, per Compendium); a custom-kind table would be a new migration, and nothing assumes the list is closed except that constraint.
- **Custom fields.** Types `text`, `number`, `date` (`YYYY`, `YYYY-MM` or `YYYY-MM-DD`) and `choice`; defined per Compendium and kind with `entity.field.set`, and an entity can hold values only for defined keys. Values are stored as canonical strings (`34`, not `34.0`), so the same value is the same bytes in the mirror and the export; an empty value clears a field. A type cannot change once defined; a choice in use cannot be dropped; removing a field in use needs `drop_values`. A new Compendium gets starter fields (a character's role, age, appearance, voice, wants, fears, secrets and arc, and a few for places, items and organisations), switched off with `starter_fields: false`.
- **Names and aliases.** A name is unique among a Compendium's live entities ignoring case and spacing (folded in Go; SQLite's `lower()` is ASCII-only). An alias may not equal another live entity's name or alias, nor the entity's own name: two entities answering to one word would make a mention ambiguous, so it is refused with a 409 that names the other entity rather than allowed and reported later. Trashing an entity frees its name and aliases; restoring takes them back and is refused if someone used one meanwhile. The rule is checked in the writer's transaction; the unique index is the backstop for names.
- **Ordering and groups.** The list is ordered by `sort_name` (else name), folded, then id, paged by an opaque cursor. Grouping is by kind (a filter, and a folder in the mirror); there are no free-form groups or tags yet. `color` is optional `#rrggbb`. Avatars wait for attachments.
- **Concurrency.** `entity.update` takes an optional `base_revision` and refuses a stale write with the current revision in `data`.
- **Relationships.** Typed edges (`family`, `ally`, `rival`, `mentor`, `romantic`, `member`, `custom`) between two entities of one Compendium, with a `label` that reads from the source, an optional `inverse_label`, and `symmetric`. Listing from either end gives the direction (`out`, `in`, `both`) and the label for that side. The same edge twice (either way round when symmetric) is a 409. Relationships scoped to one book are not built.
- **Deferred, with room in the schema:** project-local entities (`entities.project_id`, `CHECK` exactly one owner), `copy` mode (the CHECKs allow it; no op writes it), importing a Compendium bundle, and migrating the free-text POV and location to entities (the columns and the cleanup trigger exist).

**Merge**

- One function, `planMerge`, works out everything; `dry_run` returns it and the real merge applies the same value in one transaction, so the preview cannot disagree with the result (a test compares them). The target keeps its name, sort name, colour and id; the source's name becomes an alias of the target (`name_as_alias`, default true); aliases move except those the target already has and those a third entity uses (reported, dropped); custom fields only the source has are copied; **where both hold a different value the request must choose `source` or `target` per field, and a project that overrode both entities needs `override:<project id>`**, and an unresolved conflict refuses the merge with the list in `data`. Summary and body stay the target's (the request can choose the source's, or append the source's body); an empty target field takes the source's. Relationships are rewired; ones that would loop on the target or duplicate an existing one are dropped and listed. Links are deduplicated. Scenes naming the source as POV or location now name the target.
- **The tombstone.** The source row is deleted and `entity_redirects` keeps `from_id -> to_id` for a year with a snapshot of what the source held (names and text are in the snapshot, never in the audit line or the event, which carry ids and counts). Only `entity.get` follows a redirect; every other op on an old id is a 404, so a stale id can never write to the survivor. A chain is flattened to one hop at the next merge.
- **Extension point for round two.** `entities.RegisterMergeStep` lets mentions and timeline participants take part: `Count` feeds the plan's `extra`, `Apply` runs inside the merge's transaction.

**Effective entities, overrides, references**

- **Scope of a project:** its primary Compendium, the whole Compendiums it references, and single linked entities. References are followed once (a Compendium's own references do not widen a project's scope); a trashed Compendium or entity is out of scope. **The effective entity** is the Compendium record with the project's override applied: a name or summary the override holds replaces the record's, field values replace key by key (an empty override value blanks the field for the book; a key the override lacks inherits), and body, aliases, kind, relationships and colour are always the record's. If two ways lead to one entity it is shown once, by primary, then reference, then link. A name that two entities in scope share resolves to the primary's, then the references' in the order they were added, then a link (`entities.ResolveName`, which mention detection will use).
- **An override is the book's own row.** Setting, changing and clearing one never writes the Compendium's entity (a test compares the row byte for byte), and other books are unaffected. An override with nothing left in it is deleted. Overrides outlive a change of scope: when an entity leaves (primary switched, reference or link removed, Compendium trashed) its overrides go dormant and apply again if it returns; `project.compendium.set` previews how many.
- **`project.compendium.set`** needs project owner and Compendium editor, previews with `dry_run`, and drops a now-redundant reference when a referenced Compendium becomes primary.
- **Deleting a Compendium** requires the typed name, and `detach_projects` naming every project (primary or reference) that will lose it as `compendium.get` lists them, plus `hidden_projects`, the count of those the caller cannot open (counted, never named). Entity links into it and Compendium references to it are removed with it, and every affected project's mirror is rewritten.

**Mirror, events, search, export**

- **Folder names.** A standalone Compendium is `data/mirror/compendium-<slug>--<last six characters of the id>/` with `compendium.json` (settings, field definitions, references, every entity file and its hash), a README, a timeline placeholder and `entities/<kind>/<slug>--<id6>.md`. It exists only while no project has the Compendium as its primary. In a project the same files sit under `compendium/` with an `index.json`; a Compendium the project merely references or links is marked `reference: true` in each file's front matter.
- **One renderer.** Front matter (id, name, kind, Compendium, aliases, summary, colour, fields, relationships, overridden, book notes) is hand-written with every string JSON-quoted and keys sorted, so a rewrite of unchanged data changes no byte and nothing in a file depends on the clock. If two entity paths would collide on six id characters the later one gets a longer suffix.
- **Events.** `ops.Event` and `events` gain `compendium_id` (migration 1510; the SSE work should read it). The mirror follows it: a Compendium event marks the standalone folder and every project in `entities.AffectedProjects` (primary, reference or link), so a purge emits events for the affected projects inside its transaction, hidden ones included.
- **Quiet and cheap.** A project's fingerprint includes a hash of what its projection depends on, built from metadata only. While that hash is unchanged a project's flush carries the recorded `compendium/` file records forward instead of rendering them, so a chapter save in a project with a large bible renders and writes none of it. A first write is as slow as the disk: files are synced before they are renamed, about 6 ms each here, so 5,000 entities take around half a minute, in the background.
- **Search.** `search.compendium` (Compendium reader) over `entity_fts`: name outranks alias, then summary, then body; only live entities; same word-only query rules as the document search. `entity_text` is rewritten in the same transaction as every change and by `scriobh search rebuild`.
- **Export.** `compendium.export` (editor) returns a deterministic zip: the entity files, a manifest, a README and the timeline placeholder; the same state gives the same bytes. It is recorded in `compendium_exports` with the ids and hashes it held, and refuses a Compendium over 20,000 entities. Import of a bundle is not built.

**Housekeeping**

- **Migrations.** 0500 (entities, forward foreign keys to the 1500 tables, which SQLite allows because they are resolved when a row is written), 0910 (entity search), 1500 (Compendiums and everything that hangs off them, triggers that keep `projects.compendium_id` honest and clear scene references when an entity goes), 1510 (`events.compendium_id`). Existing databases take them in filename order like any others.
- **The authorization sweep** has a second test, `TestAuthzSweepCompendium`, with Compendium fixtures (owner, editor, reader, a project owner who is not on the Compendium, strangers, the admin) and a real-versus-missing 404 check; the project sweep skips Compendium ops. The sweep makes a few hundred requests as one person, so it now runs with the per-person rate limit off (`SCRIOBH_RATE_LIMIT=0`); the limit is not what it tests.
- **Review fixes (key pins, privacy).** A project key's pin is applied in every project op that reads the bible, not only the Compendium ops: `project.entity.list|get`, the override and link ops, `project.entity.link.list` and `project.compendium.get` show it its primary Compendium's entities and nothing reached by reference or link, whatever its owner may read. `compendium.get` gives any pinned key counts only (`hidden_projects`, `hidden_references`), never the titles of projects or the names of other Compendiums. `project.member.add` and `compendium.member.add` refuse a dry run (a preview would say whose address it is) and every add spends one of 20 address lookups a minute per person (429 with `Retry-After`; lookups count whether or not the address exists). `_whoami` names a pinned project only while the key's owner can still open it. An `entity.merge` plan names only projects the caller can open and counts the rest (`hidden_override_conflicts`); where a project the caller cannot open overrode both entities, the target's override is kept. `timeline.order.set` replaces only the positions of events the caller can read. The scratchpad ops are session-only (`Auth.SessionOnly`, in the schema as `session_only`): an API key of the person's, at any scope or pin, is refused; an administrator's backup archive still contains the pads, as it contains everything in the database.
- **The key sweep.** `TestKeySweep` (internal/server/authz_keysweep_test.go) mints real keys over REST, pinned to a project and to a Compendium at every scope the owner may grant (an administrator owner included, who acts as owner of everything), and calls every registered op against the pinned resource's neighbours (a referenced Compendium, a linked entity, a sibling project that shares the Compendium) and a second person's resources. The allowed set comes from each op's declared auth kind, with `pinnedMayCall` (empty) as the one documented allow-list of instance-wide ops; foreign ids must answer 404 (403 when the scope is too low), and no response may name anything outside the pin. A new op is swept without anyone remembering it; a new kind of id needs a universe in the sweep, and the sweep fails until it has one.
- **Dry runs.** Every Compendium, entity, member and project-wiring op runs in a rolled-back transaction with no sink, no services and a scratch data directory, and its side effects are all in the database: `compendium.export` records its row and returns the file inside the rollback (a preview describes the file); a purge's folder removal runs from a hook the preview does not have. `mirror.compendium.rebuild` writes files and declares `NoDryRun`. `entity.merge` has its own `dry_run` (a plan, with nothing written) and also accepts the global preview. The dry-run sweep drives all of them with fixtures (`dryrun_compendium_test.go`).
- **Names in the CLI.** `compendium.list` and `entity.list` are the list ops the CLI finds in the schema (items carry `id`, `name` and, for entities, `aliases`); path ids and `into_id`, `to_entity_id`, `target_id` carry `ref` tags so `scriobh-cli entity get "Máire Ní Bhriain" --compendium "The Salt Road Bible"` resolves with no CLI code of its own. The one CLI change: a key restricted to a project or a Compendium may not list Compendiums, so the resolver falls back to the Compendium `whoami` names, and uses it as the scope for entity names.
- **Coverage of the mirror.** `TestEveryMutatingOpReachesTheMirror` now lists the project's Compendium ops (and exempts `project.member.*` as access, not content); `TestEveryMutatingCompendiumOpReachesTheMirror` does the same for `compendium.`, `entity.` and `mirror.compendium.` ops. A new op fails them until it is classified.

## Phase 3 mentions and timeline decisions

The second round of the Compendium work: mention detection and its index, first appearance, the "in this scene" sidebar, point of view and location as entities, outline sorting, and the timeline with its contradiction finder. Ops in `internal/domain/mentions`, `scenes` and `timeline`; the word matcher and span finder in `internal/text/match.go`; dates in `storydate`; storage in migrations 0520, 0530 and 1520. What exists today is listed in [FEATURES](FEATURES.md#phase-3-mentions-scenes-and-the-timeline-what-the-backend-does-today); the design in [ARCHITECTURE](ARCHITECTURE.md#mentions-and-the-timeline).

**Matching.** `text.Matcher` reads a project's vocabulary (the names and aliases of the entities `vocab.Load` finds in scope, with the book's override name, and the Compendium's own name when an override renames it) and finds it in text.

- *Words.* A word is a run of letters, digits and combining marks; one hyphen or apostrophe (ASCII, U+2010, U+2011, U+2019) between two word characters joins them ("Jean-Luc", "O'Neill"), so "Mary" is not found inside "Mary-Anne", and "O’Neill" and "O'Neill" are the same word. Everything else separates words. Han, Hiragana, Katakana and Thai have no spaces, so each character is a word there.
- *Folding.* Normal form C and the full case fold per word, the same as `FoldTitle`; "STRASSE" matches "Straße". **Diacritics are significant**: "Maire" is not "Máire". A name written one way is found in the other quote style. One deliberate difference from `FoldTitle`: Turkish dotted and dotless I are not told apart (İ, I, ı, i all fold to i), so "IŞIK" finds "Işık" however the author types it; the trade-off is that two names differing only in those letters match the same text, which seemed better than missing every Turkish name typed in capitals. A zero-width non-joiner or joiner between two letters (Persian "می‌روم") does not split a word and is ignored in comparison.
- *Endings and prefixes that are not part of a name.* A word ending in `'s` or `’s` also matches without it, as the last word of a name; the match span stops before the suffix ("Máire’s" finds "Máire"). A trailing apostrophe after a plural ("the Brians' house") is simply a separator. A Hangul word ending in a particle (은 는 이 가 을 를 의 에 도 와 과 로 으로 에서) also matches without it as the last word of a name ("민수가" finds "민수"), but only when two or more syllables are left, so "나이" (age) is not found as "나". A word that begins with an elided French or Italian form (l' d' j' m' n' s' t' c' qu' jusqu' lorsqu' puisqu' dell' all' nell' sull' dall' coll' un', either apostrophe) also matches without it, as the first word of a name ("d'Arthur", "dell'Alba" find Arthur and Alba), and the match starts after the apostrophe; other prefixes ("x'Arthur") do not. A name of one Han, Kana or Thai character matches only where it stands alone (nothing letter-like touches it), because inside a compound it is a syllable; two characters or more are not limited, and explicit links ignore the rule. Bare web addresses (`http://`, `https://`, `www.` up to the next space) are not prose and are skipped, in the index and in the spans alike; image alt text is not indexed either. An alias is limited to 12 words by the entity ops (422), and the matcher keeps at most 12 words of any phrase (older longer aliases are truncated for matching), so a hostile alias cannot make matching slow: 1.6 MB against a 200-word alias scans in about 0.3 s, and the scan reads through a small window of words, not a copy of the text. The index version is 2, so existing databases rebuild once after upgrading; `scriobh search rebuild` also marks every project for a scan, because the index reads the text it rewrites.
- *Longest match wins*, scanning left to right, no overlaps ("Máire Ní Bhriain" beats "Máire" at the same place). A line break ends a match: names do not run across paragraphs; a soft break inside a paragraph is a space.
- **The capital guard (a deviation from "NFC + case fold").** A name or alias written with an initial capital matches only text whose first letter is not lower case, so a character called Will or Grace is not found in every "will" and "grace". A name written in lower case, and caseless scripts, match any case. Explicit `[[links]]` ignore the guard. Without it the first real manuscript with a Will in it would be unusable; with it, "Will went" and "WILL!" match, "she will go" does not. A writer who types a character's name in lower case in dialogue loses that mention.
