- *What counts as text.* The prose the search index holds (`doc_text.body`: the same text the word counter reads): paragraphs, headings, list items, quotes. **Inline code counts as prose** (the task said code is ignored; fenced and indented code, raw HTML, tables, front matter and `:::` blocks are not read, but inline code is text to the counter and to search, and one definition of "the text" is worth more than the exception). **%%Private notes%% are never read**, so a name inside one is not a mention and cannot leak into a snippet. Titles and synopses are not indexed. Notes (`kind: note`), front and back matter are indexed so the sidebar works on them, but `mention.by_entity` and `entity.first_appearance` count parts, chapters and scenes only unless `all=true`, because a note about Máire is not her appearing in the book. Research documents are not indexed.
- *Explicit links.* `[[Máire Ní Bhriain]]` and `[[Máire|the captain]]` name an entity when the target spells one of its names or aliases (same folding, no capital guard). Links are counted in `explicit` and never added to `count`: the link index keeps one row per target, so it cannot say which occurrences show other words, and a link whose shown text is the entity's own words is already in the prose. A document that only links an entity has a row with `count` 0, and counts as a mention in lists and first appearance.
- *Ambiguity.* If two entities in a project's scope answer to one phrase (a name in the primary and an alias in a referenced Compendium, or an override that renames onto another's name), the winner is the one `ResolveName` would pick (primary, then references in the order added, then links), and the row is flagged `ambiguous`. The read ops report `ambiguous` and the `also` list **only when the other meaning is an entity the caller may read**; otherwise the flag is silent, so a hidden entity is not revealed by an ambiguity. Never guessed silently, never dropped. Inside one Compendium an alias cannot equal another's name (round 1's rule), so this is a cross-Compendium case.

**The index.** One aggregate row per (document, entity) (`count`, split into `names` and `aliases`, `explicit`, `ambiguous`, `first_pos`, a snippet), not one row per occurrence as DATA-MODEL's first draft had: occurrence places are computed from the Markdown when `mention.list` is asked for `spans=true`, from the same per-block text and the same matcher, so counts and spans cannot disagree (a test asserts it across the table; a document the parser had to sanitise, or text it rewrote inside a token, gets `spans_complete: false` or an approximate span over the whole token, and the counts stay the truth). There are no manual mentions; `[[links]]` are the explicit mention. Spans are UTF-16 code units over the text with CRLF normalised to LF, the measure `doc.split` takes; front matter, an emoji or `&amp;` before the name, and CRLF are tested. A wiki link's span is the whole `[[…]]`.

**Staying current.** Every document write (create, update, split, restore a version) updates its own rows in the same transaction (`mentions.Sync`, called by the docs hooks after the text and the links are written). A project's `mention_state.stamp` changes, through triggers (migration 1520), whenever its vocabulary can change: an entity's name, case or trash state, an alias, an override, a reference, a link, the primary Compendium, a Compendium's trash or delete. A merge moves the source's rows to the target at once (counts add where a document mentions both) and the stamp moves too. Trash and restore of a *document* change nothing in the index: rows stay, reads leave out trashed documents (the text survives the trash, so a restore needs no work). Rescans are done by a background worker, one project at a time: the text is read from `doc_text` (no Markdown is parsed) and matched outside the writer, only rows that differ are written, in chunks of at most 2,000 statements or 100 ms of writer time, and each chunk re-checks the stamp and each document's content hash, so a rescan can never overwrite a newer save. A project whose scan keeps failing is left alone for 30 s. **There is no inverted-index prefilter**: matching the whole project's stored plain text is cheaper than maintaining one (below), so "adding a character" rescans every document of the books that can see it, in tens of milliseconds, and writes only the rows that change. Reads carry `indexing: true` while a scan is owed; the exit criterion ("adding a character and finding every mention takes under a minute") is met in well under a second when the worker is running, and tests call `mentions.Settle`. Bulk writers: an import marks its project (`MarkDirty`) and the worker does the rest; the links of an import are stored after its commit, so it marks again afterwards. `scriobh mentions rebuild [--project ID]`, `mention.rebuild` (owner, `dry_run`), and a one-time repair at start (`settings` key `mentions.index`) rebuild everything from `doc_text`.

**Measured** (a laptop, one run, `go test ./internal/domain/mentions -run SixtyThousand -v`; the bounds in the tests are several times these and scale under the race detector): 60 scenes of 1,000 words (60,000 words, a fifth of the words accented) and 500 entities, 125 with an alias. A cold full rebuild 40–46 ms, 1,458 rows written, the longest writer hold of a chunk 12–13 ms. A save of a 1,000-word scene 3 ms median (5 ms with a cold matcher), of a 12,000-word scene 16–20 ms, against the 400 ms budget. `entity.create` 1 ms; the rescan it queues 36–42 ms, the writer held 2 ms at most, and 16 saves landed meanwhile, the slowest 4–7 ms. `mention.list` 1–2 ms, with `spans=true` on the 12,000-word scene 7 ms; `scene.entities` 1 ms; `entity.first_appearance` for 500 entities 2 ms; `mention.by_entity` 2 ms. All under the 20 ms target.

**Privacy.** The index holds every entity in the project's scope; **reads filter at the edge** through `vocab.LoadVisible`. Compendium membership decides and project access never does: a project reader who is not on the Compendium gets an empty list with `hidden: true` from `mention.list`, `scene.entities` and `entity.first_appearance`, and a 404 (the same body as for a name that does not exist) from `mention.by_entity`, and `outline.get` and `doc.list` leave the entity references out while the free text still shows. `scene.set_pov` and `scene.set_location` answer an out-of-scope id and an unreadable one with the same 404. `scene.link_entities` (project owner) matches only what the caller can read, so it cannot be used to probe the bible; the project owner who is not on the Compendium gets `no_match` for everything. `entity.appears_in` lists only projects the caller can open and counts the rest in `hidden_projects`. **Pinned keys:** a key pinned to a project reaches that project's *primary* Compendium and no other, so through it the book's references and linked entities are not readable in any of these ops (round 1's rule for `entity.referenced.list`, extended to everything that shows entities); a key pinned to a Compendium reaches no project op and sees no project in `entity.appears_in` (only the count). An importer's entity references from a manifest are untrusted ids: `scenes.LinkImported` uses one only if it is in the project's scope and readable by the importing key, falls back to an exact name match, and otherwise leaves the text.

**Scene point of view and location.** `documents.pov` and `location` stay as free text, the display fallback, and are never deleted; `pov_entity_id` and `location_entity_id` name the entity. `scene.set_pov` and `scene.set_location` (project editor) resolve an id, name or alias among what the caller may read, filter by kind first (a point of view only considers characters, so a place called Rose does not stop the one character called Rose), and refuse a name that still means more than one readable entity with a conflict whose `data` has `reason: "ambiguous"` and the candidates (id, name, kind: only ones the caller can read); they never pick one. They write the entity's name into the text, bump the document's revision like `doc.update` does (an open editor reloads, as it does for any metadata edit) and emit `doc.updated` with `content: false`; setting what is already set, or clearing what is not set, writes nothing, bumps nothing and emits nothing. `timeline.event.update` is a no-op in the same way, and participants and places of events resolve with the same ambiguity refusal (names are unique inside a Compendium, so it can only arise from data the ops would not have written). Clearing keeps the text. **`scene.link_entities` (the migration) reverses the Phase 2 plan** ("a migration creates a character or place entity for each distinct value"): it creates nothing. It matches each distinct value exactly (folded name or alias) against the entities the owner can read, links the unambiguous ones, and reports the rest with a reason (`no_match`, `not_a_character`, `ambiguous` with the candidates); it has a `dry_run` and is also safe under the global preview. It does **not** bump revisions: the text is unchanged and an open editor's next save must not be refused. Running it again finds nothing new. Creating entities for the unmatched values is left to a person who has seen the report. A merge repoints scenes (round 1); deleting an entity clears the reference (round 1's trigger); a trashed entity drops out of the references while the text stays.

**Outline sorting.** `outline.get?sort=` is `order` (default, the tree), `timeline` or `pov`. The sorted modes are flat lists of the leaf documents (a part or chapter that holds others has no date or point of view of its own), each with its breadcrumb; `timeline` orders by story date with the rules below and undated last, `pov` groups by the linked character, or by the free text, or by none (last), with free text that is exactly a linked character's name joining that character's group; reading order breaks every tie. `groups` carries the label, entity, document and word counts and the ids of each point of view. Columns (POV, story date, status, words) are fields on every item.

**Story dates and their order** (`internal/domain/storydate`). A date is `YYYY`, `YYYY-MM`, `YYYY-MM-DD` or `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM` (a space may stand for the T), the year exactly four digits ("98" is not a year; "-0000" and "1998-04-12T" are refused) and negative for before year 0, the calendar proleptic Gregorian (leap years and month lengths are enforced); anything else is not a date. A world with its own calendar numbers its years in that range and shows its own words in the event's `story_label`, which is never used to order. Documents' `story_date` stays free text (Phase 2); one that does not parse is kept as written and treated as undated, and `timeline.check` lists it. Order: a fixed-width sort key in which an unknown part counts as before every known one, so `1998 < 1998-01 < 1998-01-01 < 1998-01-01T00:00 < 1998-01-01T00:01 < 1998-02` and a coarser date sorts at or before every finer date it contains; ties break by the end, then the folded title, then the id; undated items sort after every dated one. SQL and Go order the same because `sort_start` and `sort_end` are the keys. **Intervals:** an item occupies the whole period its precision names (a year, a month, a day) or the minute of a time, or from the start's first moment to the end's last when it has an end; two intervals overlap when they share a moment (an all-day event and an instant that day overlap; adjacent days do not). An interval is *definite* when it has an explicit end or names a day or finer; a bare `1998` says "sometime that year", not "all year".

**The timeline.** Events belong to a Compendium (`timeline_events`), with participants (`timeline_event_people`, an entity of the same Compendium and a role), a place, and the scenes that tell them (`timeline_event_docs`, in any project that uses the Compendium). DATA-MODEL's first draft had `participants_json`, `narrative_doc_id` and `narrative_order` on the event; a Compendium is shared by several books and each tells things its own way, so those became the link tables and a per-book `timeline_narrative`. `timeline.event.delete` moves an event to the trash (people, place and scene links stay, so restore is exact); permanent removal only happens when the Compendium is purged. Participants and places are not rewired to another Compendium. `timeline.scene.link` needs project editor and a readable event in a Compendium the scene's book uses (anything else is the same 404). A merge moves participants and places to the target, and a role the target lacks comes from the source. **Narrative order** is derived by default: scenes at their reading place; an event just before the first of its scenes in this book; events no scene tells last, in story order. A book may set its own order of events (`timeline.order.set`, project editor, only events it can read), which **permutes the events among the places events already hold; scenes never move**, listed events first in the order given and the rest in their derived order; `reset` goes back. The project view (`timeline.project.view`) merges events and dated scenes no event covers (a scene that tells an event is shown under it, never beside it) in story order and carries both orders (`story`, `narrative`, and each item's indexes); leaf scenes with neither a date nor an event are counted in `unplaced_scenes`. Events of a Compendium the caller may not read are left out (`hidden: true`); the mirror, which is a projection of what the owner wired in, shows all.

**Contradictions** (`timeline.check`, `timeline.compendium.check`; fixed rules, no clock, so the same data gives the same findings, each with a stable id). `location_conflict`: one person, two events that name different places, both definite, overlapping in time. It is an **error** only when both events are firm (a clock time, or an explicit end); two events on the same date can be hours apart, so day against day is a **warning**. A bare year or month is not a duration, so `1998` against `1998-04-12` does not fire; a lone `1998-04-10` to `1998-04-14` stay against a trip on the 20th does not either. Each kind returns at most 200 findings (`truncated` says how many were left out, by kind), and the location scan stops after 2,000 overlapping pairs (the sweep is by start key, so only genuinely overlapping events are compared; 300 events all overlapping check in a fraction of a second). `before_birth` and `after_death` (error): an event entirely before the person's `born` or entirely after their `died`; the convention is two optional custom fields of a character, keys `born` and `died`, type date (a book's override of them applies in that book's check; the Compendium's own check uses the Compendium's values); the day itself and the whole year of the birth do not fire. `invalid_range` (error): a scene whose story date end is before its start. `narrated_out_of_order` (warning, never an error: flashbacks are legitimate): each thing the book tells is compared with the one it told just before, and a step back in time (it ended before the previous one began) is flagged; consecutive steps back are one run and one finding, so a 2020 prologue before scenes of 1990 to 1994 is one warning and not one per scene, and the sentence says a flashback or a prologue that looks ahead is fine. Only things a scene tells take part (an event no scene tells is backstory, not "told late"); equal and overlapping dates do not fire. `scene_before_first_event` (warning): a scene dated before the first event a person takes part in, that mentions them (the mention index, so it follows whether the scan is current: `indexing` says so) or is told from their point of view. `unreadable_date` (warning): scenes whose story date is not a date (one finding listing up to 100). The task's "participants mentioned as present in a scene whose story date precedes their first appearance event" is `scene_before_first_event`. Every kind has a true positive and a near miss in the tests. Not built: spelling drift, tense and point-of-view slips (F-120's other half), contradictions between an entity's fields and the manuscript.

**Mirror and fingerprints.** `compendium/timeline.md` of a project, and `timeline.md` of a standalone Compendium, are the timeline in story order (id, where, who, told in, description; for a book also its dated scenes and the order it tells things in): deterministic, rewritten only when an event, a scene link, the book's order or one of the book's documents' outline fields changes, and never by a chapter's text. The project's `compendium/` files are carried forward while the projection hash is unchanged, so the hash includes, for projects that have anything on a timeline, the live documents' outline tuple (id, place, kind, title, story dates, point of view); a test renames, re-dates, moves and trashes scenes and reads the file. `project.json` is schema 3 (adds `pov_entity` and `location_entity`, `{id, name}` with the book's name, absent when none) and the mirror layout version moved with it, so the first start rewrites every manifest once and nothing else (the earlier bump's test, extended). `timeline.md` is deterministic and quiet (a rebuild of unchanged data writes nothing), and `TestEveryMutatingTimelineAndSceneOpReachesTheMirror` fails until a new op is classified.

**Smaller choices.** Migration `1520_mention_triggers` is in the 1500 range, not 0500, because its triggers watch tables that migration creates (a trigger on a missing table fails at creation, and `0520` runs first); `0520` and `0530` keep forward foreign keys, as `0500` does. The stamp is an opaque random token (`hex(randomblob(8))` in a trigger, `db.NewID` from Go), compared for equality, neither a clock nor an id, so a rolled-back or previewed change cannot collide with a committed one; a preview may fill the matcher cache, but only under the committed stamp with the committed vocabulary or under a token that rolls back and is never used again. `mention.list` takes `spans` as a query flag defaulting to false (the highlighter asks; the sidebar does not pay for a parse); `spans_complete` is only meaningful with it. `timeline.order.set` and `scene.link_entities` validate before they write; every new write op is safe inside the rolled-back preview (DB only), and the dry-run sweep drives them. `entity.first_appearance` takes a project (a Compendium is shared, so it is a fact about one book). Events are addressed by id in the CLI (the resolver's list op is entity-shaped). Not built here (the screens are in FEATURES, section Y): project-local entities (the matcher would take them from `vocab` unchanged), importing a timeline bundle, a prefilter, per-entity case sensitivity, the toggleable-column preferences (the data is there).


## Phase 2 Git sync decisions — decided

**Dependency.** `github.com/go-git/go-git/v5` v5.19.3 (pure Go) and `golang.org/x/crypto` (SSH, already an indirect dependency, now direct). The alternative, running a `git` program, would put git and OpenSSH in the container and give two code paths for push and import; ARCHITECTURE's table chose go-git. Cost: the stripped binary grows from 18.1 MB to 22.3 MB (+4.2 MB, about 23%), plus a dozen small transitive modules (go-billy, gcfg, ProtonMail/go-crypto, circl, and others), all pinned in `go.sum`. Pinned to v5, not the v6 alphas. `SCRIOBH_GIT_BINARY` is **not built**: nothing needed it, and an optional second code path is the thing the dependency was chosen to avoid; the settings stay listed as planned.

**Sealed secrets** (`internal/secrets`). AES-256-GCM under a key derived with HKDF-SHA-256 and the label `scriobh/secrets/v1` from the instance key, so the key that signs sessions is not used to encrypt. Each value is bound, as additional authenticated data, to its table, row and field, so a ciphertext copied to another row does not open. A server without a key refuses to store any credential (a 409 that says why) and never falls back to plain text. Credentials are opened for the length of one transport call. No op returns one, no log line, audit entry or error contains one (a test scans the log, the audit table, every op result, the remote and push rows and the whole data directory), and transport errors are scrubbed of the token before they are kept.

**Host keys.** Pinned on an explicit accept: `git.remote.hostkey` scans (a handshake that stops at the host key, nothing authenticated), `git.remote.hostkey_accept` takes the fingerprint the person compared and pins it only if a fresh scan still offers exactly that key. Presets carry no host key: one that cannot be verified here must not be trusted, and a copied-in fingerprint would age silently. The client's own configuration is complete (`ssh.ClientConfig` with the pin check and `HostKeyAlgorithms` limited to the pinned key's type; without that a host with several keys looks like it changed its key), so `~/.ssh/known_hosts`, `~/.ssh/config` and the SSH agent are never consulted. A pinned key that does not match is a typed `host_key_changed` error carrying both fingerprints; replacing the pin needs `replace=true`.

**Addresses.** Only `ssh://`, `git@host:path` and `https://`, no credentials in the address, no host starting with a dash. `file://`, local paths, `git://` and `http://` are refused (a file address would let an editor read or write a directory of the server). Tests reach local repositories through a seam (`Options.AllowFile`) and an in-process `file` transport, because go-git's own file transport runs `git-upload-pack`, which the container does not have. The remote's address is built into a throw-away remote per call, so no address or credential is ever written to a repository's configuration. Superseded by the network guard below: private addresses are refused unless `SCRIOBH_GIT_ALLOW_PRIVATE=1`.

**One remote per project** (F-350), enforced by the op, not the schema. The CHECK lists in migrations 1200 and 1310 are left open where a later phase will add values (`state`, `trigger`, `import_jobs.kind` takes every source kind), because a CHECK cannot be changed once applied. `saveConfig` writes the person's fields only; the status columns are written only by an edit that resets them, so an edit that overlaps a finishing push cannot overwrite its result.

**What is pushed.** The documents the mirror's manifest lists, plus the manifest, read in one step under `Mirror.Hold` and released before any network call. Not a walk of the folder, because the mirror never deletes stray files it did not write. The manifest is the mirror's `project.json` published byte for byte as `scriobh.json` (the importer reads either name; this settles the two names in ARCHITECTURE), except that with notes switched off the notes' entries are removed and `includes_notes` is false. The mirror's README is not pushed. A push and an import of it were tested against the real mirror's manifest, which closes the "unverified against the mirror writer" note in §39.

**The local repository** is bare, under `data/git/<project id>/<remote id>/` (the project id is in the path so a purge can remove it after the rows have cascaded away), built with plumbing only (blobs, trees in Git's order, commits), with no working tree, no index and no configuration. It receives only commits built from a snapshot. Backups leave it out; if it is lost, the remote's head is adopted back as the parent when it is exactly the last commit recorded as pushed (our own commit, fetched, nothing merged), otherwise the push is judged as usual and refused.

**Divergence is decided from local objects.** Before pushing, `ls-remote` gives the branch head. Absent: push. Equal to the local head, or an ancestor of it in the local history: push (with a lease on the head that was seen). Anything else, including a head the local repository does not have: refuse. No object is fetched to decide. ARCHITECTURE said "no objects are fetched"; the refused push does fetch, into a scratch folder and only to show the person a short list of the remote's commits that are not ours (author, date, subject; at most 20). It is not merged, never touches the mirror or the local repository, is bounded by `SCRIOBH_GIT_MAX_REPO_MB` and is deleted at once. A failure of the listing leaves the refusal standing without it.

**Force push confirmation is the remote's name**, typed exactly (not the project title, which several remotes could share and which people retitle). Owner only (administrators count as owners everywhere in the registry). The bundle is a version 2 Git bundle (header plus one packfile) made from a full fetch of the branch into a scratch repository, read back and checked (header, pack checksum, every reachable object present) before the push, and accepted by `git bundle verify` and `git clone` in the tests. A shallow fetch is not a backup, so the bundle is full-depth, under the size cap. The push is `Force` with `RequireRemoteRefs` on the bundled head, so a remote that moved in between is not overwritten. Bundles are in `data/backups/git/`, outside backup archives, never deleted by the application.

**Parking, backoff, no storms.** After a refusal by the remote's history, a secret in the text, unreadable credentials, a refusal by a server hook or a host key problem the remote is parked (*rejected* or *blocked*): automatic triggers do nothing (no row per save) until a person acts; pressing *Push now* retries. Unreachable, timeouts, a refused key or a missing repository keep the commit locally, mark the remote *behind*, and retry with a wait that doubles from 1 minute to 30 (`fail_count` and `next_attempt_at` are on the remote). The wait is one check, `autoEligible`, that the save debounce, the interval, the periodic check and the push-again-afterwards all go through, so steady writing against a dead host does not turn into an attempt per save (tested). Only a host key problem or unreadable credentials need a person; a bad deploy key is retried so that fixing it at the host is enough. History is pruned to the newest 500 pushes per remote whenever a push ends, so refusals and errors cannot grow it without bound. One push per remote runs at a time: an in-process claim and a partial unique index on `git_pushes(remote_id) WHERE status = 'running'`; a trigger that arrives meanwhile asks for another push afterwards. A row left running by a crash is closed at start. The debounce is 30 seconds per project after the mirror reports a write, never later than 5 minutes after the first unpushed change: the mirror itself writes every 15 seconds under steady typing, and a pure debounce would never fire. A periodic check (30 seconds) catches changes a missed notification or a restart left unpushed, by comparing the mirror's tree hash with the one the last commit was built from.

**Commits.** The identity is the neutral `Scríobh <noreply@scriobh>` unless the owner sets a name and email on the remote (both or neither). This supersedes §20, which derived `username@scriobh` from the owner's email: that put part of a person's address in every commit of what can be a public repository. The commit time is the push time; the message has no time or host, the tree and message are a function of the snapshot. Subject `Title · ch N: +words` for one chapter (N counts chapter-kind documents in reading order), `Title · N chapters` or `N documents` for several, `first push: N words` for the first, `project details` when only the manifest changed; the body lists added, changed and removed documents with word changes, in reading order then removed ones by path, at most 100 lines. Word changes here are net, as a commit message states what the text became; goals still count words added.

**Secret scan.** Private key headers, `sk-`/`sk_` keys, bearer tokens (24 or more token characters after `Bearer`), GitHub, Forgejo-style, AWS and Slack tokens, and passwords in URLs. A match refuses the push and names the file and the kind, never the text. There is no override; a novel that quotes a key header has to be reworded.

**Restore.** `backup.PostRestore` now receives the installation's key (the environment's or `data/secret.key`, never created by a restore). With a key that cannot read a remote's credential the ciphertext and the deploy key's public half are cleared and the remote is switched off with `credentials_unreadable` and a message to enter them again. With no key at all nothing is cleared (the original `secret.key` may still be put back); at server start an unreadable credential switches the remote off but keeps the ciphertext, and a remote switched off for that reason that becomes readable again is switched on.

**Repository import.** A job (`import_jobs`, migration 1310, with an owner and a day's expiry) because the clone is kept between preview and apply. The clone runs on a background worker (a request cannot wait on a transfer; the server's write timeout is 60 seconds) and the person polls `import.git_job`. Shallow, single branch, bare; files are read from the commit's tree, never from a working tree, so a link or path in the repository cannot reach outside the clone; blobs are read only for Markdown and the manifests. The size cap is checked while the clone grows and once more after it. Only https addresses (a token for this clone only, never stored) or a project's own remote (`remote_id`, which needs the editor role there and brings the deploy key and pinned host key); an ssh address typed in is refused because it has no key. `path` imports one folder of the repository, for a remote pushed with a folder prefix. On apply the clone moves to `data/imports/p-<project id>/` and `project_sources` records kind `git`, the address, the commit, the branch and folder; a purge removes it. Re-import and the local-folder source (F-359) are not built.

**Ops carry the project in the path and the remote in the query or body**, optional while there is one remote, so the authorization sweep (which fills every path parameter with the project id) never meets a 404 for a missing remote. The sweep's per-user rate bucket is switched off in `newAuthzServer`: it makes about three requests per op and ran out of its burst as the op list grew.

### Hardening after a security review

**Who may change what.** Creating, changing, deleting, generating a key for, accepting the host key of, and importing through a remote are owner-only (administrators count as owners), because they decide where the manuscript and the credential go; editors may look, test, push now, cancel and read history. `git.remote.update` is owner-only as a whole, not by field: an editor who could flip `include_notes` or the folder would change what is published. Before this an editor could re-point an owner's token remote at their own host and read the token from the first request.

**A credential belongs to one place.** Any change of the normalised scheme, host, port or path clears the sealed secret, the deploy key's public half and the pinned host key (one rule for both kinds of auth; nothing is ever re-sealed for a new address). The seal context is the row, the kind of secret and that normalised address, so a row altered in the database does not open. Rows sealed before this (id only) are sealed again once, in `CheckCredentials` at start or after a restore, for the address they have then; use-time never accepts the old context. Tests stand up a recording TLS server and assert that no `Authorization` header ever reaches another address through test, push or import.

**Network guard.** The dialer checks the resolved address at connect time: loopback, unspecified, link-local (cloud metadata), RFC 1918, unique-local, CGNAT 100.64/10, 0/8, 192.0.0/24, 198.18/15, reserved, multicast, documentation and NAT64 ranges, with IPv4-in-IPv6 unmapped first. HTTPS gets Scríobh's own client for go-git (no proxy, redirects capped at three and https-only, response headers 64 KiB, dial, TLS and header timeouts, idle connections cut after a minute). go-git's SSH dial has no hook, so for SSH the name is resolved and checked here and go-git is handed the IP literal (the host key pin does not use the name); `ALL_PROXY` is unset at start because `x/net/proxy` reads it. The host key scan uses the same dialer. `SCRIOBH_GIT_ALLOW_PRIVATE=1` is the opt-in; the dev compose file sets it, the production example mentions it. Errors callers see are generic (blocked or "could not be reached"), the detail goes to the server log at debug level with secrets scrubbed. The same rule covers imports (open to every instance writer). The host and port the person typed are still shown; what they resolved to and what failed are not.

**Bounded responses.** A response body is limited outside the transport (after any decompression): at 64 KiB for a refused request (go-git keeps those in memory for the message) and at the clone size cap plus 16 MiB otherwise.

**Bundles.** The bundle is the fetched pack streamed behind the header (no re-encode; a fallback encodes with the same caps) and read back by streaming: the pack is scanned object by object with the count and the declared inflated size capped (`SCRIOBH_GIT_MAX_REPO_MB` times 20, two million objects), a commit, tree or tag over 32 MiB is refused (those are loaded), the checksum is verified, and only then is the pack indexed on disk and walked. A branch of a few kilobytes of zeros no longer inflates in memory. Bundles are under `data/backups/git/<project id>/`, the newest `SCRIOBH_GIT_BUNDLES_KEEP` (5) per remote are kept, and a purge removes the folder.

**Quotas.** `SCRIOBH_GIT_USER_QUOTA_MB` (2048) for a person's imports, `SCRIOBH_GIT_TOTAL_QUOTA_MB` (20480) for everything Git sync keeps (`0` turns either off). A clone being made is counted at the full cap, in the same transaction as the count of open jobs (five per person), so concurrent starts cannot all pass a check only one fits; with the defaults that allows four clones at once. Clones kept beside imported projects expire after `SCRIOBH_GIT_KEEP_IMPORTS_DAYS` (30; the project is untouched, its source record loses the copy path).

**The row an op read is not the row it writes.** `saveConfig` writes only the columns that differ from what the op read, guarded on the address it worked from, so a scan or test that outlives an edit neither reverts it nor pins a key scanned for another address (a conflict is returned). A job snapshots the remote's address, branch (and, for a force push, name) when it is queued and aborts with `remote_changed` if they differ at run time, so a force push cannot be redirected between the owner's typed confirmation and the run; its status writes are guarded on the address too. Re-pointing or deleting a remote cancels its push and waits for it to leave before the local repository is removed. A force never applies without a head that was seen (`Force` only with a lease); a branch that appears between the check and the push is refused.

**The pre-push check.** Documented examples and placeholders pass (`EXAMPLE`, `sample`, `placeholder`, runs of `x`/`*`/`0`, the password `password` in a URL); a key header counts only with 60 or more base64 characters after it within 400 (indented and quoted keys still match; a quoted header alone, as in a thriller, does not). Titles and file names are scanned before anything is committed (titles end up in commit messages). A refusal records the files and the SHA-256 of what matched; the owner may acknowledge one file with one hash (`git_scan_acks`, audited, `git.remote.secret_ack`); a changed file is scanned again. A refusal no longer needs a manual push to clear: the save trigger and the periodic check look again when the mirror's hash differs from the refused one, and an automatic push that finds the same findings writes nothing. Repository addresses with a query string or fragment are refused (no `?token=`).

**Races.** A finished push is no longer reported as running (the claim row decides; the worker is waited for briefly rather than the stale in-memory entry trusted). The count of open imports, the quota check and the insert are one transaction; applying a job is a compare-and-set on `applying` (migration 1311: the status list is closed and `running` means cloning, which recovery deletes), so one job makes one project; an apply interrupted by a stop is settled at the next start (done if its project exists, ready again if not).

**Restore.** A restore moves aside and replaces only what the archive carries (`includes` in the manifest): an archive made with `--no-files` no longer moves the live `files/` aside and deletes it. Purge hooks (mirror, Git, conflict copies) accept only ids of the shape `NewID` makes (`db.ValidID`): `filepath.Base` lets `.` and `..` through, and `..` is the data directory. When the pre-restore backup cannot be made because the live database is damaged, the restore goes on, moving what was there aside whole and saying so.

**The bible and the push.** A review asked whether the pre-push check could show an editor without Compendium access the names of bible entities (`compendium/entities/<slug>.md`) or confirm guesses by their hashes. It cannot: the push reads only the documents the mirror's manifest lists (manuscript and notes), the attachments it lists and the manifest, and refuses a manifest entry outside `manuscript/` and `notes/`, so the `compendium/` projection and research are never read, scanned, pushed or named in a finding, a push row or a history list (tested with a key placed in an entity file and a manifest that lists one). The one path from the bible into the repository is the manifest's per-scene POV and location entity references (id and name), copied byte for byte from the mirror's `project.json`; they are published to whoever reads the repository regardless of the pusher's Compendium access, and OPERATIONS says so. An error that named an offending manifest path (which could be an entity slug) now says only that a file is not pushed, and the log has the path. If the `compendium/` projection is ever pushed, findings, errors, history and manifests must be redacted for people who are not members of the file's Compendium (`compaccess.CanRead`), with acknowledgements by an opaque finding id; that is not needed while it is not pushed, and is not built.

## Phase 3 agent platform decisions

The agent CLI, scoped keys, server-side dry runs and idempotency. Code: `cmd/scriobh-cli`, `internal/cli`, `internal/db/dryrun.go`, `internal/ops/dryrun.go`, `internal/idempotency`, `internal/domain/keys`, `internal/auth/keys.go`; migrations 1600 (idempotency) and 1610 (agent keys).

**Dry run**

- **A view of the database, not a flag on `WithTx`.** Ops write in one transaction and then read the result back through the separate read pool, which never sees uncommitted rows; a bare rollback flag would return `not_found` for the thing the op just made. `db.DB.DryRun` takes the writer connection, begins `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, and builds a `*db.DB` whose reader and writer pools both resolve to that one connection: `WithTx` on the view is a `SAVEPOINT` that is released, never committed, and `Read()` sees the uncommitted rows. The outer transaction is rolled back, always. The op runs against an `Env` copy with that view, no sink (so no event or audit row and no wake-up), no services (the mirror, backups) and a scratch data directory that is deleted afterwards. `changes` is SQLite's `total_changes()`, so it counts a row an op wrote and then rolled back inside a failed savepoint; the number is a size, not an exact diff.
- **What refuses.** `Op.NoDryRun` is set on `doc.lease` (in-memory state), `mirror.rebuild`, `backup.create` and `key.create` (a secret). Everything else is previewed, including `project.purge`, `import.apply` and `export.markdown`. A GET is run as it is. The lease taken by a `tab_id` on `doc.update`, `doc.split` and `revision.restore` is skipped in a preview. **A new op that touches anything outside the database must set `NoDryRun` or check `Ctx.DryRun()`**; the sweep in `internal/server/dryrun_test.go` drives every op with a request it knows, and calls the rest with an empty one, comparing a digest of every table and of the data directory before and after. An op with no fixture is only checked for not changing anything.
- **Exit 9 is opt-in.** A dry run exits 0 with `dry_run` in the envelope; `--fail-on-change` makes "would change something" exit 9. A script that previews every write should not need `|| true`.
- **`revision.prune` has its own `dry_run` field.** The CLI sets both: the header, and the field when the op declares one.

**Idempotency**

- **Claimed after, not before.** The record is written after the op commits, with an in-process per-key lock for concurrent duplicates (one process owns the database). The alternative, a pending row claimed first, leaves a row with no executor after a crash and no way to tell whether the op ran. The cost is the one instant between the commit and the insert: a crash there lets one retry run again. Only 2xx answers are stored, so an error is corrected and resent with the same key.
- **Scoped by key, not by person.** An API key's records are its own (`key:<id>`); a browser session's are `user:<id>`. A response with a secret leaves a tombstone, and a repeat is a 409.
- **Not applied to file responses.** An op whose output is a download (the export) skips the key; it is rebuilt on request.

**Keys**

- **A key is its owner, narrowed.** Not a separate user: its principal is the owner with `Via: apikey`, the key's scope, and its pins, so the audit log, project membership and roles all work unchanged. `is_agent` is a label. Key management ops declare the `admin` scope, so only a browser session or an admin-scope key manages keys; a key cannot make a key above its own scope; scope `admin` needs an admin owner.
- **`_whoami` is not an op.** `authorizeEarly` (auth.go, not touched here) keeps a pinned key away from every op that does not name a project, and `profile.get` is one of them. `GET /api/v1/_whoami` answers for any signed-in caller, and the CLI uses it to resolve a pinned key's project name. The `ref` tag on an input field (`ref:"project"`) tells clients what an id points at; without it the CLI infers the kind from the path (`/projects/{id}`) or a `_id` suffix.
- **Compendium pin.** The column and the principal field exist; `key.create` refuses a `compendium_id` until `keys.CompendiumAccess` (a package variable) is set by the compendium domain, which must also check that the creator may open it. The compendium work should register `<kind>.list` ops (items with `id` and `title` or `name`, and `aliases` for entities) so that names resolve with no CLI change.

**CLI**

- **Exit codes changed.** The plan and `ops.Code.ExitCode` said not_found 4, conflict 5, auth 3; the CLI contract is the table in OPERATIONS.md (3 not found, 4 conflict, 5 auth, 6 validation, 7 rate limited, 8 unreachable, 9, 10). `ExitCode` is gone from `internal/ops` (the CLI cannot import it); the table lives in `internal/cli/exit.go`. An unknown server error code is mapped by its HTTP status.
- **Names are resolved on the client**, through `<kind>.list`, with the list's `q` as a shortcut that falls back to the full list, and `state=all` as a second try so a trashed project can be restored by name. A document name needs a project: `--project`, a path whose first part is the project, or an id.
- **Skill installs.** File targets (Claude, Cursor, Windsurf) hold the whole skill and end with a hash comment; block targets (`AGENTS.md`, `GEMINI.md`, the Copilot file) hold the compact skill (no command reference; the CLI prints it) between `scriobh:skill:begin` and `end` markers whose begin line carries the hash of the body. No manifest file: the markers are the record, so `skill check` and `uninstall` work from the files alone. The recipes `story` and `review` named in ARCHITECTURE are not written; the five recipes are `quickstart`, `drafting`, `compendium`, `import` and `export`.
- **The skill is rendered by the CLI.** `internal/cli` renders the skill from a schema; `internal/skillgen` calls it with the registry's schema, so the committed `SKILL.md` is what `scriobh-cli --skill` prints for a server on that schema, and the CLI's own copy follows its server rather than its build.

### Review fixes to the Phase 2 backend

- **Linked imports write their links in short chunks.** Inserting a document no longer re-reads the tree and re-parses its text per document inside the import transaction (2,000 notes with 150 links each held the single writer for 5 to 12 seconds, past the 5-second writer wait). The import transaction stores the documents (`docs.NewDoc.DeferLinks`), re-resolves links waiting for the new titles once (`links.Reresolve` over the union), and writes a `links.pending.<project>` marker. After it commits, `links.SyncBatch` stores the new documents' links from the wiki links `docs.Prepare` already parsed outside any transaction, in chunks of at most 8,000 rows or 120 ms of writer time, each chunk its own transaction with one resolver (one tree read per chunk, never carried between chunks), then clears the marker. A chunk skips a document that is gone or whose `content_hash` changed since it was parsed (whoever changed it wrote its links). The link phase runs under `context.WithoutCancel`, never fails an import that has committed (it adds a warning), and a leftover marker makes the next start rebuild that project. The links are derived data, so the documents and their links are no longer one atomic commit; an interrupted import is repaired, not rolled back. `links.Rebuild` reads and parses a page at a time outside the writer and writes through `SyncBatch`. The documents' own insert is still one transaction (about 0.5 s for 2,000 notes).
- **The start-up rebuild is versioned.** `links.rebuilt` now holds `links.IndexVersion`; a stored value that is older (the old `1`, or none) rebuilds everything once, and `Sync` rewrites `target_key` when it differs. Version 2 is the title folding change below.
- **Titles fold with Unicode normal form C and the full case fold** (`golang.org/x/text/unicode/norm` and `cases`), so a decomposed "Café" (what macOS file names are) matches a precomposed one and "STRASSE" matches "Straße". This adds `golang.org/x/text v0.44.0` as a direct requirement: it was only in the module graph (`go.sum` already held its hashes; go-git's test package reaches it), not in `go.mod`. It is one hand-added `go.mod` line with `go.sum` unchanged, `go mod verify` passes, and `go mod tidy` could not be run offline, so run it at merge. It can be replaced by a hand-written table if a new direct dependency is unwelcome. ASCII titles take a plain lower-casing fast path; a caser is created per call because they keep state.
- **System snapshots are not pins.** The copies a restore or a split keeps now carry their explanation in the note (`Before restore`, `Before split`) and no label; only a label a person sets pins a revision. They follow the ordinary retention rules (30 days in full, then one a day). Migration 0410 moves the text of any written by the first build from label to note. Editors still cannot delete them; retention does.
- **Prune and the throttle agree on "newest".** Both mean the row most recently written (`recorded_at`, then id). Pruning never drops it, and it counts as its day's survivor in the daily thinning.
- **A trashed project is its owner's alone.** `projects.RoleFunc` (and `loadProject`, `IDBySlug`) give a member who is not the owner the same "not found" a nonexistent project gives, so editors and reviewers cannot save, restore a version, import or read it; the owner and an instance admin keep their role, so restore, purge and reading still work. Archived projects stay writable. What the owner and an admin may do there is the rule in "Phase 6 fixes after the documentation pass" (read-only, one 409). The authz sweep resets the project to live between calls (`project.trash` really trashes), and `TestTrashedProjectIsInvisibleToOtherMembersForEveryOp` sweeps every project op against a trashed project.


## Phase 3 platform decisions

Decisions made building the platform items of Phase 3 (`scriobh bench`, HTML export, covers, the installable shell). SSE and the single-writer lease are elsewhere.

### `scriobh bench` and the numbers

**Measured, on this build, on the development laptop** (Apple silicon, 16 cores, a quiet machine, `make bench` plus `--best-of 3`, 30 timings per operation, pages driven through the handler without a socket so network time is excluded). The projects: 150k is 154131 words in 296 documents, 500k is 512509 words in 989 documents, 1M is 1014298 words in 1995 documents (parts, chapters and scenes; wiki links; scene metadata on every scene; saves and named snapshots on a sample).

| Operation | Budget (p95) | 150k p50 / p95 | 500k p50 / p95 | 1M p50 / p95 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `doc.get` | 150 ms | 0.27 ms / 0.41 ms | 0.76 ms / 1.43 ms | 1.49 ms / 1.89 ms |
| `page.chapter` | 150 ms | 4.86 ms / 5.96 ms | 15.91 ms / 17.39 ms | 32.19 ms / 33.77 ms |
| `page.project` | 400 ms | 4.79 ms / 5.95 ms | 15.79 ms / 17.39 ms | 32.78 ms / 33.71 ms |
| `search.query` | 80 ms | 3.13 ms / 3.31 ms | 5.40 ms / 5.46 ms | 7.00 ms / 7.12 ms |
| `page.search` | 150 ms | 3.18 ms / 3.23 ms | 5.59 ms / 6.02 ms | 7.42 ms / 7.59 ms |
| `doc.update` | 120 ms | 2.12 ms / 4.17 ms | 2.84 ms / 4.93 ms | 3.87 ms / 5.49 ms |
| `http.save` | 400 ms | 2.22 ms / 3.18 ms | 2.88 ms / 3.80 ms | 3.93 ms / 5.26 ms |
| `doc.list` | 400 ms | 0.69 ms / 1.08 ms | 2.23 ms / 2.94 ms | 4.64 ms / 6.09 ms |
| `outline.get` | 400 ms | 0.50 ms / 0.81 ms | 1.60 ms / 2.19 ms | 3.35 ms / 4.22 ms |
| `export.md.single` | none | 1.88 ms / 3.08 ms | 6.50 ms / 7.15 ms | 13.09 ms / 13.49 ms |
| `export.html.single` | none | 12.21 ms / 12.67 ms | 39.54 ms / 40.60 ms | 77.57 ms / 78.52 ms |
| `export.html.bundle` | none | 14.28 ms / 15.77 ms | 46.60 ms / 48.84 ms | 94.52 ms / 99.65 ms |
| `mirror.full_write` | none | 1.50 s | 4.66 s | 9.13 s |
| `search.rebuild` | 60 s | 37.50 ms | 98.36 ms | 201.00 ms |
| `backup.create` | 30 s | 67.35 ms | 193.10 ms | 373.20 ms |
| `db.size` | 1 GB | 5.9 MB | 17.3 MB | 34.6 MB |

**Every budget is met at every size, with a wide margin; the 500,000-word exit criterion (open a chapter under 150 ms, search under 80 ms) is met by a factor of 15 (search) to 200 (open a chapter)** (open 0.8 ms, the whole chapter page 16 ms, search 5.4 ms, all p50). Nothing needed fixing and no profile was needed. Three honest limits on that: the machine is far faster than the stated target (a mini-PC or a Raspberry Pi 5), so the margin there is smaller and the nightly job should be run on that class of hardware; the pages are measured without the network, TLS or the browser's own rendering; and the synthetic prose is a Zipf vocabulary, so FTS selectivity is plausible, not a real novel's. What to watch: the page time grows with the number of documents (the tree is rendered on every page: 5 ms at 300 documents, 16 ms at 1,000, 33 ms at 2,000) and the full mirror write is slow (4.7 s at 1,000 documents, 9 s at 2,000; fsync-bound, a background job, not a request).

- **Where it lives.** `internal/bench` (generator, measurements, baseline and verdict) and `cmd/scriobh/bench.go`; `make bench` runs 150k and 500k, `BENCH_SIZES=150k,500k,1m` adds the million words (a few minutes on a slow disk; about a minute on this one). `make ci` and the development repository's `ci.yml` run a 5,000-word smoke size only; its nightly workflow runs all three sizes.
- **Real ops, no raw SQL.** The project is built with `project.create`, `doc.create`, `doc.update` (scene metadata, saves) and `revision.snapshot`; measurements call the registry and the handler. The one row written by other means is the user, through the sign-in code the server uses. The environment is never read and a directory without the bench marker is refused, so a run cannot touch a real library.
- **Built once, measured on a copy.** The generated project is kept in `<dir>/base` when `--data-dir` is given and copied to `<dir>/work` for each run, because saves add revisions and a database that grew between runs would read as a regression in its own size.
- **Percentiles.** Nearest-rank p50 and p95 of `--runs` timings after one untimed warm-up. Repeated metrics are timed 30 times, exports `runs/6` (at least 3), and the mirror, rebuild, backup and size once.
- **The gate.** Budgets are judged on p95, only for projects up to 500,000 words (the budgets are stated for that; 1M is shown with them, not gated). Regressions are judged on p50, more than 20% and more than a noise floor (5 ms, 15 ms for 3 to 9 timings, 75 ms and 50% for one, 256 KB for sizes). The floors were widened after a run beside a busy test suite flagged a 4 ms change in a 16 ms page: the regression check needs a quiet machine, and the absolute budgets are the load-tolerant gate. A first draft of the same 500k run under contention also read `page.chapter` 19 ms against 16 ms quiet. A failing metric is re-timed once while the project is still open and the better run counts. `mirror.full_write` and `backup.create` are disk-bound and never judged against the baseline.
- **Baselines are per machine class.** A baseline from a laptop is meaningless on a runner, so `bench/baseline.json` is keyed by `os/arch/cpu-count`, and a run on an unknown machine is held to the absolute budgets only, with a note. The committed entry is the development laptop's; to gate regressions on CI, commit the runner's candidate baseline, which the nightly job uploads next to the report. `--write-baseline --best-of 3` records the best of three passes.
- **Deviation from the plan.** The plan said a 20% regression fails the build; here that is true only on a machine with a baseline entry, which the CI runner does not have until someone commits one. The absolute budgets gate everywhere.

### HTML export

- **A sibling op, `export.html`**, not a mode of `export.markdown`: its own options (`private_notes`), its own path (`POST /api/v1/projects/{id}/export/html`), the same manifest recording (a shared `record` helper; format `html` was already allowed by migration 1110), the same caps and the same editor role. The Export dialog posts `format=html` to the existing UI route.
- **It leaves out what the Markdown export keeps.** The Markdown export writes the stored text verbatim (front matter, `%%private notes%%`, HTML comments and the alternatives annotation included). The HTML export is a document to read and share, so by default it omits front matter, HTML comments and private notes, and `private_notes: true` keeps the notes, marked. Matching the Markdown export here would have put a writer's private asides and the alternatives JSON into a file made to be sent. If the Markdown export later drops private notes (F-079) the two will agree.
- **A whitelist renderer, not a sanitizer.** `text.RenderHTML` (`internal/text/html.go`) walks the parse tree the word counter reads and writes only the elements it names, with attributes it builds itself. Raw HTML, inline and block, is shown as escaped text; comments are dropped; a link keeps its address only for http, https, mailto and in-page targets; an image is kept only as a small inline raster `data:` URI, anything else becomes a note, so the file makes no request. Tables render as tables, though the editor holds them as source. Strikethrough is read by an HTML-only parser variant (`newHTMLParser`) so the counter's parser, and every count, is unchanged. A corpus test renders all of the editor's round-trip documents, checks every tag and attribute against the whitelist and checks that the visible text contains the search text.
- **The page protects itself.** A Content-Security-Policy `<meta>` allows the page's one inline `<style>` by its SHA-256 and inline images, and nothing else (no script, form, frame or remote load; `base-uri 'none'`). The stylesheet is fixed text (system serif fonts, flat colours, a print sheet, `prefers-color-scheme`); changing it changes the hash, and a Chromium test checks the style still applies.
- **Deterministic.** Anchors are `d<reading order>-<slug of the title>`, never ids; wiki links resolve by folded title to an anchor (single) or a relative page path (bundle); the bundle is a sorted, stored zip with a fixed time, and the manifest's `sha256` per page is the hash of the Markdown it was built from. Two exports are the same bytes, and two projects with the same content produce the same file. Golden files pin the single page, the bundle index and a page.
- **Headings.** The title is `h1` on the title page; a document's heading level is 2 plus its depth under other documents (notes sit under a "Notes" heading); headings inside a document are moved down by that level, so the outline stays a tree. In a bundle each page's document title is `h1`.
- **Not done.** `include_in_compile` is ignored by both exports, as before. There is no typeface or layout choice, no RSS or static-site folder (F-225 says "or a folder"; the zip is the folder).

### Covers

- **SVG only.** `internal/cover` draws a flat fill from the cover palette (the project's own colour, or one chosen from the title), the title wrapped by measured character widths (a per-class width table for a serif face) and shrunk before it is cut, a hairline rule, the subtitle, the author and the kind. Presentation attributes only, so it is inert under the app's CSP; invalid XML characters, control characters and bidirectional overrides are removed and the rest escaped. A PNG was not attempted: rasterising text needs a font and a rasteriser, and this build bundles neither.
- **One op, served as an image.** `project.cover` is a `GET`, reader role. A new optional interface (`ops.Inline`) makes the REST binding send an op's file inline with the op's ETag and answer 304, with its own `default-src 'none'; sandbox` policy; other downloads are unchanged. The cache is in memory, keyed by the hash of what the cover shows and bounded to 512 covers.
- **The library tiles are unchanged** (they use the `cover-0..7` CSS classes). To adopt the generated cover, replace the tile's `.cover` span with `<img class="cover" src="/api/v1/projects/{{.ID}}/cover" alt="" width="600" height="800">`; the image revalidates cheaply. The HTML export's title page already uses it, inline.

### The installable shell

- **Policy: the worker keeps the shell and one offline page, never a page.** Authenticated HTML, fragments and API answers carry a person's name, CSRF token and manuscript, and Cache Storage belongs to the browser profile, not the session: the next person to sign in on that profile would be handed the last person's cached pages. Rather than key and purge a page cache by session (and trust sign-out, expiry and revocation to always run it), nothing personal is ever stored. Navigations go to the network and fall back to the offline page only when it cannot be reached; the worker installs with no cookies and the offline page is built once without a session. Chapter text that was never saved survives in the writing view's own drafts, which do not use the worker. The cost is that a chapter cannot be opened offline; offline reading (F-262) needs per-person storage and is a later feature. Cached static files are public and named by content, so cache-first is safe.
- **Versioned by content.** The cache is named for a hash of every static file, the worker's source and the offline page; any change is a new worker and a new cache; old caches are deleted on activation. The precache is every file under `/static` (about 1 MB with the editor and fonts), by hashed name, and the fonts also by the plain name the stylesheet requests. `cache.addAll` is atomic, so a missing file fails the install rather than activating half a shell, and a test fetches every listed URL.
- **Consent to update.** A new worker waits. `pwa.js` shows "A new version of Scríobh is ready" with Reload and Later and dispatches a `scriobh:update-ready` event (detail `apply()`) for the UI to use its own toast; Reload messages the worker to skip waiting and reloads on the controller change. The worker claims clients on first activation, which is not an update.
- **Where the files live.** The worker's source is `web/static/sw/worker.js`, outside `static/js` so the shell JavaScript budget does not count it, and is served at `/sw.js` with a line of configuration in front (root scope, `Cache-Control: no-store`, `Service-Worker-Allowed: /`). The CSP gains `worker-src 'self'` and `manifest-src 'self'` and nothing is loosened. `pwa.js` is 0.8 KB gzip (budget 1 KB; it is part of the shell total) and the worker 1.4 KB (budget 1.5 KB).
- **Icons without files.** The install icons (192, 512, maskable 512 and the 180 touch icon) are drawn at first request by `internal/pwa` from the favicon's geometry with the standard library, so no binary is committed. The manifest colours are Paper (`#FBFAF7`); a manifest has one theme colour, so the dark shell colour comes from the page's own `theme-color` meta.
- **Test notes.** In this Chromium, `context.setOffline(true)` does reach the worker's own requests, so the first offline step is the context switch as asked; a second step stops the server and restarts it on the same port. Playwright's blocked-worker mode logs a console warning on every page, which the e2e kit now ignores (the scripts block workers so a worker cannot answer requests they count). Not tested: an update flow end to end (it needs a second build), installation from the browser's menu, and any browser but Chromium.

---

## Phase 2 screens: budget and slot notes

- **The CSS budget is per page load.** ARCHITECTURE.md's 25 KB initial CSS means the sheets a page loads: the base sheet and that page's own. The test used to sum every sheet in the folder, which would have forced a page-specific sheet (goals, import, outline, themes) to compete with all the others. `TestCSSBudgetPerPage` renders every page and sums what it links, at most 25 KB each (the write view is the largest, at about 18.8 KB), and a 40 KB cap on all sheets together still catches sprawl. The number was not raised; it is measured where it matters.
- **The decoration slot** (see DESIGN.md, the editor): one registry, `scriobh.decorate`, so focus dimming and wiki-link marks no longer replace each other.
- **Instance administrators and the UI routes.** Decided in the Phase 3 review (see "Phase 3 screens: review fixes"): the ops keep "an administrator acts as the owner", and on the pages an administrator who is not a member is a stranger, by slug and by id alike. `TestAdminWithoutMembershipIsAStrangerOnThePagesAndAnOwnerInTheOps` pins both halves.
- **The inspector saves what was typed before a tab closes or a person signs out.** `scriobh.inspector.flush()` is awaited by the sign-out path (together with the writing view's own flush) and the sign-out stays put if it cannot; closing the tab sends the queued fields in a keepalive request. The outline queues its moves and draws only the newest refresh, so two quick moves cannot act on a stale picture of the list.


---

## Phase 2 release notes: what is not verified

- **HTTPS Git transport against a real host.** Token push, a second push and an HTTPS clone are covered by tests against a local `git http-backend` behind a TLS test server (see the Git sync decisions); nothing has been run against a real Forgejo or GitHub, over SSH or HTTPS.
- **Touch drag in the outline and the tree**, a real on-screen keyboard, Safari, Firefox and any real phone: only Chromium emulation was run. The tested touch route is the row buttons and menus.
- **Screen readers.** Roles, names, live regions and focus return were built and checked in code and Playwright; nobody listened to them in VoiceOver, NVDA or TalkBack.
- **The Docker image and compose stack**, and the CI workflows (they had never been run).
- **Target hardware.** The benchmarks ran on a laptop; the mini-PC and Pi 5 budgets are unmeasured.
- **A restore on a clean machine** is rehearsed by tests and the runbook, not on a second computer.


---

## Phase 3 research and streaming decisions

Research notes, clippings, tags, the scratchpad, attachments and the live-update stream. Ops in `internal/domain/research` and `internal/domain/attachments`, the hub in `internal/events`, the route in `internal/server/sse.go` and `files.go`; migrations `0340_research`, `0350_tags`, `0360_scratchpads`, `0800_files`, `0920_research_search`.

**Research is its own table, not `documents`.** The plan said "documents of kind `research` with `compendium_id`". `documents.project_id` is `NOT NULL`, so a Compendium with no project cannot own one; rebuilding `documents` to relax it would have meant dropping a table every other table points at, in a migration that collides with the point-of-view work; and a note in `documents` would have needed a kind filter in `doc.list` (which has none), the outline, the goals, the compile, the search and the mirror. `research_notes` (exactly one of `project_id` and `compendium_id`) keeps all of that out by construction. What it keeps from documents: a tree with REAL positions, trash and restore by batch, the `base_revision` check on save, a search index written in the same transaction (`research_text`/`research_fts`), attachment-link tracking, and tags through a join table with real foreign keys. What it does not have, and which are not built: revision history, wiki links to and from notes, and word counts in the goals (research is not manuscript). `documents.compendium_id` stays unused.

**Twin ops.** An op declares one kind of authorization, so every research, tag, scratchpad and attachment op is registered twice, `project.<noun>.<verb>` and `compendium.<noun>.<verb>`, over one handler that scopes every query by the owner the registry has already authorized. Both sweeps substitute a child id for the second path parameter (`{note_id}`, `{tag_id}`, `{att_id}`) from fixtures they make again before each request.

**Compendium research is invisible through the project.** A project reader who is not on the Compendium cannot list, get, search or board the Compendium's notes by any id (404, the same body as a missing one), and a project's research search never reads a Compendium's rows: the index tables carry the owner and every query is scoped to one. `search.compendium` also returns the best research matches (`research`, `research_total`) beside the entities.

**Clippings are typed, not fetched.** `source_url` must be http or https (a `javascript:`, `data:` or `file:` address is refused), `retrieved_on` a real date, `quote` up to 20,000 characters. The server makes no request for an address, ever.

**Tags.** Per owner, unique by folded name, one of eight colour tokens; renaming moves every note, merging keeps a note's other tags and drops the duplicate, deleting leaves the notes. Saving a note with a new tag name creates the tag. Tags on manuscript documents are not built (`document_tags` exists). Tag colour and an unused tag are not in the mirror; a tag on a note is.

**The scratchpad.** One private pad per person per project and per Compendium, 256 KB, saved with the revision it started from. Rules (each tested): every op reads and writes only the caller's own row, so an administrator's get is the administrator's own pad; no event, no audit detail, no search row, no export, no mirror file, no Git push; membership is all it needs (a reader may keep notes on a book they read) and ending membership hides it again, with the text kept for if they return; every foreign key cascades, so a purge of the person, the project or the Compendium takes it. It is in the database, so a backup holds it and so does anyone with the file: it is private from other users of Scríobh, not encrypted at rest.

**Attachments: content-addressed per owner.** `files/projects/<id>/attachments/<sha256>.<ext>` and the Compendium equivalent; the same bytes uploaded twice to one owner are one row (`duplicate: true`), to two owners are two files. The row never stores a path; the display name is a label only (`CleanName` keeps the last path segment, drops separators, control and invisible characters, and caps the length).

**Type policy.** Allowed: PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, PDF, plain text and Markdown. The bytes decide (magic numbers, `http.DetectContentType`, UTF-8 with no NUL for text); the file name and the declared Content-Type are only cross-checked, and a name or type that claims one kind while the bytes are another is refused rather than believed or corrected. An extension outside the list (`.exe`, `.html`, `.svg`, `.sh`, a double extension ending in one) is refused. Text that starts with `#!` is refused (a script). **SVG is refused**, by extension, by contents (`<svg`, `<?xml`) and wherever it is hiding in a text file: it can carry script and external references, a sanitiser written here would be the weakest link in the program, and rasterising needs a renderer that is not bundled. A writer who needs a vector figure uploads a PNG or a PDF. HTML inside a `.txt` is accepted and inert: text is only ever served as a `text/plain` download. Images are checked with `image.DecodeConfig` (damaged or over 100 megapixels is refused); a text file is read end to end for NUL bytes and invalid UTF-8, not only the part detection sees.

**Serving.** `GET /files/{id}` for sessions (an API key does not read files through it; it uses `*.attachment.download`). Images and PDFs inline, everything else attachment; `nosniff`; `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; sandbox` (a PDF omits `sandbox`, which stops the browsers' PDF viewers, and gets `frame-ancestors 'self'` and `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN` so the page can show it in a panel once its own CSP allows `frame-src 'self'`; that is the UI work); `Cache-Control: private, no-cache` with an ETag, so every use is revalidated and still authorized; ranges work. A missing attachment and one you cannot read are one 404.

**Event cursor in the mirror.** The mirror now moves its event cursor after it has marked what it read, so a reader that waits for the cursor (a test, a status page) cannot see "caught up" while a change is not yet pending. The start-up race in the tests was a different one (the test created a project before the start-up check had run) and is fixed by waiting for `Started()`.

**Quota defaults.** 25 MB a file, 2 GB a person, 1 GB a project or Compendium, no instance cap (`SCRIOBH_UPLOAD_MAX_MB`, `SCRIOBH_QUOTA_USER_MB`, `SCRIOBH_QUOTA_PROJECT_MB`, `SCRIOBH_QUOTA_INSTANCE_MB`; `0` is no cap). Counted after de-duplication, per person by who uploaded. The counters are rows kept by triggers on `attachments`, so a purge's cascade is counted too, and the upload reads them inside its own transaction: the writer is single, so two uploads cannot both squeeze under a quota.

**Upload is `NoDryRun`.** It writes a file, so a preview is refused with `dry_run_unsupported` after authorization and before a byte of the body is read (tested: no bytes read, no file or row left). The limits are captured in a closure at registration, so they are the same in a preview's environment, which has no services and a scratch data directory. The raw route does not use the idempotency store: content addressing makes a repeat harmless, and a body of up to 25 MB is not hashed twice.

**Refcount delete.** `*.attachment.delete` is refused with 409 (`refs` in the error) while a document or a note links to it, unless `force`. After the commit the file is removed under the lock uploads take, after checking that no row names it again. A purge removes the whole owner folder through the purge hooks (which also removes a project's covers folder: all of a project's files go with it).

**Where links are tracked.** `attachment_refs` is written when a document or a research note is saved (including a revision restore and a split) and is a hint. A delete, and the list of uses, also search the owner's text for the attachment's id: documents (trashed ones too) and research notes, and for a Compendium its entities' bodies and summaries; at most 500 hits, a substring match on one id, run only for those two ops. So a link that has no recorded row (a bulk import, text written before the attachment existed, a lost record, an entity body) still blocks a delete without `force`. The recorded rows remain what the mirror and the exports read.

**Links in exports.** The Markdown is kept exactly as stored: `/files/att_...` is not rewritten to a relative path in the Markdown bundle, the mirror or a Git push. Each carries the files under `attachments/<sha256 prefix>-<name>.<ext>` and a manifest entry per file (`id`, `path`, `sha256`, `bytes`, `mime`, `filename`, `alt`) that maps the id to the file. Rewriting would change the text's hash and the importer's round trip; a later round can do it in the reader. The importer ignores attachments (not done). The bundle caps its attachments at 192 MB (it is built in memory) and refuses with a way out (`attachments: false`). A Git push leaves out an attachment over 16 MB, one the mirror lists but cannot supply (a restore made without files) and one whose bytes do not match their hash, with its manifest entry in each case, so the published list never names a file that is not there and a missing image never blocks a push; the Git quotas (`SCRIOBH_GIT_TOTAL_QUOTA_MB`) count the local repository, attachments included, and gate imports and the scratch fetch of a refused push, as before, while an ordinary push is bounded per snapshot (512 MB in all, 16 MB a file); the pre-push secret check skips binary attachments (a PNG can match a pattern by chance) and still reads text ones.

**Cost of the mirror's new content.** The research of a project (and of the Compendiums it uses) is read and compared on every flush, not carried forward; measured here, a chapter save in a project with 600 notes of about 2 KB reaches the mirror in about 30 ms with nothing in the research rewritten (`TestChapterSaveWithALargeResearchLibrary`, which prints the numbers), and the cost grows with the amount of research text, not with its number of files, since unchanged files are compared by hash and left alone. The flush is on the mirror's worker, never on the save. The first write of a large library is as slow as the disk (files are synced before they are renamed, about 5 ms each).

**Mirror.** A project's own notes are `research/<folder>--<id6>/<note>--<id6>.md` (a folder's own text is its `_folder.md`), a project's view of the research of the Compendiums it uses is `compendium/research/<compendium>--<id6>/…` (rendered every flush, not carried forward with the entity projection, because its fingerprint is not the entities'), and a standalone Compendium has `research/` and `attachments/` of its own. Research front matter is hand-written in a fixed key order with every string JSON-quoted, so an unchanged note is the same bytes; the notes' and attachments' metadata is part of each fingerprint, so a restart finds a stale mirror. Binary files are copied from `data/files` and recorded by SHA-256 (their name on disk); a missing or damaged source is skipped and logged, not a reason to stop. Project-local research is not in the Git push (only manuscript, notes and attachments are).

**SSE: one sequence, a hub that exists only while someone listens.** `events.seq` is the cursor. The hub reads new rows when the store signals a commit (with a 5-second backstop) and hands each event to the subscribers of its project and its Compendium; a subscriber records the hub's cursor under the lock delivery takes, replays the table up to it, then reads its channel, so there is no gap and no duplicate. Replay window: 1,000 events or 24 hours (`SCRIOBH_SSE_REPLAY_*`); past it, or from the future, the client gets `reset`. Heartbeat 25 s; write deadline 10 s per frame (the server's own read and write timeouts are cleared for the stream); buffer 256 events, then `reset` and close; 10 streams a person, 200 an instance. Access is re-checked on every heartbeat (membership, key and session) without counting as activity (`Sessions.Peek`), so an open tab cannot keep a session alive past the idle cap. Shutdown ends the streams with `bye` before `Shutdown` waits for them.

**What an event may carry.** Ids, types, revisions, counts, short enum tokens and the changed field names (the allow-list is `internal/events/payload.go`; a value must be a number, a flag or a token of letters, digits and `_.:-`, up to 64 characters, or a short list of them). Nothing else leaves the process even if an op puts it in a payload, and a test runs every op that has a dry-run fixture for real and checks the stored payloads against the same list. A project stream drops `compendium_id`, `target_compendium_id`, `previous` and the entity ids (`entity_id`, `to_entity_id`, `source_id`, `target_id`) from payloads, and a Compendium stream drops `project_id`, because a project reader is not thereby a Compendium member and the reverse. `by_you` replaces the actor's id. Events are never pruned (nothing deletes from `events`); the replay window bounds what is read, not what is kept.

**Security review follow-ups**

- **Dry-run headers fail closed.** `Prefer` is parsed per RFC 7240 and `X-Scriobh-Dry-Run` accepts 1/true/yes/on; a value that is neither a yes nor a no is a 400, because the one thing a preview must never be is a commit.
- **Keys made by keys.** `api_keys.parent_key_id` (migration 1620) records which key made a key. The child's expiry is cut to the parent's (including "never"), revoking a parent revokes its whole tree in one transaction (a recursive query, audited with the count), and revoking a child leaves the parent. A key pinned to a project or compendium cannot reach key management at all; `create` also refuses a pin different from the caller's if that ever changes.
- **Rate limits.** A key spends its bucket and its owner's; failed key lookups spend a fixed 30-a-minute allowance per client address (the same shape as the sign-in limit) and are answered 429 once it is gone. The limiter takes an injected clock for tests. The user bucket is shared with the person's browser session, so an agent that runs flat out slows the person's own tabs; that is the price of one allowance per person.
- **Idempotency storage** is capped per caller in entries and bytes with newest-kept eviction; a response bigger than the whole byte allowance leaves a tombstone like a secret does.

**Review fixes.**

- **Replay starts at access.** A stream replays nothing from before the caller's access began: their membership of the project or Compendium, and for a key the later of its creation and the creator's membership. `Last-Event-ID: 0` from someone added today is a `reset`. An administrator with no membership and no key is not clamped.
- **Project streams and the bible.** On a project stream, an event about the bible (`entity.*`, `compendium.*`, `project.compendium.*`) reaches someone who cannot read the project's primary Compendium as an anonymous `compendium.changed` with an empty payload; a member of that Compendium gets the specific type. `event_id` (a timeline event) never rides a project stream. A Compendium that is only referenced is judged by the primary: a member of the primary hears the specific type for events about a referenced one.
- **Files are opened, not followed.** `GET /files/{id}` and the download op open a stored file only if no component of its path under `data/files` is a symbolic link, it is a regular file, and the file opened is the one checked; anything else is the same 404 as a missing file.
