A preview and an apply parse the source again each time (the source is sent twice; nothing is kept between).
- **Provenance:** `project_sources` kind `markdown` (the `kind` list is a CHECK constraint; changing it means rebuilding the table, as for `export.submission`), `layout`, the hash of the binder or export file, and a report of counts in `metadata_json`. Each document has a `document_sources` row whose path is where it was in the source (`Draft/Part One/The Road/Dusk`, `wordpress/post/<slug>`).
- **Layouts are chosen, or recognised by their own markers.** `layout` is `auto` (default), `obsidian`, `scrivener`, `wordpress` or `ghost`. With `auto` a source is one of the three only when the files hold **no Markdown** and there is a `.scrivx`, or exactly one `.xml` or `.json` whose first 64 KB say it is a WordPress or Ghost export; the preview's `layout` says what was read. A folder of Markdown with a stray XML file is a folder of Markdown. A Git import never takes them. A wrong source is refused with the reason, never guessed at.
- **Options** (all four ops): `scrivener_research` (`notes` default, `skip`), `scrivener_notes` (`private` default, `skip`), `wp_status` and `ghost_status` (`published` default, `all`), `wp_types` and `ghost_types` (`posts` default, `pages`, `both`). An unknown value is a 422.
- **Result shapes** (what a screen must show). `PreviewOut` gained `research` (nodes: `key`, `parent_key`, `kind` note or folder, `title`, `words`, `tags`, `entity_candidate`), `research_total` and `report` (a map of counts by what was read and not kept: for Scrivener `labels`, `keywords`, `statuses_not_mapped`, `annotations`, `comments`, `footnotes`, `images`, `underline`, `document_links`, `document_links_made`, `trashed`, `research_images`, `research_pdfs`, `files_not_read_by_type`, `snapshot_files` ...; for WordPress `comments`, `attachments`, `attachment_urls` (the first 20), `categories`, `tags`, `custom_field_keys`, `image_addresses`, `shortcodes_removed`, `classic_editor_posts` ...; for Ghost `sources` (lexical, mobiledoc markdown card, markdown, html), `unknown_nodes`, `embeds_noted`, `markdown_raw_html_dropped`, `markdown_broken_up` ...), and `warnings` says each in a sentence. Layout is one of `markdown-folder`, `scriobh`, `obsidian`, `docx`, `scrivener`, `wordpress`, `ghost`. `edits` by `key` may rename (`title`) or leave out (`skip`) a research note or folder (a folder takes what is inside it); a research note's kind cannot be edited. `ApplyOut` gained `research_notes` (notes and folders written). A screen must show the research tree and the report before apply, say **in words** that nothing in Research that is a file (an image, a PDF, a web page) was imported, and offer the character and place sheets as a list to turn into entities one by one.
- **Caps** (the folder importer's own, plus): a zipped Scrivener project has at most 40,000 entries (the directory is walked before the archive is opened), 32 MB for one RTF file, 1 MB for a synopsis, 32 MB for the binder, **96 MB of text in all, however the archive describes itself**; an export file at most 64 MB (96 MB in all, in a zip), 60,000 WordPress items or Ghost posts; 2,000 documents of 2 MiB, 5,000 documents in all, 5,000 research notes of 1 MB. Over HTTP the body is 48 MiB: a big Scrivener project is sent as a zip. A file that is there and is not read (over a limit, or listed without its bytes) leaves its document empty and the preview names it. The command line reads a `.zip`, a `.scriv` folder (symbolic links are not followed, dot-folders are skipped) or an `.xml` or `.json` file.

### Safety of the readers

- **RTF** (`rtf.go`): one pass, constant work per byte, no rescanning; groups nest at most 64 (deeper ones are counted and dropped, no stack); `\bin` and pictures, objects and every destination the reader does not know are skipped and counted without buffering; text is capped at 8 MB per file, runs at 600,000 (past that the formatting goes and the text stays), paragraphs at 400,000, notes at 5,000 (a note in a note is dropped); control words longer than 32 letters are ignored; parameters are clamped; `\uN` joins surrogate pairs, a lone half and the non-characters are U+FFFD, `\ucN` (0 to 16) skips what follows; `\'hh` uses the document's `\ansicpg` for the single-byte code pages (437, 850, 852, 866, 874, 1250 to 1258, Mac Roman), and a double-byte one is counted and replaced. A panic is recovered where the converter starts (the command line has no HTTP binding to do it).
- **XML** (`xdom.go`): decoded to UTF-8 first (a byte-order mark, UTF-16 both ways, a declared single-byte encoding, Windows-1252 when invalid with no declaration; control characters XML forbids become spaces and are counted); strict, no entities (an undefined one is an error with a line), a DOCTYPE or entity definition refused outright; every token of every kind pays from a budget, depth is 80 (420 for a binder), text is bounded; a skipped subtree (a post's 200,000 comments) is read under the same budget and the same depth limit and not built.
- **HTML** (`htmlmd.go`): a tokenizer, not a tree: nesting costs a counter (200 open frames, then counted and unwrapped), no token size limit (a limit would end a post silently: a 5 MB data address, a long paragraph or comment is read through), one run of text is one string builder (400,000 one-letter spans are one run). Dropped with their contents: script, style, iframe, object, embed, form and its fields, svg, canvas, audio, video, noscript, template, math. Links are kept for http, https and mailto only (the address is percent-encoded so it cannot end its link); an image is `*[image: its description]*`, its address counted and the first 20 reported, never fetched.
- **Markdown from an export** (Ghost cards, mobiledoc): never passed through. It is parsed (goldmark, raw HTML off), rendered to HTML and read by the same walker, so what it says is text; raw HTML in it is dropped and counted; a leading `---` block, `%%`, `[[`, `<scriobh-ghost>` and the alternatives comment are text. `mdbound.go` first breaks up what makes a Markdown parser quadratic: more than 16 quote or list markers on a line, more than 64 columns of indent, a paragraph over 8 KB (cut at a blank line, a very long line at a space); four shapes took **minutes** at 2 MiB before (deep quotes, `- - - -`, a run of unclosed `[a](` and `![a](`) and the worst takes 2.6 s now, with the heaviest peaking at 674 MB of heap (tables); real text (no real quote is sixteen levels deep, no real paragraph 8 KB of one unbroken run) is unchanged, and a test pins that. A Markdown card over 2 MiB is not imported (the document would be refused anyway) and the Markdown handed the parser is 24 MB in all. A lexical document nested past 64 levels or past 2,000,000 nodes is cut and counted.
- **Private notes** (`%%…%%`). An annotation is one pair of delimiters per **text block**: it is closed at the end of the paragraph, heading text, list item or table cell it is in and opened again in the next one (a heading's note moves to a paragraph of its own after it), an empty one is no note (`%%%%` would be literal text), `%%` in a note's own words is escaped, and a percent sign that touches a delimiter is escaped (`a\%%%secret%%\%b`), which is the form the editor's corpus holds as canonical. Tests assert, for sixteen block contexts (inline, across paragraphs, a whole paragraph, a heading, a list item, bold, a link, a footnote, a comment, empty, two in a row ...), that nothing of a note is in `text.Structure`, the search text or the word count and that no wiki link is made. The notes of a document (`notes.rtf`) are one private note per paragraph after the text, prefixed `Notes:`; research notes get none of the document links.
- **Nothing can become a construct it was not written as**: a line that starts `#`, `-`, `+`, `=`, `:`, `~`, a number and a dot, or `>`; `[[`; `[^1]`; `%%`; `<scriobh-ghost>`; `<!--`; four spaces; a link address with a bracket, a parenthesis or a newline: all are escaped or encoded, and a test per source asserts it through the structure parser. A link to another Scrivener document is a wiki link only when the title is the only one of its name in the manuscript and holds none of `[]|#^\<>%*_~&{}()!:$` or a control character; the text of a link whose label is unsafe stays as it was written, with no link.
- **No outbound request** is made by any of this: an image address, an attachment address, a bookmark, an embed, a `src` or an `href` is text or a count. The converters take no network and no clock.

### What becomes what

- **Scrivener.** Draft: a folder that holds folders is a `part`, a folder of documents a `chapter`, a document in a chapter a `scene`, a document in the Draft or a part a `chapter`; a folder named Front Matter, Title Page, Copyright, Dedication, Epigraph, Foreword or Preface (or Back Matter, Acknowledgements, Appendix, About the Author or Afterword) at the top is `front_matter` or `back_matter` and what is in it scenes; the preview lets the writer change a kind. A folder's own text is its document's text. **Status** is mapped by name: `to do`, `idea` idea; `outline` outline; `in progress`, `first draft`, `draft`, `started` draft; `revised draft`, `second draft`, `revise`, `editing` revise; `final draft`, `done`, `complete`, `finished` done; anything else draft and listed by name. **Labels** and **keywords** of manuscript documents are counted and listed in the import's record, not kept (a document has no label, and `document_tags` has no writer); on a research note they are its **tags**. **Include in Compile: No** is "do not compile". A synopsis is the document's synopsis (cut at 300 characters with the whole in the long synopsis). Bold, italic and strikethrough are kept; **underline is dropped and counted** (Markdown has none, and in a typewriter manuscript it means italics, which a program cannot tell from emphasis: the warning says to check them). Headings come from the paragraph style (`heading N`, `title`, `subtitle`) or `\outlinelevel`, lists from `\ls` with the marker text, a quote from a style named quote; a table row is flattened to a paragraph and counted; hidden text is left out and counted; images are marked `[image]`. **Research** is notes in folders (a text document with children is a folder holding its own text as its first note); a file that is not text becomes a note that names it (`[attachment: Map (Image) was not imported]`). Notes under Characters, Places, Locations or Settings are **candidates** (`entity_candidate`) and are still research notes. **Trash**, anything outside Draft, Research and Trash, snapshots, collections, custom metadata and settings are counted. A duplicate binder id is reported (both documents read the same file). The project's title is `ProjectTitle`, else the folder's name; its author `FullName`; its kind `blank` (the writer sets it).
- **WordPress.** Posts oldest first (the date as `story_date` after the `storydate` package accepts it, `YYYY-MM-DD`), pages in a part called Pages by `menu_order` after them, kind `blog`, language and subtitle from the channel, the author when one person wrote them all (a display name; addresses are never read). **Status:** `publish` done; `private` done; `pending` revise; `future` outline; `draft` draft; the original status is in the report; anything but `publish` is "do not compile"; `auto-draft`, `trash`, `inherit` and other types (menus, revisions, custom types) are never imported and counted. A title is HTML-decoded (WordPress stores `&amp;`). The slug is in the source path; there is no slug field. Gutenberg blocks and classic text both: with no `<!-- wp:` the post is classic, a blank line is a paragraph and a single new line a break, whatever tags it also has. `[caption]`, `[gallery]`, `[embed]` and the like are removed (their tags, not what they enclose; unknown `[brackets]` stay text); `[footnote]…[/footnote]`, `[ref]` and `[fn]` become footnotes, and the footnotes block (`sup data-fn` with the `footnotes` post meta) too; a table is a pipe table. Comments are counted (their authors, addresses and text are never read), attachments listed by address.
- **Ghost.** As WordPress, with `published` done, `sent` done, `scheduled` outline, `draft` draft and anything not published "do not compile"; the date is `published_at`, else `created_at` (text or milliseconds); `custom_excerpt` is the synopsis; a page has `type: page`, or the old `page` flag. A post is read from its **lexical**, else a **mobiledoc that holds only Markdown cards**, else its `markdown`, else its **`html`** (a mobiledoc with other cards is read from the `html`; the mobiledoc sections themselves are not read). A lexical document that gives no text falls back to `html`. Lexical: paragraph, heading, quote, list (nested), horizontal rule, code block, image (a note, with its caption), html, markdown, callout, toggle, bookmark (a link), embed, gallery (a note each); text format bits for bold, italic, strikethrough and code, links; a card it does not know is listed by name.

### Decisions the owner may want to revisit

- **Attachments were not built** (the brief allowed report-only). `attachments.upload` takes a request body, an upload slot and files placed after the transaction; an import would have to store blobs and rows in its own transaction, give the text their ids before it is hashed, and remove the blobs on a rollback. It is a piece of its own for every importer (Word, Obsidian, Scrivener), and the importer counts the files and says so. Nothing in this work makes a later attachment path harder: a converter already knows each image's name, alt text and place.
- **Entities were not built.** Characters and Places become research notes with a mark; a Compendium is shared by several books, creating into one needs an explicit choice of which and a check of the person's role there, and `entity.create` refuses a name another entity has (which would roll back a whole import). The preview lists the candidates so a screen can offer them one by one.
- **Comments** (Scrivener's, WordPress's) are reported, not imported as project comments: an anchored comment needs an author and a place, and inventing either would be a guess. Scrivener annotations and comments found inside the text are kept as private notes, so what the writer wrote to themself is not lost and never leaves the document.
- **Not published is "do not compile", not a status**, because `documents.status` has no `published` or `private`; the original status is in the report.
- **Underline** is dropped and counted, as in the Word import.
- **`project_sources.kind` is `markdown`** for all three, not a new value (see above).
- **Not done:** Scrivener snapshots as revisions, collections, custom metadata, targets, the Scrivener 1 and Windows 1.x formats, `.webarchive` research, mobiledoc sections and atoms, Ghost newsletters and members, WordPress menus and custom post types, a re-import diff.

### After an independent review

Findings, each with a test that failed before the fix.

- A Scrivener document over the file limit was imported empty with no word, and an RTF with a big picture in it never reached the reader that skips pictures: the limit is 32 MB and the document is named when it is not read; the check for "has files" scanned the whole index for every item (now a set).
- The HTML walker stopped without a word at any token over 4 MB (a data address, a long paragraph or comment): the limit is gone and a read that ended early is counted.
- A classic WordPress post that has any block tag lost its paragraphs: classic mode now reads blank lines and new lines in text whatever tags are around.
- Ghost Markdown cards went to the parser at any size: four inputs took minutes at 2 MiB; bounded as above, with a test for 19 shapes and a peak-heap measure.
- A link with an unsafe label replaced the writer's words with the title: the words stay.
- `[footnote]` shortcodes were found by a search that rescanned the post for each (100,000 took 14 s; 0.15 s now); a post with no `excerpt:encoded` crashed the reader (found by the scale test).
- A lone high surrogate was dropped without U+FFFD and U+FFFF was kept as a character.
- Imported research is in the mirror (a test); the private-note forms are in the editor's corpus.


### Phase 6 import fix round (after a second independent review)

Each item below has a regression test that fails on the code before it.

- **A link inside a private note was a visible wiki link** (`rtfmd.go`): the `scrivlnk` case dropped the private flag, so an annotation holding a link to another document put a `[[link]]` with the annotation's words in the text. A link is now made only from visible text; inside a note, a comment, a footnote's note or a link label that holds a note, the words stay private text. `TestWikiLinkInsideAPrivateNoteStaysPrivate` (annotation, comment, word annotation, nested, in a footnote, in a list item, inside a link label, across paragraphs).
- **The 1 MB cut of a research note could split a `%%` pair** (`research/importer.go`): the cut now takes the whole last line from its first `%%` on (a note is one pair inside one line, as the emitter writes it), so no opener is left; the word count is of the cut text. Every other cut was checked: titles, tags, synopses and addresses are single-line fields with no private-note meaning; a Markdown body is refused over 2 MiB, never cut; the footnote budget drops whole notes; the run limit never merges across a note (below). `TestImportedBodyCutDoesNotSplitAPrivateNote`, `TestResearchCutNeverLeavesAnOpenPrivateNote` (the note straddles the cut at 30 offsets), `TestImportedResearchOverTheLimitKeepsItsAnnotationsPrivate` (a research RTF of about 1 MB that ends in an annotation, through the converter).
- **Zip entries are never allocated by what they declare** (`convin.go`, `files.go`): the buffer starts at 32 KB and grows with what comes out; every byte read counts against a budget of twice the total, rejected entries included, for the Scrivener, export and generic folder readers alike. 39,000 stored entries declaring 32 MB each: 54 ms, 61 MB peak (before: an allocation of 32 MB per entry). The archive reader itself refuses a stream longer than its header says, so an entry that holds more than it says is unreadable and listed. `TestZipEntriesAreNotAllocatedByTheirDeclaredSize`, `TestZipTotalIsBoundedWhateverTheEntriesSay`.
- **Ghost JSON is read as a stream** (`ghost.go`): one list item at a time with `json.Decoder` tokens, counting posts (60,000), tags, post-tag pairs, users and post-author pairs (200,000 each) as it goes and refusing above them. `{"posts":[{},{},...]}` of 36 MB (12 million posts): refused in 24 ms, 75 MB peak (before: the whole list built). A field of the wrong kind (a number for a title, an object for html) is read as empty and counted in a warning; the export is no longer refused for it. A bare `{"posts":...}` root keeps its tags, post-tag pairs, users and settings. `TestGhostExportIsRefusedWithoutBeingBuilt`, `TestGhostTolerantOfOneWrongField`.
- **XML builds only what is read** (`xdom.go`, `wordpress.go`): attributes are allocated when there is one and at most 64 are kept per element, an element keeps at most 100,000 children, and a post and the channel build only the children a list names (the rest is discarded as a comment is, and counted without a warning). The decoder reads every attribute of a tag before handing it over, so a tag over 1 MiB is refused first, on the bytes (CDATA, comments and processing instructions are skipped, so the HTML of a post is not mistaken for a long tag). 9 million empty children in an item or in the channel: 2.4 s, 217 MB peak with a 108 MB input in memory; one element with 36 MB of attributes: refused in 24 ms. `TestXMLManyChildrenAndAttributesAreNotBuilt`.
- **Footnotes are written once** (`htmlmd.go`): each footnote id is read and rendered once (a cache per post), reading stops when the notes are as many or as large as they may be, and a marker forged with a character reference cites the same note. 20,000 citations of a 100 KB note: 18 ms, 8 MB (before: 2 GB of copies). `TestFootnotesAreWrittenOnceHoweverOftenTheyAreCited`.
- **The RTF run limit was vacuous** (`rtf.go`): it flushed first and then checked an open run that was never there. Past 600,000 runs new text joins the run that is open, never across a private note or a link; those still start a run up to half as many again and then the file is refused. 4.8 million alternating runs: 176 ms, 149 MB peak. The `strings.NewReplacer`s built per call (`span`, `escURL`, `privateNote`, the note markers) are package variables. `TestRTFRunLimitBoundsRunsAndNeverMergesAPrivateNote`.
- **One budget for Ghost Markdown** (`ghost.go`): the cost of a post is its bytes plus 64 KB for each place `mdbound` had to break up, the cost of all of them is at most 48 MB, and a time budget of 15 s for the Markdown of the whole export backs it up; a post past either is left empty and counted (`markdown_not_read`, and a warning). Fourteen posts of 2 MiB of unfinished links: 2.6 s (before: 28 s), 13 of them refused. `TestGhostMarkdownCostIsOneBudget`.
- **Report keys are the file's, so they are bounded** (`convert.go`): every name from a file (types, statuses, visibility, tags, categories, custom fields, extensions, labels, keywords, unknown cards) goes through `cleanText` (one clean line of at most 60 characters) and a counter that keeps the first 30 distinct and counts the rest as `(other)`; a warning says "30 or more". 50,000 distinct types, statuses, visibilities: 31 keys. `TestReportKeysAreBounded`.
- **A picture and its stand-in were not one image** (`rtf.go`): `{\*\shppict{\pict...}}{\nonshppict{\pict...}}` was counted as none and marked as none; the `shppict` group is now transparent so its picture is marked and counted, and `nonshppict` is skipped, not counted. `TestPictureWithItsStandInIsOneImage`.
- **Concurrency** (`limiter.go`): a process-wide semaphore around every reading of a source (preview, apply, append, a dry run of an apply, Word, Obsidian, Scrivener, WordPress, Ghost, folders): `SCRIOBH_IMPORT_CONCURRENCY` (default 2, at most 16), three seconds of waiting, then `429 rate_limited` with `Retry-After`; a caller that goes is released at once. The converters look at the caller's context between binder items, posts, list items and every 256 of them and stop with its error. A dry run converts (it goes through `resolve` like an apply) and is covered. `TestImportConversionsAreLimitedForTheWholeProcess`, `TestConvertersStopWhenTheCallerGoes`.
- **The double conversion is reduced** (`limiter.go`): a preview and the apply after it read the source once. What a converter makes is a function of the bytes, the layout, the name and the options and nothing else, so the intake is kept for three minutes (two at most, 64 MB of text at most each) under a SHA-256 of all of those, only for the three converted sources, and is only ever read from (the plan copies what it changes: `finish` no longer appends into the intake's own slice); the apply still checks its token against it, and a source that differs by a byte, another option or another name is read again. `TestApplyReusesTheConversionOfItsPreview`. Not done: the intake of Word, Obsidian and folder sources is not kept (their reading is the cheap part of an apply).
- **Smaller.** A `\u` skip count ends with its group (`{\u8212}abc` kept `a`), and a high surrogate left at a group's end or the end of input is U+FFFD (`TestRTFSkipCountEndsWithItsGroupAndASurrogateIsNeverLost`). A code block or a table written after a list item's text, or in the middle of a table cell, came out before it: the text of an open item is written first, and code in a cell or a note is text of it (`TestHTMLBlocksComeOutInTheirOrder`). Ghost dates are the day in the offset the post carries (`2023-12-31T23:30-05:00` is `2023-12-31`), as WordPress's `post_date` is local; a time with no offset, and the millisecond form, are UTC. Binder items nested over 200 levels are counted and said (`TestBinderItemsPastTheDepthLimitAreCounted`). `shown()` and `jsonErrShort` cut between characters (`TestShownCutsBetweenCharacters`).
- **Markers in code from foreign sources.** `<scriobh-ghost>`, `</scriobh-ghost>` and `<!-- scriobh:` cannot be escaped with a backslash in code, so a zero-width space is written after the bracket (what a person sees and copies is the same; fenced code, indented code, inline code and a code span in a table cell, from HTML, Markdown cards and the DOCX converter). In ordinary text they are escaped as before. `TestCodeFromForeignSourcesCannotCarryMarkers`.
- **Fixtures are in a clone** (`TestFixtureFilesAreTracked`): fails if a file under `testdata` (or the editor's `import-private-*` corpus) is ignored or not added; `Files/Data` is kept by a negation in `.gitignore`.

## Phase 6 integration decisions

MCP server, the agent skill's MCP reference, and webhooks. Nothing here has a screen yet. What was run and what was not is at the end.

### The MCP endpoint

- **Two eras on one endpoint.** The current revision of the protocol (`2026-07-28`, read from the specification at modelcontextprotocol.io on 2026-11-13) has no handshake: every request carries its version and capabilities in `params._meta`, and over HTTP in `MCP-Protocol-Version`, `Mcp-Method` and `Mcp-Name` headers that must agree with the body (`400` with `-32020` when they do not, `400` with `-32602` for a missing `_meta`, `400` with `-32022` and the supported list for an unknown version, `404` with `-32601` for an unknown method; an unknown tool, resource or prompt and a bad cursor are errors of the call and answered `200`). Clients that still open with `initialize` (every client shipped before this revision) are answered by `initialize` with the revision they asked for, or `2025-11-25`. The two are told apart per request by `_meta.io.modelcontextprotocol/protocolVersion`; results carry `resultType: "complete"` only in the modern era.
- **Stateless.** No `Mcp-Session-Id` is minted and one that arrives is ignored; `GET` and `DELETE` are `405`; every `POST` is answered with one `application/json` document (never a stream), so the reverse proxy needs nothing for it. A notification is `202` with no body. Cancelling a call is the client closing its connection; `notifications/cancelled` over HTTP (legacy clients) stops the call in flight for the same key, and the stdio proxy does it by closing the request.
- **Batches** are accepted only in the legacy era, up to `SCRIOBH_MCP_MAX_BATCH` (16), each message after the first paying a rate-limit token; a modern message in a batch is refused. An empty or oversized batch, a non-object element and a malformed message are answered, never crashed on.
- **Auth is the API key** (`Authorization: Bearer`, or `X-API-Key`). A session cookie is never read on `/mcp`, so a signed-in browser cannot be ridden. This is not the OAuth flow the specification describes for HTTP servers; a client that can only do OAuth cannot use this endpoint, and one that takes a header can. The stdio mode takes the key from the environment, as the specification says it should.
- **Origin** is an allow-list, not a comparison with `Host`: under DNS rebinding a browser's `Origin` and `Host` are the attacker's and agree. A request with no `Origin` (every non-browser client) passes the check and still needs a key; `null`, junk, two `Origin` headers and any origin that is not the instance's own (`SCRIOBH_BASE_URL`) or listed in `SCRIOBH_MCP_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` are `403` before the body is read. The standard library's cross-origin guard is bypassed for `/mcp` only, so an origin allowed for MCP is not thereby trusted with the pages (tested).
- **Tools are the registry.** `internal/mcp.BuildCatalog` reads the op schema (the document the CLI snapshot is made from) and decides every op: a tool, or excluded with a reason that is written into the snapshot and the generated reference. Excluded: session-only ops, ops whose body is a file, ops that take a credential (any input named secret, password, token, api key or credential), the `key`, `session`, `notification`, `prefs`, `profile`, `share`, `webhook`, `import`, `git`, `backup`, `mirror` and `ai` groups, `*.purge`, the ops that hold the author's contact block (`compile.contact.get/set`, `compile.preview`, `export.compile`, `export.submission`), and a few editor-internal ops (`doc.lease`, `doc.conflict`, `project.open`, `project.pin`). Group-level exclusion means a new op in those groups is excluded by default; a new group is a tool by default and shows in the snapshot diff.
- **Read-only by default** means by scope: a `read` key sees the read tools; a `write` key adds the writes; a key pinned to a project sees project and Compendium tools; a key pinned to a Compendium sees only Compendium tools. Hiding is not the gate: a hidden tool is "unknown" on call, and every visible call is authorized again by the op. `?readonly=1` removes the writes from a `write` key's view. The administration tier (admin-scope ops and anything with `.member.` in its name) is offered only to an `admin`-scope key on a server with `SCRIOBH_MCP_ADMIN_TOOLS=1`; nothing in the tier is a secret.
- **A tool call is the REST call.** The server builds the op's request (path, query, JSON body from the arguments, `dry_run` as `X-Scriobh-Dry-Run`, `idempotency_key` as `Idempotency-Key`) and runs it through the registered handler in process, with the key's principal. A tool whose op has its own `dry_run` flag (`entity.merge`, `scene.link_entities`, `mention.rebuild`, ...) sets both. Argument mistakes (unknown name, wrong type, missing required, a path value that is not an id) are tool errors the model can read, never sent on; protocol mistakes are JSON-RPC errors.
- **Results are data.** The text is the op's JSON; a second block says it is the writer's data and not instructions; every reading tool's description says so; the server's `instructions` say so. A file result (an export) is an embedded resource, text as text and anything else as base64, never past `SCRIOBH_MCP_MAX_RESULT_KB` (512): a larger result is refused with a message, not cut. No tool takes an address to fetch (a test checks no input is named like one). Resources are Markdown and URIs are checked segment by segment against the id alphabet, so none can reach outside its project or Compendium.
- **Discovery size.** About 200 tools is about 58,000 tokens of descriptions (a `read` key: about 16,000). `?groups=` takes op groups (`doc`, `search`, `compendium`, `entity`, ...) and the presets `core` (project, doc, outline, search, comment, suggestion, revision, review, goal, stats), `bible` (compendium, entity, timeline, mention, scene) and `all`. The docs recommend naming the groups an agent needs.
- **The stdio mode is a proxy** in the slim CLI (`scriobh-cli mcp`, stdlib only; the import-boundary test still passes): each line is POSTed to `/mcp` with the key and the headers the modern era wants, each answer written back as a line. Notifications are not forwarded (they mean nothing to a stateless server and each would spend a rate-limit token); `notifications/cancelled` closes the request's connection. Stdout carries protocol messages only; diagnostics go to stderr; a line over 8 MiB is dropped with an error and the session goes on; the key is never printed.
- **What a screen must do** (the "connect an agent" page, documentation only so far): pick a key (or make a read-scope one pinned to a project), pick tool groups and read-only, and show copyable configuration. Claude Desktop and Cursor take `{"mcpServers": {"scriobh": {"command": "scriobh-cli", "args": ["mcp", "--groups", "core,bible", "--read-only"], "env": {"SCRIOBH_URL": "https://write.example.com", "SCRIOBH_API_KEY": "scr_..."}}}}`; a client that speaks remote Streamable HTTP with headers is given `https://write.example.com/mcp?groups=core&readonly=1` and `Authorization: Bearer scr_...`; OpenCode's `opencode.json` takes the same two shapes under `mcp` (`{"type": "local", "command": ["scriobh-cli", "mcp", "--read-only"], "environment": {...}}` or `{"type": "remote", "url": "...", "headers": {...}}`). These shapes are from memory of those programs' documentation and were not run; the page should link to each program's own page rather than promise them.

### Webhooks

- **Owner only, admin scope.** All nine ops need the project's owner; a key needs the `admin` scope, so an agent that can write a manuscript cannot aim the project's notifications at an address, and a reader cannot see where they go. A reader cannot force a replay: `webhook.redeliver` is the owner's.
- **Events.** `document.updated` (document id, revision, words, whether the text changed), `comment.created` (comment, thread and document ids, kind), `suggestion.created` (suggestion and document ids), `export.completed` (export id, format, documents, bytes). Compendium and entity events are never delivered (the bible's events are anonymised on the stream for people who are not on the Compendium; webhooks do not carry them at all), nor anything from private notes, research, the stash, the Lab or the AI recipes. A payload is `events.Clean` of the stored event (the stream's allow-list: ids, counts and short tokens), with the project id in the envelope and the document id added; a test emits events with titles, names and text in their payloads and checks none arrives, and another drives real ops through the real server and checks the manuscript, titles, comment words and entity names are in no body. `share.viewed` is not an event (a view is counted, not written to the stream).
- **`include_text`** is off by default and its description says in capitals what it does. When on, the comment's words (up to 2,000 characters, `text_truncated` if cut) are read at send time and added to the body; they are never stored in the outbox, so a backup and the delivery list never hold them, and a comment deleted before it is sent is not sent. It is the only text a webhook can carry.
- **The outbox.** `webhook_deliveries` rows are written in the same transaction that moves `webhook_state.cursor` past the event, so an event is one delivery per webhook and a crash loses none (`UNIQUE(webhook_id, event_seq)` besides). The feeder tails `events` and does nothing at all when no webhook is enabled; creating the first webhook moves the cursor to now, so a webhook never hears of the past, and switching one off and on does not replay what it missed. The save path is unchanged: with three webhooks and every receiver stalled, the median save of a 1,200-word chapter cost 535 µs without and 639 µs with (p99 1.58 ms and 1.71 ms), budget +1 ms; no save waited on a delivery. At 150,000 words (150 chapters of 1,000 words and one of 10,000, `TestMCPAndWebhookBudgetsAt150kWords`, run alone): saving the 10,000-word chapter 2.37 ms without webhooks and 2.47 ms with three stalled (budget +1 ms); a tool call (`doc_get` of a chapter) 53 µs over the same REST request (budget +2 ms); `tools/list` for a read key 1.3 ms and for a write key 3.2 ms, all pages (budgets 25 and 60 ms); the outline resource of 151 documents 1.1 ms (budget 60 ms). The timing tests do not run under `-race`, which makes SQLite about 40 times slower. 1,000 events became 2,100 outbox rows (three webhooks, saves of one document coalesced) in 468 ms, budget 2 s.
- **At least once.** There is no exactly-once over HTTP: a process that dies after a receiver answered and before the answer was written sends it again, with the same `Scriobh-Webhook-Id` and `Scriobh-Webhook-Attempt` raised. A receiver stores the ids it has seen. The tests kill the service at both seams (between enqueue and send, and between send and record) and check the row survives, the repeat carries the same id, and a receiver that stores ids applies it once. Order is not promised (a retry can arrive after a later event); the revision in a `document.updated` orders them.
- **A double delivery that was found and fixed.** The dispatcher lists the due rows, then takes a webhook's one send slot. A send that finished between the two left its row, listed a moment before as due, looking due again, and it was sent twice (seen once in a long run of the real-server test: two `document.updated`, the same event). The slot is now taken first, the row is read again (still pending, same attempt count, webhook on, project not in the trash, its time come), and every result is written only if the row is still the pending one it was sent from. `TestAStaleListingDoesNotSendADeliveryTwice` and `TestConcurrentPassesSendEachDeliveryOnce` cover it. A test ping is never the dispatcher's and is never left pending.
- **Per-project rate.** Besides the instance's 300 a minute a project may use 120 (`SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_PROJECT_RATE_PER_MIN`), so one project's flood goes out at its own pace while another's webhook is served in full (`TestOneProjectCannotUseTheWholeRate`). What is held back waits; it is not dropped.
- **Feeder transactions are small** (50 events), because a feeder transaction holds the one writer: a save waits for at most one of them.
- **Retries and the dead-letter list.** A `5xx`, `408`, `429` (honouring `Retry-After` up to an hour) and any network failure are retried by the schedule 10 s, 1 m, 5 m, 30 m, 2 h, 6 h, 12 h, 24 h, each plus or minus 20 %, eight attempts by default (`SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_MAX_ATTEMPTS`), then the delivery is `dead`. `410`, any other `4xx` and a `3xx` are dead at once (a receiver that says no is not asked again). `webhook.deliveries` lists state, attempts, the last status and a failure kind (`blocked`, `timeout`, `dns`, `refused`, `tls`, `network`, `redirect_refused`, `rejected`, `server_error`, `rate_limited`, `gone`) and a 160-character cleaned excerpt of a failing response (no markup, no line breaks); no payload, no body. Ten dead deliveries in a row switch the webhook off with a reason; a success resets the count; switching it on clears both. A project in the trash sends nothing and keeps its queue; restoring it resumes. Delivered rows are kept 14 days, dead ones 28 (`SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_KEEP_DAYS`).
- **Outbound safety.** Everything goes through `internal/netguard` on the address actually dialled. The address is also read when it is typed: https only; no user name, no fragment, no non-ASCII; decimal, octal, hex and short IPv4 spellings refused by shape; internal literals and names (`localhost`, `.local`, `.internal`, single labels) refused unless `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_ALLOW_PRIVATE=1`, which also allows plain http, but only to addresses on this machine or its own network, and never to link-local (the metadata address) or the reserved ranges. Turning the setting off later leaves existing local webhooks unable to connect (found by a test, fixed: plain http was reaching loopback with the setting off). No proxy, no redirect, no connection reuse (each delivery resolves and is checked again, which is what catches DNS rebinding), 10 s per attempt, headers capped at 16 KiB, at most 512 bytes of a failing answer read. An address with a path or query that is itself a secret is the owner's to avoid: the address is shown back to the owner and stored as given, but never goes to the audit log; the signature is the authentication.
- **Signature.** `Scriobh-Webhook-Signature: v1=<hex>` is HMAC-SHA256 under the secret of `<unix seconds>.<body>`, with the timestamp in `Scriobh-Webhook-Timestamp`; a receiver rejects a signature that does not match or a timestamp more than five minutes off. Known-answer vectors from an independent implementation are in `unit_test.go`. During a rotation the header carries two `v1=` values.
- **Secrets.** Sealed with `internal/secrets` bound to `webhooks/<id>/secret` (a ciphertext copied to another row does not open), shown once, never listed. `webhook.create` and `webhook.rotate_secret` are `SecretOutput` and `NoDryRun`, so an idempotent replay never stores the secret. A secret that cannot be opened (a restore without `SCRIOBH_SECRET_KEY`) switches the webhook off with a reason instead of retrying; rotating it fixes it.
- **Dry run.** `create`, `rotate_secret` (a secret) and `test` (a request) are `NoDryRun`; `update`, `delete` and `redeliver` preview faithfully (the dry-run sweep drives them).
- **Mirror, Git, export, backup.** Webhook ops write nothing the Markdown projection shows (`TestEveryWebhookOpIsClassifiedForTheMirror`), so none of it is in the mirror or a Git push (the mirror tree is searched for the address, ids and secret); no export holds any of it (single file and bundle searched); a backup holds the tables, so the configuration and the outbox are in it, with the secret sealed and no plaintext secret anywhere in the archive (the key file is never in a backup, so a restore on a machine with another key finds the secrets unreadable and the webhooks switched off until rotated). The delivery log is part of the backup because it is the same database; it holds no payload text.
- **`export.completed`** is a new event written by `export.Record`, so every Markdown, HTML, DOCX, EPUB and submission export (and any format added later through `Record`) produces it. Its payload keys (`export_id`, `format`) were added to the stream's allow-list. The mirror's poll skips it (an efficiency choice: the mirror finds nothing to write either way, which a test shows), and the live stream shows it to editors and owners only, since only they can run an export.
- **What a screen must do** (the project's Webhooks page): list `webhook.list` with state and counts; a create form with name, address, event checkboxes and the `include_text` checkbox with its warning; show the secret once with a copy button and the verification recipe; per webhook: Test (shows status and failure kind), Rotate (with "keep the old secret for N hours"), Enable/Disable (with the reason when the server switched it off), Delete (typed confirmation); a deliveries view over `webhook.deliveries` with a Replay button on dead ones. The instance setting `allow_private` in `webhook.list` says whether to offer plain http and local addresses.

### Smaller decisions

- `webhook` ops are in the REST API and the CLI snapshot and not in MCP: configuring where data goes is not an agent task.
- The op schema generator reports an embedded struct (`docs.Doc` embeds `Meta`) as a field named `Meta`, where the JSON it describes is flat. It does not affect inputs here, and the MCP tools do not publish output schemas; it is worth fixing in `internal/ops/schema.go` when someone is there.
- Config: `SCRIOBH_MCP`, `SCRIOBH_MCP_ADMIN_TOOLS`, `SCRIOBH_MCP_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, `SCRIOBH_MCP_MAX_RESULT_KB`, `SCRIOBH_MCP_MAX_BATCH`, `SCRIOBH_MCP_CALL_TIMEOUT_S`, `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOKS`, `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_ALLOW_PRIVATE`, `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_MAX_PER_PROJECT`, `_MAX_TOTAL`, `_RATE_PER_MIN`, `_PROJECT_RATE_PER_MIN`, `_QUEUE_MAX`, `_QUEUE_PER_WEBHOOK`, `_CONCURRENCY`, `_TIMEOUT_S`, `_MAX_ATTEMPTS`, `_KEEP_DAYS`, `_DEBOUNCE_MS`; see OPERATIONS.
- Migration `1700_webhooks` opens a new range ("integrations") after `1600` (agent keys, idempotency): `webhooks`, `webhook_deliveries`, `webhook_state`.

### Not verified

- **No real MCP client was run.** Claude Desktop, Cursor and OpenCode were not available. Conformance is against this program's own JSON-RPC client, the specification text as fetched, and the hostile-input suite. In particular unverified: how those clients handle a tool list of this size and `nextCursor` pagination, whether they send `_meta` and the three headers as specified, and the configuration snippets above.
- The modern era's `resources/*` and `prompts/*` result shapes were taken to be the same as the legacy ones plus `resultType`; only `tools/*`, `server/discover` and the transport rules were read in detail.
- Tool-list caching fields (`ttlMs`, `cacheScope`) are not sent; `subscriptions/listen` is not implemented (so `listChanged` is false and a client that wants list-changed notifications gets none).
- Webhook delivery was tested against local TLS servers and fake resolvers, not against a real receiver on the internet (Slack, a Zapier hook, a Cloudflare worker); real-world TLS chains, HTTP/2 receivers and slow-loris receivers were not tried. The sender speaks HTTP/1.1 only, with a new connection each time.
- The soak case (a receiver down for the whole of the default schedule, about 20 hours) is the schedule's arithmetic and an injected clock, not two days.

### Phase 6 fix round (after an independent review)

Each item was reproduced by the reviewer's probes, which were adapted into regression tests.

1. **Queue squatting (fixed).** Rows of a webhook that was switched off, or of a project in the trash, used to stay pending for ever and count toward the instance's queue, so one person could keep everyone else's events out. Now only deliveries that could be sent (webhook on, project live) count toward `QUEUE_MAX`; there is a per-project cap (`SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_QUEUE_PER_PROJECT`, 1,000); switching a webhook off (by hand or by the ten-dead-in-a-row rule) deletes what was waiting for it; a trashed project's queue is kept for a restore but not counted against others; the sweep drops a pending delivery that has waited the keeping time (14 days). Tests: `TestSwitchingOffAWebhookFreesItsQueue`, `TestATrashedProjectsQueueTakesNoRoomFromOthers`, `TestOneProjectCannotFillTheQueue`, `TestSweepKeepsRecentAndDropsOld`.
2. **Head-of-line blocking (fixed).** The dispatcher listed the 100 oldest due rows, so 150 rows of one webhook hid another project's. It now lists at most one due delivery per webhook (a subquery on the new `(webhook_id, state, next_attempt_at)` index) and skips webhooks with a send in flight, and one project may hold at most half the send slots. Tests: `TestAWebhookWithABacklogDoesNotBlockTheOthers`, `TestOneProjectCannotHoldEverySendSlot`.
3. **Coalescing (fixed).** A save that arrived while the previous notification was being sent was folded into the row in flight and lost, and folding replaced the payload (a scene change after a text save left "no text changed, no words"). Sending now claims the row first (`claimed_at`, a write only one sender wins); the feeder folds only into an unclaimed row, in the same statement; folding merges (highest revision, `content` true if any save changed the text, the words of the last text save). A claim older than twice the send timeout plus a minute is dead, and starting the service gives back every claim. Tests: `TestASaveDuringASendIsNotLost`, `TestFoldingKeepsWhatTheEarlierSaveSaid`, `TestAStartGivesBackWhatTheLastProcessClaimed`, `TestCrashBetweenSendAndRecord`.
4. **MCP false success (fixed).** A path argument of `.` or `..` passed the id check; the router cleaned it away and answered a redirect, which the tool reported as success. Path values are now ids only (never empty, never dots alone, no separator, escape, query or fragment) and any result that is not a 2xx is an error result. Tests: `TestPathArgumentsThatAreNotIds`, `TestOnlyA2xxIsSuccess`, `TestMCPDotIDsAreRefusedThroughTheRealServer`.
5. **"Nothing is deleted for good" was false (fixed).** `Op.Permanent` marks the 27 ops that destroy data beyond the trash (stash and revision deletes, pruning, merges of entities and tags, comment, tag and attachment deletes, field, alias, relationship and override removal, goals, presets, purges, webhook delete, and the AI, Git and import ones that are excluded anyway). A registry test fails when an op that deletes, removes, merges, clears, prunes or dismisses has neither `Permanent` nor an entry in a reversible table with its reason. Over MCP they are hidden unless `SCRIOBH_MCP_PERMANENT_TOOLS=1` **and** the connection asks (`?permanent=1`, `scriobh-cli mcp --permanent`) **and** the key can write; their `destructiveHint` is true and their description says PERMANENT. The server's instructions, OPERATIONS, the agent guide and the generated reference now say what is true: deletes go to the trash except these. Purging a whole project or Compendium is never offered. Tests: `TestEveryDestructiveOpIsDecided`, `TestKnownPermanentOps`, `TestPermanentToolsAreOptIn`, `TestTheCatalogKeepsItsPromises`, `TestMCPPermanentToolsThroughTheRealServer`.
6. **`SCRIOBH_MCP_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` could not work (fixed, implemented).** `/mcp` now answers a CORS preflight for the instance's own origin and the listed ones: 204, the matched origin echoed (never `*`), `Authorization, Content-Type, MCP-Protocol-Version, Mcp-Method, Mcp-Name, Mcp-Session-Id, Last-Event-ID` allowed, `POST, OPTIONS`, `Max-Age` 300, `Vary: Origin`, no credentials flag; every answer to an allowed origin, a refused key's 401 included, names it; any other origin gets no CORS headers and a 403 preflight; no other route has CORS. Test: `TestMCPCORS`.
7. **Contact block (fixed).** `compile.contact.get/set` are excluded with the reason in the snapshot. Checking what else carries it: `compile.preview` (its answer holds the block), `export.compile` and `export.submission` (the title page of the DOCX carries it) are excluded too, with the reason; the Markdown and HTML exports do not carry it. Test: `TestTheCatalogKeepsItsPromises`.

Plausible items, all done: one deadline for a whole legacy batch (the call timeout; messages not reached are answered with an error carrying their own id, and a notification is not answered; rate-limit refusals carry ids too: `TestBatchHasOneDeadlineAndRefusalsCarryIDs`); the feeder's cursor only moves forward (`SET cursor = MAX(cursor, ?)`); the path and query of a webhook address are sealed (`url_path_sealed`; the listing shows scheme, host and port and `url_path_set`; `update` replaces the whole address; an address that cannot be opened switches the webhook off with a reason: `TestTheAddressPathIsSealed`); no excerpt is kept when local targets are allowed (`TestNoExcerptWhenLocalTargetsAreAllowed`); an oversize resource says "larger than this server returns", not "not found" (`TestAnOversizeResourceSaysTooLarge`); the cancel map is keyed by key, client address and request id (`TestCancelIsMatchedToTheCallersAddress`); an event that was already a delivery is not counted as queued (`TestAReplayedEventIsNotADuplicate`); the ping record is pruned (`TestThePingRecordIsBounded`); the `(webhook_id, state, next_attempt_at)` and `(project_id, state)` indexes and the new columns are in `1700_webhooks.sql` itself, edited in place because that migration has been applied nowhere but this branch.

## Phase 6 publish and connect screens

The static site page, the Webhooks page and the connect-an-agent page (FEATURES section AI). No op was added and none changed, so the skill, CLI and MCP snapshots are untouched.

### Decisions

- **Server-rendered forms**, posting to `/ui/projects/{id}/site/{action}` and `/ui/projects/{id}/webhooks/{action}` and redirecting back (Post/Redirect/Get), as the share links page does. Every write is a registry call; the handlers write no SQL. The pages work without script; `connect.js` only replaces the request's origin with the page's own, shows copy buttons and focuses a secret.
- **Where things live.** The site page is a project page for owner and editors (like Compile: anyone else gets 404). The webhook ops are project-level and the owner's, so the Webhooks page is a project page for the owner (another member gets 403 with the reason, like Share links) and there is **no admin console area** for webhooks: the ops have no instance-wide listing. The connect page is `/settings/mcp`, a page of its own linked from the API keys section, because the settings page itself is not in the message catalogue and these strings are.
- **The secret once.** Create and rotate redirect to the page with the secret in the session's one-shot flash (in memory, two minutes, taken by the next page), the same way a share link is shown. The redirect carries no fragment: Chromium takes the focus off the secret when it scrolls to one.
- **Delete is confirmed with `arm.js`** (focus on Cancel, the confirming button off for half a second, Escape cancels), as the brief asked, rather than the typed confirmation the integration notes suggested; the same panel guards Rotate, Publish and Reset every option.
- **Overwrite edited files** is offered only after a publish was refused for them. The page recognises that refusal by the op's message naming `overwrite_edited`; a change of that wording would hide the checkbox (the publish would still be refused, in words).
- **The options form sends only what changed**: the handler reads the stored options and builds a patch of the fields that differ, so a save never writes a default into a field the person did not touch. Number fields that are not whole numbers are refused before the op.
- **Posts are capped at 40 edit forms** with "show all", so a long blog's page stays light; pages and the left-out list are not capped.
- **Server messages are shown as they are**: a refused address, a refused publish, an exclusion reason and a warning's message come from the ops in English and are shown untouched beside the catalogue's words; the pseudo-locale sweep lets them through by class (`cx-op`), with what the person wrote (`cx-their`) and identifiers in `<code>`.
- **The MCP address** is built from the request (`X-Forwarded-Proto` only with `SCRIOBH_TRUST_PROXY`) and then replaced in the browser by `location.origin`. Read only is on by default, and the default set is the manuscript (`core`), as the integration notes recommend. The OpenCode snippet is the remote shape; the client snippets follow the shapes in the integration notes and were not run against those programs.
- **The connect page lists the person's live keys** with scope and restriction; the settings page still offers only read and write keys.
- **A post on a left-out document** is offered only for a part: the op's selection keeps a document out for its status, a do-not-compile mark or an unpublished parent even with role post, so for those the page links to the outline (status) instead. A left-out document that keeps a pinned address says so and offers to free it (`clear: ["pinned_slug"]`), so the 409 "keeps it" refusal has a visible cause.
- **The accent swatches use literal colours**: `TestStylesheetIsTokensFirst` now allows them for `.a-<accent>` swatches only (they are the generated site's palette, not the app's), and `TestTheSiteSwatchesAreTheGeneratorsAccents` fails if they drift from `internal/site/css.go`.
- **The service worker never stores a page**: navigations go to the network and fall back to the offline page, so a page that showed a webhook secret is not kept in Cache Storage (only `/static/` files are).

### Not verified

- No real MCP client was pointed at the page's configuration (Claude Desktop, Cursor, OpenCode were not available); `tools/list` was answered at the shown address with a read key, from Playwright.
- Webhook delivery from the page was tested against a plain HTTP receiver on 127.0.0.1 with `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_ALLOW_PRIVATE=1`, not against a public HTTPS receiver.
- "After a reload with the network cut": these pages are server-rendered and the service worker answers a navigation it cannot fetch with the offline page (read in `web/static/sw/worker.js`); the e2e kit blocks service workers, so that path was not run for these pages.
- A screen reader was not run; the pages have landmarks, labelled fields, live regions for the copy and the flash lines, and `role="alert"` on refusals.
- `scripts/desktop-smoke.sh` passed on this branch, and `scripts/csp-smoke.mjs` now visits the three pages (no violation, no inline style or script in the live DOM).

## Phase 6 soak

Measured with `scriobh bench --soak` (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`, "Soak"), on darwin/arm64 with 16 cores, 2026-11-14.

1. **RSS grew with the database; the cause was the writer's page cache (fixed: 8 MB on every connection).** The 65-minute soak at 150k words failed the rule (RSS 279.9 to 358.0 MB, allowed 349.9), and a later one climbed from 250 to 416 MB with the runtime's own memory flat at 230 to 237 MB: about 180 MB outside it, 2.5 to 3 MB per MB the file grew. The earlier reading here ("bounded by the five connections' caches, 5 x 64 MB, so cut the readers to 8 MB") was wrong and the reader cut changed nothing: the four readers' caches hold about 0.4 MB between them (the memory map serves their reads), and the one that fills is the writer's, whose pages cost about four times their bytes (3 to 5 in the runs) in the driver's allocator. The evidence is in `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`, "Soak": with the writer cache at 2 MB RSS grew 11 MB per 10 minutes against 63 to 72 with 64 MB, at 8 MB 12 to 43, and the footprint (dirty pages, not clean file pages) moved with RSS in every run, so the rule's measure was right and is kept; the soak reports the footprint beside it. Switching the memory map off made it worse, `temp_store FILE` and one reader changed nothing, and recycling the writer connection or `shrink_memory` every 30 to 60 s halved the slope without stopping it. Decided: one `cache_size(-8192)` for every connection (`TestPageCacheStaysWithinItsLimit`), no measured cost. The growth rule was not loosened. The bench's times were the same with the writer cache at 64, 8, 4 and 2 MB (three runs each); 8 MB is kept over 2 as margin for bulk imports and restores, which the bench does not time. With the change the 65-minute soak at 150k words passes with the cache filling and then stopping (RSS 289.7 to 302.5 MB between the first and last third, allowed 362.1; per 10 minutes +10.8, +2.2, -1.7 MB over its three thirds while the file grew 3.8 MB per 10 minutes throughout; footprint 243 to 249 MB), and a 20-minute soak at 500k words held (RSS 362.3 to 370.4 MB, allowed 452.9).

   What is not known: (a) whether Linux behaves the same. There the driver's C library uses musl's `malloc` (`modernc.org/libc` selects it by platform), not the allocator measured here, so the cost per cached page and the retention may differ; the soak has not been run on Linux and its footprint sample there (`RssAnon`) is untested. The 8 MB cap bounds the cache on both. (b) The 65-minute run is the longest made: the anonymous memory outside the runtime (footprint less go_mapped) levelled at 37 to 39 MB from 13 minutes on, while the file kept growing, which is the plateau; but the 500k-word soak lasted only 20 minutes, and RSS less the footprint (clean pages) wandered between 36 and 60 MB in the 150k run without a trend that was separated from the mapped file's. (c) The retention behaviour of the allocator was read from its documentation, not tested.

2. **The event log was never pruned (fixed).** One row per save and nothing removed them, against the documented 90-day log. `events.Sweep` now runs with the hourly sweeps, in chunks of 2,000, keeping the newest row and, while a webhook is on, every row its feeder has not read.
3. **Export records are never removed (open).** Each compile or export records a manifest row (about 35 KB for a 150k-word book; 31 rows, about 1 MB, in 22 minutes of the soak's pace). Nothing decides how long they are kept.


## Phase 6 import screens, pictures and sheets

The import page offers every format, pictures are imported, and Scrivener's character and place sheets can become entities. Rows: [FEATURES § AL](FEATURES.md#al-implementation-status-phase-6-import-screens-pictures-and-sheets). This revisits two decisions of "Phase 6 import decisions" above ("Attachments were not built", "Entities were not built"); those paragraphs stay as the record of that round.

### Decisions

- **One page, a chooser.** The import page keeps one URL and one apply path; a fieldset of formats shows one panel at a time, with where the export comes from in plain words. The Markdown panel still sends `layout: auto`, so a Scrivener zip or an export chosen there is recognised by its markers. The new formats' code is a second file (`import-formats.js`) so `import.js` stays inside its budget; it uses two hooks (`label`, `show`) and a source's own options function.
- **A `.scriv` folder is accepted** as well as a zip: the browser reads only the binder, `.rtf`, `.txt` and picture files, at most 34 MiB, and lists the rest by name for the report. A zip is recommended in the words, because it is one file and has no browser differences.
- **Pictures: one path for all converters.** A converter writes `![description](scriobh-picture:N)` through the sink (foreign text cannot write one: its brackets are escaped, and a forged one could only ever name a picture of the same import); the picture's cleaned hash and number are folded into the token; the apply gives every picture its attachment id before the bodies are hashed, writes the address in, stores the rows first in the import's transaction and records the references (`DeferLinks` skips the hook that would). The attachments package owns the SQL and the files (`PreparePicture`, `NewImport`, `Commit`, `Done`); the import takes the files lock before its transaction, the order an upload takes them in.
- **Cleaning:** sniff by bytes (PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP), `imagesafe.Clean`, then the upload's own `detect` and pixel limit on the cleaned bytes. 25 MB a picture as sent, 2,000 pictures, 192 MB read and 128 MB kept per import, a picture budget of its own in the zip reader so photographs cannot crowd out the text; a picture over the server's configured file limit is marked `[image]` at apply and said so.
- **Cost of cleaning.** A JPEG that says it is rotated is decoded, turned and written again; sixty blank 16-megapixel rotated JPEGs zip to 49 KB and take 17.5 s to clean (`TestPictureCleaningIsBounded`). The pictures of one conversion share a 20-second budget (past it a picture is refused and counted), the conversion stops when the caller goes, it holds one of the two conversion slots, and the apply after a preview reuses the conversion. A tighter budget would refuse an honest folder of phone photographs; a pixel budget for rotation is the next step if this matters.
- Only pictures that a kept document or note shows are stored and counted: one left out with its text is not.
- **Quota:** an import whose new pictures would pass a quota is refused whole (all or none, as everything else in an import), and the message says to import again with pictures left out (`pictures: skip`). Pictures the project already holds, byte for byte, are reused, not stored again.
- **No fetching, ever.** Ghost and WordPress name pictures by address; only the path after `content/images/` or `wp-content/uploads/` is compared with what is in the zip (Ghost's `size/w600/` and WordPress's `-300x200` copies map to the original). The writer is told in the panel to put the images folder in the zip.
- **Sheets become entities only when asked** (`scrivener_sheets: entities`; default `notes`). A new project gets a primary Compendium made for it and owned by the importer: nothing is written into a bible other books share without a choice. Adding to a project uses its primary Compendium only when the person may edit it; a project-pinned key reaches only its own project's. A clash, a refused sheet or a sheet with a picture stays a research note; it never rolls the import back.
- **Kept as reported, not built:** pictures of Word, Obsidian and Markdown imports (each a separate reader of relative links), RTF `\pict` data, Research PDFs and web pages, TIFF and HEIC (no decoder: refused by type and counted).

### Not verified

- A real Scrivener 3 project with pictures: the `{\NeXTGraphic name \width...}` form with the file in the item's `Files/Data/<UUID>/` folder is recalled, as the other Scrivener markers are; the fixture is hand-written. A picture Scrivener stores another way stays `[image]` and counted.
- A real Ghost "download your images" zip or a WordPress `uploads` backup: the folder names follow the documented layout; any other layout imports no pictures and reports `pictures_not_sent`.

## Phase 6 compile screens

The print presets, the print options, the proof, the printer's checklist and the paperback cover on the Compile page (FEATURES, sections AI and AJ). No op was added and none changed shape.

### Decisions

- **The proof is drawn, not the PDF shown.** `compile.preview` returns the first pages as placed lines (position, size, text, kind), the same layout the export runs; the page draws each as an SVG of the trim's proportions with SVG attributes only (no style attribute, no markup from the text, every word set as text). The alternatives were an embedded viewer of the exported file (a `blob:` frame needs the page's CSP widened with `frame-src blob:`, records an export for every look, and Chromium headless, the phone browsers and an embedded desktop web view do not reliably show a PDF in a frame) or page images rendered by the server (a rasteriser is a new dependency). The drawing has the file's breaks and places and the browser's letters, and says so on the page; the file itself is one press of Download away, which also records it. If a viewer is wanted later, it is the attachment manager's narrow allowance (`cspFrames`) for this page only, showing the bytes the export returned.
- **The preview's centred lines were misplaced (fixed in the backend).** `PDFLayout.Page` gave a centred or right-aligned line the left edge of its measure; it now gives the start the renderer draws (`drawLine`), so a chapter heading, the contents title and the copyright page sit where they print. A test covers it. The op's shape is unchanged.
- **Justified lines in the drawing.** The preview does not say which lines are justified. A line is filled to the measure when the preset justifies, it starts at the text block's edge or the first-line indent, the next line is text one leading below at the edge (so the line is not a paragraph's last), neither is a contents entry, and the browser already sets it at 85 per cent of the measure or more; quotations, lists and code are inset or short and stay as set (checked on a contents page and a page with all three). Lines the browser sets wider than the measure are squeezed (`textLength`, spacing and glyphs). This is only how the drawing looks; the file is unaffected.
- **Which options are shown for which format.** Only the fourteen options that only print reads (`margin_top`, `margin_bottom`, `margin_inside`, `margin_outside`, `gutter_inches`, `footnotes`, `running_heads`, `drop_cap`, `chapter_ornament`, `toc`, `hyphenate`, `copyright_page`, `imprint`, and `crop_marks`, which is for `pdf` alone) are hidden for a Word file, an EPUB or Markdown. A Word-only option stays visible on a print preset: a writer that ignores an option is harmless, an option hidden by mistake is not. The table is in the page glue (`internal/server/compileui.go`), next to the groups.
- **`hyphenate` is shown, switched off,** with the reason, because the op refuses it; it is not left out, so the person knows it exists and why it cannot be had.
- **Switches that are on when unset.** The writers read some switches with `publish.On` (contents, running heads, copyright page, front and back matter, title page, part pages, the EPUB cover, the contact block). A Word preset leaves the print ones unset, so when its kind of file is changed to a PDF by hand the form shows them on, as the file will have them (`on` in the option descriptor).
- **The checklist ticks only what the preview knows.** The layout raises `gutter_small`, `image_low_resolution`, `missing_glyph` and `script_unsupported`, so those are ticked or flagged from the preview; `image_not_embedded` is raised only by the writer (the preview does not read picture bytes), so that line has no tick and says it is known when the file is made.
- **The paper's thickness has no default and no list of stocks.** The Phase 6 print decisions made it a required input for the cover; the page keeps that: an empty field is refused before anything is asked, and the hint says the figure is in the printer's cover guide and differs by paper and printer.
- **The cover drawing is a sketch.** It is drawn in the page from `compile.cover_spec`'s panels and the project's title, subtitle, author, kind and colour (handed to the page as the cover writer reads them, `cover.Spec.Clean`); the title is wrapped by an estimate. The dashed trim and folds are always drawn in the sketch; the file has guides only when asked.
- **No layout shift on a result.** At 1000 px and wider the preview column is in place from the start (it says to press Preview); the result fills it. Every proof page of a preview is the same size. The manifest box after a download and the cover's measurements grow below the button that was pressed, never above it.
- **The submission bundle** is offered only for a Word preset (the op refuses the others); the button is disabled with the note hidden otherwise.
- **The cover's download** is a fourth action of the page's existing UI route (`/ui/projects/{id}/compile/cover`), which reads the numbers from the form and passes the op's own refusals through (422). The manifest summary shown after it leaves out the comparison with the book's preview: a cover's input hash is its own.
- **Strings.** All in `internal/i18n/locales/en.json` under `cmp.` (template) and `cmp.js.` (scripts, including `compile-print.js`, which the catalogue test now reads). The test that logged an option without a group now fails on it, and a new test fails when an option, a group, an allowed value or a built-in preset has no words.

### What was checked, and what was not

- Checked in Chromium (`scripts/e2e-compile-print.mjs`): the presets and options with their words; a print PDF made through the page whose bytes start `%PDF-` and whose page objects number the preview's page count; the cover measured (the spine is pages times thickness) and downloaded as a two-page PDF; the whole path by keyboard alone (Tab, type-ahead and arrows on the preset, Enter on Preview, the arrows, Home and End in the proof, Enter on Download, Enter in the thickness field); the live region on success and with the network cut; no shift of the Preview button when the result arrives and the same page size across page turns; reduced motion; both themes; 390 px with no sideways scroll and every control 44 px or taller. `scripts/e2e-compile.mjs` still passes (its page-size count now expects the seven sizes the schema has had since the print work).
- Not checked: a screen reader in person (the live region, the labels and the SVG's text order were checked from the DOM, not heard); the desktop wrapper's web view; how the drawing looks for a right-to-left book (the print export refuses right-to-left languages, so there is no proof to draw); a printed copy of the PDF against the drawing.

## Phase 6 fixes after the documentation pass

Six defects found while documenting v1.0. Each was reproduced first and has a test that fails on the earlier code.

- **A project in the trash is read-only, in one place (for its owner too).** The owner could still save, create, import and push into their own trashed project over REST, the CLI and MCP, because the role function does not see which op is asking (a member who is not the owner already got the 404 a missing project gives). The rule is now the Compendium's: the registry gives every write op that names a project, or a document, comment, tag or anything else that belongs to one, a `409` with `data.reason` `project_trashed` ("This project is in the trash. Restore it to change anything in it."), after the role check, so a stranger still gets the 404. A write is any op that is not a GET and not scope `read` (the previews). What still works is `projectTrashedOK` in `internal/ops/registry.go`, the only list: trash, restore, purge, archive, unarchive, membership, `project.open` and `project.pin` (the person's own bookkeeping), `mirror.rebuild`, the five exports and `site.build` (an archive download); reads are unaffected. A new op is covered by default. Dry runs are refused the same way. `compendium.create` with a `project_id` (the registry does not see the id in its body) checks in its own transaction. The editor's autosave turns the reason into "This project is in the trash. Your text is kept here." with a link to the library. Background and in-flight writers: a queued Git push was **not** paused, because the engine looked at the reserved `deleted_at`, which nothing sets (its test did too); it now looks at `trashed_at` and drops an automatic push or fails a manual one with the trash message (`TestTrashedProjectPushesNothing` queues one before the trash). An import that was already preparing when the project went to the trash is refused by the transaction that would write (`TestAppendIntoATrashedProjectIsRefused`). Webhooks (rows wait, take no queue room, resume on restore), `site.publish` and the AI run start already refused and still do. Left as they were, on purpose: the mirror keeps writing a trashed project's folder (it is a projection and says the project is trashed); the mention and link index workers refresh derived rows only; an AI run already streaming finishes and settles its budget, and what it produced can be applied only after a restore (every accept op is refused until then); an entity merge in a Compendium still rewrites a trashed project's override and mention rows. The sweeps: `TestTrashedProjectIsReadOnlyForItsOwnerForEveryWritingOp` calls every project op as the owner and as an admin on a trashed project and names the exceptions by hand (so widening the list means changing the test), and `TestTrashedProjectIsInvisibleToOtherMembersForEveryOp` still proves the 404s. The carry-forward "the owner can still call content ops on their own trashed project" is closed (see "A trashed project is its owner's alone" above).
- **An issuer mismatch, or a refused client secret, shows an operator page.** `signInFailed` had a case for an unreachable provider and one for a refused sign-in, and nothing for `ErrIssuerMismatch`, so it fell through to the generic 500. It now renders "Sign-in is not set up correctly", which names `SCRIOBH_OIDC_ISSUER` and the issuer as configured, and nothing the provider reported (that string came from the network). The log line carries `configured_issuer` and `reported_issuer`. A token endpoint that answers `invalid_client` (or 401) was the 403 "rejected the sign-in code"; it is the same operator page naming `SCRIOBH_OIDC_CLIENT_ID` and `_SECRET`, and the log has the client id and the provider's error code, never the secret or the provider's words. An unreachable provider already had its own page (502, naming the issuer) and is now pinned by a test. Tests: `TestSignIn*` in `internal/server/signin_errors_test.go` against a fake provider.
- **`config check` says what is true about identity.** With no issuer and no stub the line was `identity` and nothing; it is now `not configured (SCRIOBH_OIDC_ISSUER is empty and SCRIOBH_OIDC_STUB is off)`, and the check still fails with the reason.
- **Mistyped settings are no longer silent.** Every yes/no setting goes through one parser (`config.ParseBool`: `1 true yes on 0 false no off`, any case, empty is the default); anything else fails the load and `config check`, naming the variable, the value and the accepted spellings, all such problems at once. That includes `SCRIOBH_TRUST_PROXY`, which a typo left off. `SCRIOBH_IMPORT_CONCURRENCY`, which the importer read and ignored if it could not, is checked in `Load` too. There are no duration settings (seconds and milliseconds are integers, which already named the variable). A `SCRIOBH_` name that this version does not read is warned about once each in the log at start and in `config check` (exit status unchanged), with the nearest setting as a hint (`did you mean SCRIOBH_TRUST_PROXY?`), or the reason for a removed or planned one (`RetiredEnv`), and never the value. What counts as known is what `Load` actually read (recorded as it reads, not a second list) plus `ToolingEnv`: the CLI's variables, the build and compose label, the development clock and the switches of tests and scripts. `TestEverySCRIOBHNameInTheRepositoryIsAccountedFor` scans Go, YAML, scripts and the docs and fails for a name that is none of those, so a compose file or a test cannot pass a variable that is then flagged, and a new setting cannot be documented without being read. The warning is raised only by `serve` and `config check`, not by `healthcheck`, which runs on every probe.
- **The webhook schedule comment said "about two days".** Eight attempts are seven waits (10 s, 1 m, 5 m, 30 m, 2 h, 6 h, 12 h), 20 h 36 min before jitter; the 24-hour wait is reached only if `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_MAX_ATTEMPTS` is raised. The docs (API.md, OPERATIONS.md) already said so; the comment and the soak note above now agree, and `TestDefaultScheduleIsAboutTwentyHours` pins the sum.
- **Two features no longer share an F-id.** Rows of the Phase 6 status sections had been numbered over each other: F-760 and F-761 (site, section AH, and webhooks and MCP, section AG), F-770 to F-773 (site and imports, AK) and F-780 to F-787 (the site and webhook screens, AI, and the import screens, AL). The later row of each pair is now F-798 and F-799, F-800 to F-803 and F-804 to F-811; the two references that meant the renumbered rows (in the intros of AG and of the screens section) were updated, and each section says what its ids were. F-340 was named as a duplicate but is not one: it is the catalogue row of Q and the status row of X for the same feature, which is how every catalogue id is carried forward. The rule, written at the top of FEATURES and checked by `TestFeatureIDsAreDefinedOnce` (`internal/repocheck`): an id is defined once, by its catalogue row or, if it has none, by its one status row.

## Phase 6 accessibility: findings and what is not verified

The audit script, the fixes it led to and [ACCESSIBILITY.md](ACCESSIBILITY.md) (FEATURES, section AO). No op was added and none changed shape. The standard is WCAG 2.2 AA as a target, never as a claim.

### What the first full run found

70 pages (the script's first complete walk, before any fix), counted as one entry per element and message, not per page:

- **Real findings, 433 entries, about a dozen causes.** Field edges at 1.29:1 against the page where WCAG 1.4.11 wants 3:1 (339 fields; one token, `--field-line`, now the muted colour). Placeholders at 3.75:1 (29). The word-count and number in the manuscript tree's current row at 4.40:1 on the accent wash in Paper and 4.12:1 in Ink (6). States shown only by colour or a shadow, which forced colours removes: the current tab, the chosen tree row, a pressed toggle, in 35 places (the system keeps no shadows or our colours); the editing surface and the title field had no focus outline in forced colours (2). Eight primary controls under 44 px on a phone (six number, date and password fields at 28 px, the formatting bar's overflow button at 43). Four search forms that were landmarks with no name. The shared dialog's `aria-labelledby` pointed at a heading that does not exist until the dialog is filled (on every page). The Compendiums header ran 44 px past a 320 px window. Two controls hidden under the sticky Save bar when tabbed to. Six breadcrumb clippings under the text-spacing override (allowed, below).
