- **Found when the contents of dialogs and panels were scanned and walked.** The command palette's and the chapter jump's search fields had `outline: none` and nothing in its place, so a keyboard user could not see where focus was (and in forced colours either); the account menu put focus on its last item, Sign out, so it read bottom to top; every heading in the inspector's panels was an `h3` straight under the page's `h1`; muted text (counts, numbers, dates) on the accent wash of a current row was 4.40:1 in Paper, 4.12:1 in Ink, 4.27:1 in Moss and 4.39:1 in Sepia, in the versions list and the jump list as well as the tree (the contrast script had no rule for that pair; it has now, and the four `--muted` tokens moved by a few units); "Change or revoke" and its twin on the sharing and webhook pages were 22 px tall on a phone; the writing partner's sticky footer covered the buttons above it when tabbed to; the inspector's foot sat under the status bar.
- **Found by the later passes.** At 200% text (every element's font size doubled in place, which the first emulation did not do because the stylesheets set type in px): the header's page tabs pushed the account button off a 1280 px window (3 pages), the writing view's status bar clipped (1), four kinds of label cut by an ellipsis where they could wrap (the cover's kind, a session's device, a timeline's dates: 12), and the breadcrumb ellipsised on 32 pages (allowed). 18 buttons shared two names with nothing to tell them apart (14 "Save" in the Compendium's field list, 4 "Copy" in Connect). A review's comment buttons said only the verb.
- **Artefacts of the script, fixed in the script, not counted above:** 37 entries (a Tab walk that began at an autofocused field and so never reached the controls before it, a modal dialog reported as leaking focus when it handed focus to the browser's own controls, a date field's parts read as a trap, two pages with one title, the author's own `h1` inside the text counted as the page's, a live region that is `#live` rather than the save-state element). They are listed so that nobody mistakes the first run's total for a count of defects.

After the fixes the last full run (`make a11y`: 87 pages, 8 minutes 29 seconds, exit 0) reports no violation outside the five allow-list entries, with 15 occurrences of those entries excused.

### Decisions

- **The edge of a field is the muted text colour.** `--field-line: var(--muted)`: it already passes 4.5:1 on every surface in every theme under `check-contrast.py`, so no theme needed a new token and the contrast script already guards it. The hairline `--line` stays for separating things. The cost is visual: fields have a firmer outline than before. Hover is the full text colour.
- **Forced colours are met with shape, not with a second theme.** An underline for the current page, an outline for a chosen, pressed or selected item, an outline for focus. No new theme; F-291's separate high-contrast mode stays unbuilt.
- **The page tabs scroll in their own bar** when the header is crowded, and the breadcrumb gives way first. Before, the header overflowed the page. The alternative, moving the tabs into the More menu at large text, cannot be told from a narrow window by the page.
- **The 200% text emulation doubles every element's computed size in place.** A browser's larger-default-text setting does nothing to this application (type is set in px); browser zoom does what WCAG 1.4.4 needs. Converting the type scale and the fixed bar heights to rem is a larger change than an audit should make and is left as a decision: it would let a person's default text size reach the application, and the header, rail and status bar would have to grow with it.
- **Page titles read "Goals · The Salt Road"** (the ten that joined the project name to the page name with a space now use the separator the rest already used).
- **`--muted` moved in four themes.** Paper `#726D63` to `#6F6A60`, Ink `#8E897E` to `#969187`, Sepia `#6E5E45` to `#6B5B43`, Moss `#8D9889` to `#939D8F`: the smallest step that makes muted text 4.5:1 on the accent wash, which `scripts/check-contrast.py` now requires. Slate already passed.
- **The account menu focuses its first item when it opens** (a few lines in `shell.js`; Sign out no longer has `autofocus`, which made it the first thing Enter did).
- **The inspector's section headings are `h2`** (they were `h3` under the page's `h1`): the inspector is a region of its own and its panels are its sections.
- **The audit reuses one session for the touch browser.** The server's limit on `/auth/` requests (30 a minute from one address) is easy to reach with seven sign-ins; the audit signs in three times and copies the cookies.
- **A part of the audit that cannot run fails the run.** A page that never loads, an override that does not apply, a flow that throws: the run exits 1 with the reason, rather than passing with fewer checks. Each rule has a negative control that runs first.
- **The allow list is five entries**, each with its reason in the script and under Known gaps: the document title field has no box and is not 44 px tall (it is the page's `h1` edited in place); the command palette's and the chapter jump's search rows are a bare input beside an icon (their focus rings are drawn on the input); the breadcrumb ellipsises under the text-spacing override and at 200% text.

### What was checked, and what was not

- Checked in Chromium by `scripts/e2e-a11y.mjs`: everything in the table in ACCESSIBILITY.md, on the pages listed there, in Paper and Ink, at 1280, 640, 390 and 320 px. Existing scripts were run again on the pages that changed most: `e2e-write2` (13 of 13 steps), `e2e-review` (23 of 23), `e2e-compile` (12 of 12), and `e2e-themes` (6 of 6) and `csp-smoke` (91 checks) because stylesheets and the shared frame changed; the account menu, whose first focus changed, is opened by `e2e-pwa`, `e2e-admin` and `e2e-library`.
- Read, not heard: the accessibility tree of the writing view and ten other pages, for names, order and structure. The announcement of an offline state was driven; "Conflict", "Locked" and "Signed out" are read from `doc.js`.
- **Not verified, and the release is not ready until it is:** any screen reader (VoiceOver on macOS and iOS, NVDA, JAWS, TalkBack, Narrator); real Windows high contrast; Firefox's text-only zoom and the operating systems' larger-text settings; Safari and Firefox at all (the audit is Chromium); the Sepia, Slate and Moss themes in a browser; right-to-left; the exports; pages reached only through a flow; a drag; voice control and switch access; an audit by people who use these every day. The checklist is in ACCESSIBILITY.md and is unticked.
- **Known gaps stated as gaps:** alternatives, ghosted text and the stash give no cue in the editing surface's tree; the breadcrumb ellipsises at large text; the document title is not boxed and not 44 px; the application's text does not follow a larger default size.

## Phase 6: AI management while AI is off

**Decision.** The server's AI switch (`SCRIOBH_AI=off`, or the administrator's switch in the AI settings) is a promise that nothing is sent to any provider. It is not a promise that a person loses access to their own records. Refusing every AI op meant a person could not delete a kept chat, lift or set a never-send flag, or see what they had used while AI was off, which made "off" a reason to keep data rather than a safe state. So the switch now covers what could send and what changes what may be sent, and nothing else.

**No longer refused while AI is off** (they send nothing):

- `ai.chat.list`, `ai.chat.get`, `ai.chat.delete`: a kept chat is the person's own record. The API-key rule stays: a key reads or deletes kept chats only where the owner allowed agents to use AI.
- `ai.exclude.set`, `ai.exclude.list`: a never-send flag only narrows what may ever be sent, so setting it is safe at any time, and lifting it sends nothing by itself (any later request is still previewed and still needs AI on). Session-only and Compendium editing rules are unchanged.
- `ai.usage.get`, `ai.usage.project`, `ai.budget.get`, `ai.run.list`: read-only views of what was already counted. Owner-only (`ai.usage.project`) and the owner-or-own-runs rule of the run log are unchanged.

**Still refused while AI is off:** `ai.run`, the stream route, `ai.context.preview`, `ai.cancel`; `ai.chat.create`, `ai.chat.append` (they only make sense alongside a run), `ai.chat.preview`, `ai.chat.send`; `ai.policy.get` and `ai.policy.set`; the provider, price, cap-setting and chain ops; every recipe op in `internal/domain/aiwork` that runs a model. The AI page does not ask for the policy while AI is off and says so in words.

**Left as they were:** the kept-results reads in `aiwork` other than chats (reviews, notes, the rolling summary) still go through `gateRead`, so they stay refused while AI is off; the Lab's local marks already worked with AI off. Whether reviews and notes should also be readable and deletable while AI is off is open: the same argument applies, but their screens live in the writing view's inspector, which draws nothing of AI while it is off, so a place to show them would be needed first.

## Phase 6 review findings: what was changed

An independent security review of the Phase 6 tree raised five findings. Findings 1, 2 and 4 were reproduced with a test that failed on the old code first; finding 3 (a policy) and finding 5 (documentation, then a small behaviour) had a test written against a new name, so their first run was a build error, and the behaviour they pin is described in each entry.

- **Publishing from a key (medium).** A write-scope key, over REST or `/mcp`, could publish the manuscript to the public site folder. `site.publish`, `site.options.set` and `site.doc.set` are now session-only; the MCP snapshot lost exactly those three tools. `TestSitePublicationOpsRefuseKeys` mints keys of every scope (pinned and not, owner and editor), refuses all three over REST, checks the tool list and the site folder, and checks that a session still works and that the read tools stay. See the decision under "Phase 6 site decisions".
- **Event-log pruning and replay with webhooks off (low).** With `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOKS=off` and an enabled webhook row, the feeder cursor held the log back for ever, and switching the feature on replayed the backlog. Start now parks the cursor (a negative value, no migration) and empties the outbox when webhooks are off; the sweep treats a parked cursor as no feeder; the next start with webhooks on moves the cursor to the newest event. The rule: nothing that happened or waited while the feature was off is ever sent, and the webhook rows stay as they were. An event the feeder has not read is still never pruned while webhooks are on. Found in review of this fix: turning a webhook off and on, or rotating its secret, while the feature is off used to move the parked cursor to the head again; it is now left alone while parked, and the feeder moves a parked cursor to the newest event the first time a webhook is on. Documented under Webhooks in OPERATIONS.
- **New op groups were exposed by default (policy).** Every op group is now listed as exposed or excluded in `internal/mcp/catalog.go`; an op of an unlisted group is excluded with a reason naming the group, `BuildCatalog` refuses a group listed as both, and `TestEveryRegisteredGroupIsClassified` fails on an unclassified group or a stale entry. The groups exposed today are unchanged apart from the three site ops above.
- **PDF JPEG copy was a deny-list (low).** `cleanJPEG` in `internal/publish` kept every marker it did not name (JPEGn 0xF0 to 0xFD, the reserved 0xC8, an ICC profile the PDF does not use). It is now an allow-list: tables, restart interval, frame header, scans and the Adobe marker. `internal/imagesafe.Clean` was not reused because it re-encodes rotated pictures while the PDF path applies the orientation itself. Dropped as unneeded: ICC (the PDF names DeviceRGB), DNL (the Go decoder, which the writer checks every picture with, does not read height-zero frames), TEM and stray restarts outside a scan.
- **Restore on a second machine (documentation and behaviour).** Chosen: `scriobh restore` pauses every webhook (switched off, reason "Paused when this copy was restored from a backup"), empties the outbox and parks the feeder, and says so. It was small because restore already reopens the database for post-restore steps and a webhook already has an enabled flag and a reason the Webhooks page shows; no migration and no new screen. The owner or an administrator switches each one on again, which starts it at the newest event. The cost: after a disaster restore on the same machine the owner must switch webhooks on again, which was judged better than a copy that sends the old outbox. The restore rehearsal checks the paused state and the re-enabled API answers. `SCRIOBH_WEBHOOK_ALLOW_PRIVATE=1` now says in OPERATIONS that any project owner gets a reachability oracle against the internal network.

What remains: the MCP exposure of the existing groups is as reviewed before; a webhook's "pending" rows that were already queued while the feature was on are dropped only at the next start with it off (they age out after 14 days otherwise).

## Phase 6 release notes: what is not verified, and decisions taken at the tag

Gates run on the tag: `make ci` (formatting, vet, tests with and without the race detector, contrast, build, skill drift, bench smoke, editor build and tests, the authorisation sweep, the editor round trip, the desktop smoke test, the CSP smoke test and every browser script in `e2e-fast`, ending with the accessibility audit over 87 pages), then the browser scripts that only `make e2e` runs. The full `make bench` at 150,000 and 500,000 words is inside every budget. All exited 0. Restore and upgrade rehearsals have their own tests and scripts (`make restore-rehearsal`, `make upgrade-rehearsal`).

Not verified:

- **The soak.** A 65-minute soak at 150,000 words (66,764 saves, 13 backups, 32 DOCX compiles, the file growing from 6 to 30 MB) passes the growth rule: resident memory went from 289.7 MB (first third) to 302.5 MB (last third, 362.1 allowed), and after about 13 minutes the memory SQLite and its allocator hold stayed at 37 to 39 MB while the file kept growing. An earlier run of the same soak failed (279.9 to 358.0 MB); the cause was the writer connection's 64 MB page cache (see below). Not covered: a 500,000-word soak ran 20 minutes only (362.3 to 370.4 MB, 452.9 allowed), too short to call a plateau; nothing was run longer than 65 minutes; the soak was not run on Linux, where the allocator and the per-page cost differ (the cap bounds the cache there too, but that is not measured); why recycling the connection or shrinking memory only halves the growth, and how much the allocator retains, were read from its documentation and not tested.
- **The restore exit criterion is only partly met.** "A restore from backup is performed on a clean machine and verified" was done into a fresh data directory on the build machine, by `scripts/restore-rehearsal.sh` and `TestRestoreRehearsal` (projects, documents, saves, snapshots, goals, keys and webhooks checked afterwards), and not on a second computer. Likewise, "a real book goes from blank project to a printed proof and a validated EPUB" is covered by the compile, print and EPUB tests and the browser scripts; no page was printed on paper and no EPUB went through epubcheck.
- **No second person has installed it.** The exit criterion "two independent users complete the install guide without asking a question" has not been tried. The guide was written and followed by the people who built the thing.
- **Not run anywhere.** The Docker image and compose file; the CI pipeline; a real device, a real input method editor, Safari, Firefox or Windows file systems; a real identity provider other than the development issuer and the test one; HTTPS and SSH Git hosts other than ones started by the tests; a real AI provider; a real MCP client; public HTTPS webhook receivers.
- **Output not opened in the programs that matter.** Word (the DOCX files), a Kobo or other reader and epubcheck (the EPUBs), a printer (the print PDFs and the paperback cover), a feed reader, a search engine's sitemap tools. Real Scrivener, WordPress and Ghost export files were not used; the importers were built from the formats' documentation and from fixtures made for the tests.
- **No screen reader was used anywhere.** The accessibility audit is automated rules plus keyboard and text-size passes (see [ACCESSIBILITY](ACCESSIBILITY.md)). Known gaps listed there: the alternatives, ghost and stash cues are not in the editing surface's accessibility tree; the header breadcrumb ellipsises at 200% text size (the page's `h1` and the control's name carry the words); type sizes are in pixels, so the text-size control and not the browser's default scales them; the generated site's stylesheet (`internal/site/css.go`) still holds the old muted colour.
- **The AI screens' words are not in the i18n catalogue** and were not looked at under the right-to-left pseudo-locale. Two pieces of code still guard unsaved work (`arm.js` and the AI screens'); they behave the same today and may drift.
- **Phase 5's open items** (see the Phase 5 notes) are as they were: nothing here was run against a real mail or chat service.
- **Export records** (about 35 KB each) are never removed. No retention rule has been decided.
- **Trashed projects** are read-only and every op refuses them, but a few background paths were left, and are documented where they are: the on-disk mirror, the mention and link index workers, an AI run that was already streaming when the project was trashed, and merging entities.
- **The agent command line against old servers:** `scriobh-cli --dry-run` exits 0 against a server that predates dry runs (it cannot tell). `scriobh-cli site publish`, `site options set` and `site doc set` no longer work, because those three ops are session-only (see the Phase 6 site decisions).
- **Mirror:** the first write after a project is created is slow; it is not on the save path.
- **AI:** "Accept as alternative" for an AI rewrite does not mark the alternative as generated (there is no op for it).
- **Writing view, inspector:** a live redraw of the Versions list can detach a button while it is being tapped. The browser script that checks touch targets was changed to measure in one step; the redraw itself was not.
- **Disk:** the screenshots committed with each phase are a subset (desktop light and dark, phone light), kept under 15 MB a round.
- **Size:** the CSS shipped across the app is 55,242 bytes of the 64 KB cap. The next screen that needs its own sheet has to make room first.

Decided at the tag:

- **Field borders.** The accessibility audit raised the contrast of form-field borders (the `--field-line` token) and added a forced-colours block. Fields look slightly heavier than in earlier tags in both themes. This is deliberate and visible; it is the one design change of the audit.
- **Scrivener sheets.** A Scrivener project's character and place sheets are not turned into Compendium entities by default (`scrivener_sheets: entities` opts in). Flipping the default is one line; it was left off so a first import never creates entities nobody asked for.
- **Session-only publication.** Publishing a site and changing what is published need a signed-in session, not a key of any scope, and the MCP catalogue exposes only groups named on an allow-list (a new op group is excluded until someone decides it).
- **Webhooks.** The feeder's cursor is parked while webhooks are off and `scriobh restore` pauses them, so a restore never replays into the outside world.
- **Reader and writer caches.** Every connection, writer and readers, has one 8 MB page cache (`cache_size(-8192)`). The writer had 64 MB, and its cache grew by about 1.1 MB per MB of database written; the four readers held under 1 MB between them because the memory map serves their reads. Bench medians (save, search, page, search rebuild, backup, mirror write) were the same at 64, 8, 4 and 2 MB at both sizes; 8 MB was chosen over 2 MB as margin for bulk import and restore, which the bench does not time. `TestPageCacheStaysWithinItsLimit` fails if a connection's cache passes about 10 MB. The soak now also reports the process footprint (macOS `footprint`, Linux `RssAnon`) beside resident memory, without judging it.

## Phase 7 release notes: what is not verified, and decisions taken at the tag

Every backend and every set of screens in this phase was reviewed by someone who did not write it, against the code, in a browser where there was a screen, and the findings were fixed and re-checked before the merge. The details of each (what was built, limits, measured numbers, what was not checked) are in `docs/p7-notes/`: `epub.md`, `pdfword.md`, `editiondiff.md`, `gitpub.md`, `mail.md`, `semantic.md`, `pandoc.md`, `passphrase.md`, `presence.md`, `typography.md` and the four `ui-*.md` files. This section is the summary and the decisions.

Gates run on the tag: on the build machine (a Mac), on the release tree: `make ci` (formatting, vet, the race pass four packages at a time with a 90 minute limit, the plain pass, contrast, build, skill drift, bench smoke, the editor build and tests, the authorisation sweep, the editor round trip, the desktop smoke test, the CSP smoke test and the 40 browser scripts of `e2e-fast`, ending with the accessibility audit: 134 pages, 0 violations, 16 excused), the 17 further browser scripts of `make e2e`, the full `make bench` at 150,000 and 500,000 words against a baseline refreshed on a quiet machine, `scripts/restore-rehearsal.sh`, and `scripts/upgrade-rehearsal.sh --all` (seven tags, v0.1 to v1.0, rollback included). The stages were run in sequence over several starts, not in one unbroken run: each start that failed was fixed and the failed stage and everything after it run again. What the starts found and fixed: a fresh checkout had no editor dependencies (`make editor-deps` now comes first); the race pass at one package per CPU saturated the machine and timing tests measured the machine (four at a time, and race-scaled budgets where a test had none); library titles were cut off at 200% text size (they wrap now). In CI (a runner with four vCPUs, one job at a time): the push gate on `443ae38` passed all three jobs: `quick` (workflow syntax, formatting, vet, contrast, build, skill drift, the editor dependencies, bundle check and unit tests, bench smoke, the authorisation sweep, the editor round trip, the plain pass one package at a time on linux/amd64, and the desktop smoke test), `race-core` (the race detector on the concurrent packages) and `image` (the container image built with buildah and published). Getting there took several runs: tests that waited for the mirror read paths before the folders existed, or looked for a file once outside their wait loop, failed only on the slower runner and were fixed. The on-demand browser jobs (`deep`) were not run in CI for this tag; they ran on the build machine inside `make ci`. The build machine's gates ran on `688c1c9`. The commits after it, up to the tag, change tests, the Makefile, the workflows, CI scripts, the editor's test-file globs, documentation and one flush in the test Git server's shutdown handler; no product code. The CI run above and a local run under the runner's limits (four cores, the plain pass and race-core) covered the tip. Since then `make test` runs the plain pass and the race detector on the concurrent packages only; the whole-tree race pass is `make test-race`, run by hand.

Not verified:

- **Nothing here has met a real outside system.** GitHub, Forgejo and Gitea (the repository, release and account code ran against a fake host written from the providers' documentation; sixteen provider behaviours are listed in `gitpub.md`), an SMTP relay and Amazon's Send to Kindle (assumptions are from third-party guides; Amazon's own page could not be read), a real embedding model or an Ollama or OpenAI-compatible server (the index ran against a fake that hashes words into vectors: it proves the plumbing, not the quality of results), and Pandoc (every test used a fake `pandoc` script; a real-Pandoc integration test exists and has never run).
- **No real book files.** Every EPUB, PDF and Word fixture is a miniature built for the tests, imitating the habits of Sigil, Calibre, Pandoc, Vellum, Project Gutenberg, Word, LibreOffice, pdfTeX, Chrome and InDesign. Kindle Previewer's EPUB was not modelled at all. The PDF reader was also run over real PDFs from TeX, Ghostscript and Apple Quartz on the build machine, which found and fixed two bugs; those files are not in the repository. No output was opened in Word, a reader, epubcheck or a printer.
- **The PDF reader runs in a child process with a time and memory limit** (20 s and 512 MB by default). The Linux limits (`RLIMIT_DATA`, parent-death signal, `/proc` polling) were exercised only by the Go tests in CI (linux/amd64), including the PDF child-process tests in `internal/imports`; the build machine is a Mac, where only polling and the Go memory limit apply. Whether `ps` was present for the one child test that needs it is not recorded in the log. The reader is hand-written; two independent reviews found and had fixed five classes of resource exhaustion and a stack overflow, and a third round replaced in-process hardening with isolation. It is the part of this release most worth fuzzing again with real hostile files.
- **Pandoc's own safety was reasoned, not observed.** The bridge refuses any Pandoc older than 3.1.6 (an arbitrary-file-write flaw, CVE-2023-38745, that `--sandbox` does not block), extracts pictures only when the person asks, and runs the converter with a stripped environment, a size-capped call folder and a helper that sets resource limits. Whether `--sandbox` blocks file inclusion in each input format, and whether `-o`, `--extract-media` and the memory option behave as the notes assume, can only be confirmed by `pandoc.config.test` and the skipped integration test on a machine that has Pandoc.
- **Semantic search quality** with a real model, and the remote-endpoint path, are unverified. The 80 ms query budget was measured against a synthetic 10,000-chunk index (about 3 ms warm, about 30 ms cold).
- **Browsers.** Chromium only. No Safari, no Firefox, no real phone, no screen reader, no real input method editor. The offline reload keeps the app shell and says what is not kept; typed form text is not kept across a reload with the network cut on the screens that submit to the server.
- **The project passphrase is not encryption** and the screens say so. It does not protect against the server operator, the database file, backups, the on-disk mirror, Git pushes or exports, nor against browser developer tools after unlocking. Unsaved drafts are kept in the browser's storage in the clear until saved or until sign-out. On a lock the page removes the manuscript from the document and goes to the unlock page; while offline it keeps a cover with nothing behind it. A tab hidden for longer than the idle limit and an offline reload on the locked cover were not driven in a browser.
- **Platforms.** Everything was run on macOS (Apple silicon), and the Go tests also on linux/amd64 in CI (Debian 12 containers). No browser and no desktop build ran on Linux. Windows file systems and FreeBSD were compiled only.
- **Timing tests need a quiet machine.** Several tests assert time budgets. They pass alone and in the gate above, and fail when something else is using the cores: in CI, a push gate that ran while a `deep` job was running failed `internal/text`'s budget (seen three times). The workflows keep two runs of the same workflow apart; they do not keep `ci` and `deep` apart, so do not dispatch `deep` while a push is being checked.
- **Continuous integration.** The push gate (`quick`, then `race-core` and `image`) has no browser and runs the race detector only on the concurrent packages; the browser jobs are in the development repository's `deep.yml`, run on demand, and the whole-tree race pass is run by hand (`make test-race`). The image job builds and publishes the image but no longer starts it to check its user and its refusal of the development issuer. Every pushed branch moves the `:latest` image tag.
- **The stored sources of imported books are kept intact on the server** (32 MB each, 50 per project, counted against the quotas) and are not in the mirror, a Git push, a share or an export bundle. A hard crash between placing a file and committing leaves an orphan that an hourly sweep removes after an hour.
- **Carried forward unchanged from v1.0:** the unverified list in the Phase 6 notes, narrowed: the container image is now built and published by CI but never started there, compose is untested, and real identity providers, real MCP clients, real devices), the two-independent-users install criterion, export records that are never removed.

Decided at the tag:

- **Email, reversing an earlier "never".** FEATURES had listed email digests as never built, for the privacy invariant. Mail is now allowed only as a service an administrator configures (off by default, the password sealed, STARTTLS or TLS required, a relay on a private address only if the administrator says so), sends nothing unless a person turned that feature on, never carries manuscript text, comment text or document titles, is paused by a restore, and is bound to the address the person gave. Send to Kindle goes only to the person's own stored address, limited to `kindle.com` and `free.kindle.com` unless the administrator widens the list, with a preview first. The digest carries counts and project titles only; it names nothing about a project that has a passphrase.
- **A remote's audience.** A new Git remote defaults, at the op, to the strict public filter (what a reader sees). The screens make the audience an explicit choice with nothing preselected. Anything that widens what reaches a public place needs a typed confirmation that the op itself enforces, and those changes are refused for API keys and the command line.
- **Publishing needs a person.** Creating or widening a public repository, signing, triggers and releases are reachable only through a browser session. Over MCP, the repository, mail, Kindle, Pandoc, passphrase and presence operations are not offered, nor are `edition.diff`, `edition.compare` and `edition.take` (they can return an edition's text or overwrite a chapter); `edition.suggest` is offered, needs an editor key with write scope, and returns identifiers and counts only.
- **An instance administrator counts as the owner of every project** in the REST ops, as before this phase (the sweeps pin it). So an administrator who is not a member of a project can compare one of their editions with it, send it to their own Kindle address and read its editions over REST; the project pages find a project through membership and answer 404 to the same person. The difference is documented, not removed.
- **Presence is off by default and symmetric** (a person who does not share also sees no one), lives in memory only, never names a chapter to someone who cannot read it, and cannot keep a session or an unlock alive.
- **Typography never overrides a reader.** A person's own non-default font, line height and measure, or their personal override, always win over a project's typography. A project can never make text smaller, tighter or narrower than a reader chose.
- **Editions.** Importing a book keeps the original file as an edition. Edition text (the book's own words, including chapters left out at import) is returned only to editors with write scope; chapters left out are invisible below editor. A comparison is one at a time per person. The EPUB exporter now writes a chapter's title into the file only when the title is printed, so a book with numbered chapters round-trips as numbered chapters without titles.
- **Word tracked changes** import as suggestions by default: the chapter holds the original text and each change waits to be accepted or rejected, grouped by author; where two authors edited the same sentence the later author's change is already in the text, and the preview says so.
- **Stylesheet cap.** The sum of all stylesheets may now reach 96 KB (was 64 KB); the 25 KB per-page budget that protects a reader is unchanged.
- **The writing view's hot path** was measured against v1.0: the editor mounts about 160 ms after its bundle arrives on both (budget 250 ms). The save path is 4 to 6 percent slower than v1.0 on a quiet machine (`http.save` p50 2.36 to 2.49 ms, `doc.update` 2.25 to 2.33 ms); the cause was not found and it is far inside the budgets. The passphrase check costs about 20 microseconds for a project with no passphrase. A 20-minute soak with every new background worker present and idle held memory, goroutines and open files flat.
- **Restore never leaves a sender on.** The pauses (Git remotes and triggers, webhooks, mail, the semantic index, the Pandoc bridge) are made on the staged database before a file is installed; a marker makes the server refuse to start until every pause is made, and `scriobh restore --finish` makes them by hand.

## Phase 7.5 release notes: what is not verified, and decisions taken at the tag

The studio was built in two halves and each was reviewed by someone who did not write it, against the code and, for the screens, in a browser; the findings were fixed and re-checked before the merge (the review's findings and what was changed are in `docs/p7-notes/ui-studio.md`, "Fix round"). The details of each half (what was built, limits, measured numbers, what was not checked) are in `docs/p7-notes/studio.md` (the backend: FEATURES section BB, F-1000 to F-1025) and `docs/p7-notes/ui-studio.md` (the screens: section BF, F-1101 to F-1119). This section is the summary and the decisions.

Gates run on the tag, on the build machine (a Mac, Apple silicon, 16 cores, with a short clean `PATH`), on the release tree. The product code is that of `e69dae8`; the commits after it change one browser-script helper, one test, the bench baseline, the rehearsal script and documentation, and no product code.

- **`make ci`** (workflow-file check, formatting, vet, the plain pass and the race detector on the concurrent packages, contrast over five themes with 0 failing pairs, the production build, skill and CLI drift, bench smoke, the editor build and its unit and Chromium tests, the authorisation sweep, the editor round trip, the desktop smoke test, the CSP smoke test with 91 checks, and the 41 browser scripts of `e2e-fast`, ending with the accessibility walk). The first run, on `e69dae8`, passed everything up to `e2e-mail`, which failed (see below), so the run stopped there after 35.6 minutes. The run was then repeated whole on the release tree after the fixes below (`07acb43`): `make ci` ran to the end and exited 0 in 51.5 minutes, with every stage passing: the race pass, the plain pass, the 41 browser scripts of `e2e-fast` and the accessibility walk (144 pages, 0 violations, 16 excused) among them.
- **The full accessibility walk:** 144 pages, 0 violations, 16 excused (the same 16 as before; none added), nine of the pages studio pages, in 14.2 minutes (it is also the last step of `e2e-fast`).
- **`make e2e-extra`**, the 17 further browser scripts of `make e2e`: all passed, 7.0 minutes. The scripts of `e2e-fast` after the failure were run one by one after the fix (`mail`, `pandoc`, `publish`, `gate`, `compmanage`, `semantic`, `studio`, `typography`, `a11y`): all passed.
- **The full `make bench`** at 150,000 and 500,000 words, run on a machine with only its ordinary desktop programs running (load average 2.4 to 2.9 on 16 cores before the first two runs, the machine's own desktop programs only; the third started with the load at 7 as its build began): every budget held, and the first run, made before the baseline entry existed, showed no regression in any metric against the baseline of 15 November. `studio.dashboard` over five years of daily writing on 20 projects (36,520 daily rows) is p50 40.3 ms and p95 43.2 ms at 150k words, p50 40.8 ms and p95 42.5 ms at 500k, against a budget of p95 under 150 ms; `studio.calendar.all` is p50 17 ms and p95 17.5 ms. The save path is 6 to 9 percent over the baseline of 15 November (`doc.update` p50 2.42 ms against 2.28 at 150k and 3.32 against 3.06 at 500k; `http.save` 2.55 against 2.39 and 3.49 against 3.17), inside the 20 percent tolerance and far inside the budgets of 120 and 400 ms; the backend note measured this branch against the `phase-7` tip with the runs interleaved and found the same save time (3.0 to 3.2 ms on both), so the difference from the baseline is taken to be the machine's day-to-day spread; it was not separated further here. The two metrics were added to `bench/baseline.json` (best of three, 30 runs each, at 150k and 500k) and nothing else in the file was changed; `make bench` passes against the new entries, and a baseline altered to say 10 ms makes the run fail as a regression. Two single-timing metrics read higher than their baseline (`mirror.rebuild_noop` 17 to 35 ms and `search.rebuild` 35 to 48 ms at 150k words), both under the 75 ms noise floor for a metric timed once, so not flagged. It is the machine and not the branch: the studio's fixture is built after those two are timed, and the `v1.1` tag, built from `git archive` and run twice at the same time, reads 37 and 31 ms for `mirror.rebuild_noop` and 44 and 42 ms for `search.rebuild` at 150k words, the same as this branch (31 to 36 and 42 to 48 ms).
- **`scripts/upgrade-rehearsal.sh --all`**, now eight tags (v0.1, v0.2, v0.3, v0.3.5, v0.4, v0.5, v1.0 and v1.1, rollback included): all passed, 33 seconds. For v1.1 six migrations were applied (79 before, 85 after), the schema equals a fresh install's, every old answer carries every old field, a second start applies nothing, and the rollback to the pre-migration copy works. **`scripts/restore-rehearsal.sh`** passed in 96 seconds.
- **In CI:** the push gate passed on `e69dae8`.

What the run found and fixed:

- `e2e-mail` failed on this Mac: the fake SMTP relay made its certificate with `/usr/bin/openssl`, which on macOS is LibreSSL 3.3.6, and LibreSSL writes the key's curve parameters in full, which Go refuses (`invalid ECDSA parameters`). This is the fault `scripts/fakehost.mjs` already had; `scripts/fake-smtp.mjs` now asks for the curve by name. Proven by making the certificate both ways with that program and loading each in Go (the old one fails with that error, the new one loads), and by `e2e-mail` going from 6 of 16 steps to 16 of 16.
- `TestUpgradeRehearsal` failed for v1.1, the first time it was run with that tag: it required every old Git remote to read as private after the upgrade, which holds only for a release before the audience setting. A v1.1 remote made without an audience is public, and the upgrade keeps it public. The test now records the audience the old release gave and requires the upgrade to keep it; a release with no such field is still expected to read as private. Proven by the failure (v1.1 failed with the audience `public` against `private`, every time) and the pass after (v0.1 to v1.0 are unchanged).

Not verified:

- **No screen reader was used on the studio.** The calendar is a table of 24 columns with a sentence in every cell, the charts carry their numbers in a title and a table, and the walk checks rules and keyboard use; how a screen reader reads a 24-column table is not known, and a person may prefer the chart's table or the CSV.
- **No real phone, and Chromium only.** 390 px was run in Chromium; no Safari, no Firefox, no touch device, no real input method editor. 200% text was checked by the walk's rule, not by eye.
- **The progress email as delivered.** It was tested against the fake relay of `internal/mail`: a real relay, a real mailbox and how a client shows the plain text were not seen. A daylight-saving change at 07:00 on a Monday was not tested (the week and month slots are pure functions tested with fixed dates and zones).
- **A real 15-minute sprint.** The browser script moves the page's clock. A sprint that runs across local midnight was proven in the backend (30 words before and 70 after make a sprint of 100 beside days of 30 and 70) and not driven in a browser, and neither was the sprint line after a laptop sleeps (the script re-reads on `visibilitychange`).
- **The Hebrew name on the card outside Chromium.** The card's SVG puts a project's name in its own run with `unicode-bidi: plaintext`; it was looked at in Chromium. How Safari, Firefox or an image program draw it was not seen. The PNG, which the page makes from the SVG in a canvas, was checked for its type, size and width and not looked at.
- **Not looked at.** The review listed the pages that were taken as screenshots and not looked at (Moss, the compare and export page, the project's Goals page, the new person at 390 px, the public card in light); the contrast and reflow checks of the browser script cover them.
- **Offline.** Every studio page is `no-store`: a reload with the network cut shows the offline notice, and nothing typed into a studio form survives it. The backend note's "show the last answer from memory" was not built.
- **Platforms.** Everything was run on macOS (Apple silicon), including the desktop smoke test, which is part of `make ci` (the studio names no origin: the card's link is built from the page's own). No browser and no desktop smoke ran on Linux.
- **Dashboard size.** Measured on five years and 20 projects of daily rows; the read is linear in a person's daily rows, and a larger person was not measured. No soak was run for this tag, and the new background parts (the card view counts, the progress email's schedule) were exercised by tests, not by a soak.
- **The history in the browser scripts** is written by a Go helper (`TestSeedStudioHistory`), not through the save loop: the rows have the bench fixture's shape and the pages are checked against the API over them.
- **The counting rule for words added** is the existing one (`words.go`) with its approximations (a replacement by the same word earns nothing, a move between documents is credited again); every figure here shares them.
- **Carried forward unchanged from v1.1:** the unverified list in the Phase 7 notes (nothing met a real outside system; no real book files; Pandoc and the quality of semantic search; the passphrase is not encryption; timing tests need a quiet machine), the two-independent-users install criterion, and export records that are never removed. Phase 8 is not built on this branch.

Decided at the tag:

- **Goals count words added.** Net words are carried beside every figure and never fail a day, a week or a goal. A day with no writing is empty: no value anywhere in the studio is called missed, behind, failed or lost, and a test scans the message catalogue for those words. The one place a goal reads a net figure is a total goal in words, whose value is the manuscript's size.
- **The forecast reads net words, in plain terms.** A total goal in words is reached by the manuscript growing, so its finish is projected from the manuscript's growth over the 28 days ending today, and the rate is worded "about N net words a day". When the net rate is not positive, or there are fewer than five writing days, no date is shown and the line is the neutral "No date can be estimated yet from the last weeks of writing". A projection later than a deadline is never stated as days behind; the margin is information, not a verdict.
- **Compare uses finished days only.** The presets set the days of this period before today against the same days of the one before (31 March is 1 to 28 March against 1 to 28 February), and today is not in them: it is on the Today card. A period with no finished day (a Monday, the first of a month) is shown so far in one column, with no difference and a note that the comparison starts tomorrow. Two ranges of unequal length show a dash and no signed figure.
- **A lapsed streak is never drawn as a hero zero.** A run of 0 draws no numeral: the dashboard says the past month's writing days and what starts a run, the longest run only when there was one. The card's picture, the public page and the progress email leave out "0 days in a row" and a month of 0.
- **Today's card never says "fewer".** It says "more than yesterday", "the same", or nothing; a card whose previous period is empty says nothing. The 7, 30 and 365-day cards keep their "fewer than the N days before" sentence (a complete period against a complete period), and a percentage is said only of a gain. There is no arrow and no colour in a comparison.
- **A goal day is a day a daily goal in force that day was met**, judged by the versions then in force: raising a goal never takes a met day or week away, lowering one helps the period it was lowered in, and a week is never judged against a target that did not exist yet. Today does not break a streak until it is over. Completion of a total goal is sticky; un-archiving a goal is not built.
- **Only what the viewer may count is counted.** One rule (`Viewer`) decides which projects count for every consumer: a project that is locked, in the trash, left, or protected where there is no session contributes nothing, and the streak says so. The cost: while a project is left out a past period of a raised goal may read as not met, and it reads right again when the project counts again.
- **The public card.** A private link, not a social network: a 256-bit token whose hash is all that is stored, shown once, expiring (1 to 365 days, 30 by default), revocable, at most five live, browser session only. It shows the streak, writing days in the last 30, goal counts and up to three goals, never a document title, a comment, a name or the owner's label, and no project title unless the card was made with that choice; a project with a passphrase is never on a card. The page has no script and sends no cookie; a public GET never writes (views are counted in memory and written once a minute). The PNG is made by the owner's screen from the SVG, and no rasteriser was added.
- **The progress email** is opt-in and off by default, weekly or monthly, bound to the address it was turned on for, once per period, silent for a period with nothing to say, built from fixed words and numbers with no manuscript text, comment text or document title, and made again from its dates at the moment it is sent. A restore pauses it with the rest of mail.
- **Analytics stay on the server.** No request leaves it; the only exits are a card a person made and an email a person turned on.
- **MCP is fail-closed.** The `studio` group is exposed through an allow-list of eight read ops; the writes, the card ops and the progress email are not offered. A key pinned to a project reaches no person-wide figure, and `studio.card.*` and `mail.progress.*` refuse every key.
- **One place for goals.** `/studio/goals` lists every goal and is the one place a new one is made; the project's Goals page keeps its shared target and the legacy personal goal (which `daily_stats.goal_hit` freezes) and gains the person's own weekly words on that project. A separate page per project was not built.
- **Nudges are recorded when dismissed, not when seen.** A GET that wrote would repeat on reload, so a nudge stays until the person presses Dismiss. The cost: a person who dismisses nothing can meet more than three different nudges across a day. Holding the daily limit exactly needs a server-side record made by the read, which is a backend change.
- **The writing view does not move while a sprint runs.** The status bar has a sprint line only while a sprint runs, its place is as wide as the longest thing it can say, and `sprint.js` is fetched only when a sprint is running or started. A sprint's words are the words recorded while it ran, counted once in their day and once in the sprint; a sprint left running is read as completed after its closing time, never as failed. On a phone there is no status bar, so the minutes left are the first line of the Sprint menu.
- **Stylesheets and scripts.** All stylesheets are 69.1 KB of the 96 KB cap; `studio.css` is 3.2 KB gzip on the studio's pages only; `sprint.js` 2.7 KB (its limit in `internal/server/web_test.go` is 2,944 bytes gzip) and `card.js` 1.1 KB are lazy; `doc.js` and `tree.js` are untouched.
- **The accessibility walk's backwards Tab rule** now allows a date or time field the several stops the forward rule already allowed. It was an intermittent finding on a page this phase did not change; the rule's self-test, which breaks every rule on purpose, still passes.
- **The upgrade rehearsal's default list** now names v1.1 (`--all` already read the tags), and its Git expectation follows what the old release reported (above).

## Phase 8 release notes: what is not verified, and decisions taken at the tag

The desktop app was built in two halves, the Go side (desktop mode of the server, `docs/p8-notes/desktop-go.md`, FEATURES section BD) and the Tauri shell in `desktop/` (`docs/p8-notes/desktop-shell.md`, section BE), to a contract (`docs/p8-notes/desktop-contract.md`) the Go side amended after review (thirty numbered changes), with a threat model (`docs/p8-notes/desktop-threat-model.md`) and a release runbook (`docs/p8-notes/release-runbook.md`). Each half was reviewed by someone who did not write it, the shell was run against the Go side's real server, and the findings were fixed and re-checked before the merge. `docs/DESKTOP.md` is the page for a person. This section is the summary and the decisions. **Neither half had run the whole `make ci` or the whole `make e2e` before this tag**; this is the first time they ran on the merged tree.

Gates run on the tag, on the build machine (a Mac, Apple M4 Max, 16 cores, macOS 26.6.2, with a short clean `PATH`, Go 1.27.4, Node 22.22.2, Rust 1.100.0), on the release tree. The product code is that of `5e542aa`; the commits after it change browser scripts, two Go test files, a rehearsal script and documentation, and no product code. The stages were run in several starts, each failure fixed and the failed stage and what follows it run again; **`make ci` did not exit 0 in one unbroken run on one tree**, and this section says which tree each part ran on.

- **`make editor-deps`, then `make ci`.** First start, on `2060c55` (the merge as handed over): every stage passed up to `e2e-write2`, which failed after 26.5 minutes (see below). Second start of `make ci`, on `7f5d31d` (with the two fixes of the first two failures): every stage before the browser scripts passed (workflow-file check, formatting, vet, the plain pass, the race detector on the concurrent packages, contrast over five themes with 0 failing pairs, the production build, skill and CLI drift, bench smoke, the editor build and its unit and Chromium tests, the authorisation sweep, the editor round trip, the desktop smoke test, the CSP smoke test with 91 checks passed) and `e2e-fast` failed in `e2e-library` after 24.2 minutes in all. The stage that failed was then run on the fixed tree: **`make e2e-fast`, on `c90ac38`, exited 0 in 29.0 minutes**: all 42 browser scripts passed, ending with the accessibility walk (150 pages, 0 violations, 16 excused, the same 16 as before; none added; the three desktop pages are among the pages).
- **`make e2e-extra`**, the 17 further browser scripts of `make e2e`, on `c90ac38`: all passed, 7.1 minutes. In between, after the editions fix, the 13 scripts of `e2e-fast` from `e2e-editions-diff` on and the 17 of `e2e-extra` (30 in all) were also run one at a time on `5e542aa` without stopping at a failure: all passed.
- **The desktop gates**, on `c90ac38`: `make desktop-sidecar && make desktop-test` (format check, clippy with warnings as errors, and the Rust tests through `desktop/scripts/test.sh`, which points the five real-server tests at the production server it has just built): passed in 53 seconds with a cold target folder (against the server `sidecar.sh` built from `c90ac38`, which reports `v1.1-123-gc90ac38` because `v1.2` is not an ancestor of this branch; 70 unit tests, 18 launch tests against the stand-in, 8 lockdown tests, 5 real-server tests, 1 argument test, 2 review launch tests and 1 trickle test pass: 105; 1 is ignored, the one that needs a login keychain). `desktop/scripts/smoke.sh` on a development build against the stand-in server, which opens a real window on this Mac: 66 checks passed, 25 seconds, and no process of ours left. `scripts/desktop-smoke.sh`, the production binary in desktop mode driven with `curl`: passed, 15 seconds (it also runs inside `make ci`).
- **The push gate's own pass on the tip**, on `884d9f2` (the last commit before these notes, after the Go test files and scripts changed): `make workflows-check fmt-check vet skill-check` clean (no drift), and `SCRIOBH_TIMING=off make test-plain` (every package, `-count=1`, four at a time, as the forge runs it) passed in 8.0 minutes. The race pass and the timing tests ran on `7f5d31d`, before the later changes, which touch test files, scripts and documents only.
- **`make bench`** at 150,000 and 500,000 words, on `1fed168`, against the entry for `darwin/arm64/16cpu` in `bench/baseline.json` (every metric has an entry, so the regression check ran; the run printed no note about a missing entry or a generator mismatch): `ok`, 27 seconds. Load average before the run 2.5 (one minute; 4.5 and 4.0 over five and fifteen) with only the ordinary desktop programs and this session running, nothing of the gate alongside. `doc.update` p50 2.33 ms against 2.28 at 150k and 3.15 against 3.06 at 500k, `http.save` 2.45 against 2.39 and 3.24 against 3.17, `page.chapter` 5.89 against 5.61 and 17.69 against 17.09; `studio.dashboard` p50 38.7 ms and `studio.calendar.all` 16.5 ms at 150k (budget p95 under 150 ms); every p50 of a metric timed thirty times is within 6 percent of the baseline except `revision.list` at 150k (0.20 against 0.18 ms, 13 percent, far under the 5 ms noise floor), and the metrics timed once or three times read higher at 150k (`mirror.rebuild_noop` 23.0 against 17.5 ms, under the 75 ms noise floor for a single timing; `search.rebuild` 40.0 against 35.4 and `search.semantic.load` 34.8 against 31.0, also single timings; `import.epub`, three timings, 687 against 625), none flagged by the tool. Phase 8 touches no bench metric and no entry was added or changed: `git diff bench/` is empty. The 1M-word run and the soak were not made.
- **`scripts/upgrade-rehearsal.sh --all`**, now nine tags (v0.1, v0.2, v0.3, v0.3.5, v0.4, v0.5, v1.0, v1.1 and v1.2): all passed, 38 seconds. **v1.2 was tagged while this gate ran** (`075ba02`, the merge of `phase-7.5` into `main`; its tree is that of `5cf687c`, the commit this gate was told to use), so it was rehearsed as a tag. **`scripts/restore-rehearsal.sh`** passed in 98 seconds.
- **On the forge** (the facts only): web run 146 passed the push gate on `c543a46` (`phase-8` with the studio merged). Web run 147, the `linux` desktop job on `2003268`, built the `.deb`, installed it with apt, launched the installed app under Xvfb, saw its bundled server answer `/healthz` with the lock held and `secret.key` made with mode 600 (no keychain), and quit it with `SIGTERM` with the server gone and the lock released. Web run 148's `image` job checked that the image's user is 65532:65532, that its production binary refuses the development issuer, and that `/data` is owned by 65532.

What the run found and fixed:

- `e2e-write2`, "signing out first saves a detail typed a moment ago", failed on every run (`2060c55`). Earlier steps of the script leave a held field and a draft in the browser, so signing out asks first (the page's own question, "This browser holds unsaved text that was never saved to the server (chapters or notes: 2)"). The step used to be answered by the harness's blanket acceptance of the browser's `confirm()` (`dialog` in `scripts/e2e-kit.mjs`), which is what the page no longer calls, so nobody answered and the step waited for a sign-out that was waiting for the answer. The step now answers the page's question with `armOk` and checks that it was asked, as the seven scripts the Go side changed do. Proven by the failure (twice, the same step, 15 s) and the pass (13 of 13).
- **`e2e-editions` found a product regression.** The Go side put `download` on the edition download links so that the macOS webview saves a PDF instead of showing it. A link with `download` saves whatever answers it, and the download's answer to a missing source file was a redirect to the edition's page with a one-time sentence, so the browser saved the edition's page as a file named for the book (probed: `edn_….html`) and the person never saw the sentence; the Go test pinned the redirect, so only the browser script saw it. The Editions pages now look at the disk (`editions.Present`: a regular file of the recorded size, not hashed) and show the sentence where the link was, in the list and on the edition; the download itself answers 404 with the sentence as plain text; the script clicks a link on a page drawn before the file went and fails if a file is saved. Proven by the old code failing the new script with "a link on a stale page saved a file: edn_….html" and the new code passing, and by the same step passing with the attribute removed from the old code (which fixes the cause the other way). The statement in the shell notes that `download` "changes nothing in a browser" was wrong and is corrected (F-1149, `desktop-go.md`). Not covered, and said in those places: a session that ends between drawing a page and pressing the edition's link is answered by a redirect to sign-in, which a browser saves for a `download` link (a Go test pins the redirect; not driven in a browser); the file page's links answer a 401 for it (read, not driven); a project that locks in between was not checked.
- `e2e-library`, "writing modes: dimmed paragraphs and typewriter scrolling follow the settings", failed once in three runs ("the paragraph with the caret (block 0) is at full strength: got 0.55"). The caret starts in the first paragraph, which is already marked, so the wait for "one paragraph is marked" was true before the click had any effect, and the step read which paragraph was marked and then its opacity on either side of the mark's move. It now waits for the mark to be on the paragraph it clicked (block 40) and reads after that. Proven by forcing the interleaving in the code under test (the mark is held on the first paragraph and the click's selection is released in the microtask after the page is first asked for `.scriobh-prose > *`): the old script fails with the original message, the new one passes; and with the click's effect only delayed, the old script is shown reading the wrong paragraph (block 0 after a click on block 40). Five runs of the new script on the unchanged code pass.
- `TestUpgradeRehearsal` failed for v1.2, the first time it was run with that tag: it required exactly one pre-migration copy, and v2.0 adds no migration, so the server correctly takes none. The rehearsal now works out whether any migration is pending: if so, everything is as before (v1.1 passes through that path, strict); if not, it requires that none was applied and no copy taken, that a second start applies nothing, and that the old binary starts on the upgraded database and reads every project with its own session, in place of the copy-back (which cannot be done without a copy, and would drop the edit the rehearsal makes after the upgrade). Proven by the failure ("pre-migration copies: []") and the pass; v0.1 to v1.1 are unchanged.
- Two statements corrected: the limits table of `desktop-go.md` still said the launch token had a lockout after five wrong guesses (contract change 7 removed it, and the code has none), and the smoke's check count and the forge's installed-package run were out of date in `DESKTOP.md` and `OPERATIONS.md`.

Not verified:

- **macOS signing and notarisation.** Nothing is signed: no Developer ID identity exists on the build machine, and only the command-line tools, not full Xcode, are installed. No `.dmg` was made or opened, and **`make desktop-build` (the unsigned `.app` with the server inside) was not run in this gate**; the one unsigned debug `.app` of the shell notes pre-dates the rename of the bundled server to `scriobh-desktop-server`, which is unverified in any bundle.
- **Windows, entirely.** The Go side compiles for it (`GOOS=windows go build -tags prod`); the exclusive-open single-instance lock, the exclusive listener, `%AppData%`, file modes, the stop on standard input (the only clean stop there), the `windows` job of `desktop.yml` and WebView2 were never run. The forge has no Windows or macOS runner, so those two jobs have never run.
- **A real keychain on any platform.** The macOS Keychain was exercised in development runs under a throwaway service name (a key made at first run was given again at the next launch; the one ignored Rust test makes and deletes a throwaway entry and was not run in this gate). The entry the shipped app makes, a macOS prompt, a locked keychain, and the stop the app makes when the keychain cannot be read over data with no key file (covered by the decision function's tests and by reading) were never seen in a window. Windows Credential Manager and the Secret Service were never used; on the forge's Linux run there was none (the first-run key file).
- **A real external identity provider.** The switch to one (F-390) was tested with the development issuer standing in as the provider. Not tried: a provider that wants an exact redirect port (the fixed-port setting exists; the bind and its fallback are not covered by a test), one that sends `email_verified` as a string, one that rotates keys during the proof, and any provider's real sign-in page reached through the one-time address for a web browser.
- **A real Ollama.** The look for a model on `127.0.0.1:11434` was tested against stand-ins; nothing was listening on the build machine.
- **WebKit and WebView2 beyond what the shell's smoke saw.** Every browser test is Chromium. The fixes for what the shell found in WKWebView (sign-in under the service worker, `confirm()`, printing, files that show instead of save, per-origin storage across a restart) were tested with WebKit's header shape in Go, with Chromium and with the stand-in; **they were not seen again in a window with the real server**, and nothing past the first-run profile form has been seen in the shell. The `download` change above is seen in Chromium only; whether WebKit saves the 404 as a failed download or shows it is not known.
- **The auto-updater.** (Written for v2.0; **UPDATES.md has the state of the updater now**: a first-run question, a daily check after a yes, a background download, Restart to Update. The committed configuration still has a placeholder key and an `.invalid` host, so a build from source says updates are not set up; only a release build for a tag has the key and the GitHub address.) Making and signing an update package with a throwaway key was seen; checking, downloading and installing one against a real release, and the restart for an update, were not.
- **A Linux desktop session with a real display and a real keychain.** Run 147 used Xvfb and no Secret Service; nobody has seen the app in a Linux window, on GTK or with WebKitGTK, and whether `localhost` resolves to `::1` first there is not known.
- **The `.deb` on a clean machine.** The job installs the package where the build's `-dev` packages are already installed, so the `Depends` line is not proved to suffice.
- **Restoring a server's backup into a desktop instance** (and the reverse, a desktop database run as a server). Tried after review in one direction with test databases and the production binary (`scriobh desktop use-local-profile --adopt-admin`); not tried with a real server backup with real data.
- **Anything that needs eyes or hands.** No screenshot of the app could be taken where it was built (the system refused screen capture), no menu item was clicked, no key pressed, no file dragged: how the window, the menus, the dialogs and the error window look, whether each menu item does what its handler says, whether the editor's chords reach the editor under the native Edit menu, the real save dialog, zoom, full screen, and File, Print… were not seen. Deep links and file associations were exercised through a second launch's arguments, not as the system delivers them.
- **Not covered by this gate.** The forge after the fixes (nothing ran there), the `deep` workflow, the whole-tree race pass (`make test-race`), a soak with desktop mode, the 1M-word bench and a bench of the desktop mode itself (none is set; the Go note measures a first start at 0.8 s and a later one at 0.2 s on this Mac), a release-mode build of the shell, and the image started to serve (the forge checks its user and its refusal of the stub only). `internal/db/testdata` has no fixture for v1.1 or v1.2 (the rehearsal covers them). `main` has two commits that `phase-8` does not (the v1.2 merge and a mirror test change); the merge into `main` was not made or tested.
- **The roadmap's exit criteria for v2.0 are not met**: a non-technical person installing a signed `.dmg`, `.msi` or AppImage on a clean machine and updating in place, with Windows required before v2.0 is called done. `2.0.0`, which the desktop bundle carries (`desktop/src-tauri/tauri.conf.json`, `Cargo.toml`, `package.json`, which agree), is the code's version, not a claim that they are.
- **Two question paths no browser test drives:** sign-out from the command palette with unsaved text (the question then opens the account menu while the palette closes), and the stash and alternatives buttons that now ask in the page (they use the call sign-out and the compile preset use, which are driven in Chromium).
- **Carried forward unchanged from v1.2:** the unverified list in the Phase 7 and 7.5 notes (nothing met a real outside system; no real book files; the quality of semantic search; no screen reader; Chromium only), the two-independent-users install criterion, and export records that are never removed.

Decided at the tag:

- **The window is the web app and nothing else.** The shell loads one origin, `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, and never `localhost` (the server refuses it as a Host, an Origin and a redirect address, and it can resolve to `::1` where nothing of ours listens). A page can reach nothing of the shell: no capability grants any command, no command, script or URI scheme is registered, and the dialog, shell, fs and http plugins are not linked (the dialog plugin replaces `window.confirm` with a function that returns a promise, which is truthy, so every "are you sure?" would be answered yes). Tauri's own bootstrap script is on every page it shows and has no switch; every call through it is refused, checked live (sixteen calls in the smoke). The shell's own error page can be read by a page script and holds nothing secret, pinned by a test that its folder holds exactly three files.
- **There is no lockout on the launch token.** The token is at least 128 bits, so counting wrong guesses protects nothing, and a limit any local program could use up would only keep the shell's own window from its cookie. A wrong token is a 404 that counts for nothing; the right one is accepted once; a cross-site request is refused without spending it. The cost: a window that loses its launch cookie (site data cleared) cannot sign in again until the application is restarted.
- **`/healthz` is a challenge and a proof.** The shell sends a fresh challenge with every request and counts a 200 only with an HMAC under a key derived from the launch token, compared in constant time, so the token is never sent to a different program that took a dead server's port. What is left, which the shell cannot close: the instant between the last check and the window's own request. A program run by the same user can read the server's environment and is outside what this defends.
- **The server survives `SIGPIPE` and stops when its input ends.** A shell killed outright closes the server's standard input, output and error together; in desktop mode the server asks to be told of `SIGPIPE`, so the log line that cannot be written no longer ends it before the clean stop (status 0, the write-ahead log checkpointed). The stop on standard input is the only clean stop on Windows and was never run there.
- **The keychain refuses to start rather than make a second key.** The server makes its own `secret.key` whenever it is given no key and finds no file, and credentials sealed with a key that then disappears are stranded. So where the keychain cannot be read, or holds something that is not a key, over a data folder that has a database and no key file, the shell does not start the server: the window says so and offers Restart. There is no "continue without the key" button. A key file in the data folder wins over a keychain key (said once); an existing key file is not copied into the keychain; the cost is that a person with a permanently broken keychain and no key file stays at the error window until they act.
- **`localhost` is refused, only `127.0.0.1`,** by the server as a Host, an Origin and a redirect address, and by the shell as an app address (a `localhost` link is an outside link, offered to the system browser).
- **The port is kept between launches** (`desktop/port`, a number from 1024 to 65535, read up to 16 bytes), so what the browser keeps by origin (unsaved drafts, held fields, the service worker's cache) survives a restart; a free port is taken, and said in the log, only when the old one is in use. What is lost when the port does change is what the browser kept for the old origin; everything the server saved is untouched.
- **A switch to an external provider binds the identity shown.** The provider is proved by a completed sign-in before the switch, never matched to the local profile by address; the switch form carries a fingerprint of the identity shown and `desktop.idp.switch` answers 409 unless it is the identity recorded now; the switch is made at the next start, with a typed confirmation, and the way back is the same. The provider's page is another site, which the window does not open, so the proving sign-in and the instance's sign-in go through an address for a web browser (one-time, five minutes, for the administrator who asked). `scriobh desktop use-local-profile` is outside the contract: with the application stopped it asks for the switch back, for a provider that is gone, and `--adopt-admin <email-or-id>` binds the local profile to one named administrator of a restored server database, by no inference.
- **No browser dialogs, anywhere.** `confirm()` returns false without asking in the desktop webview, so the four questions that used it ask in the page (`scriobh.confirm`, the `arm.js` panel); a repository test fails if `confirm(`, `alert(`, `prompt(` or `hx-confirm` returns. This applies to every deployment, and the browser scripts answer the panel, not a dialog.
- **A question before anything leaves the window, never a click guess.** Neither wry nor Tauri says whether a navigation came from a click, so every outside `http`, `https` or `mailto` address is offered with a native question showing the address, and only Open hands it to the system (a mail link loses any parameter that asks the mail program to attach a file); everything else is dropped. No dialog is ever put up inside a webview callback. Downloads are written by the webview into a private folder under a cleaned name, at most four at a time, and moved to where the person chose in the system's save dialog.
- **The shell never reads a file.** Opening a `.md` or `.epub` with the app, or dropping one on its icon, opens the import page and names the file; the person chooses it in the page's own input after signing in. A deep link names one of five page shapes, compared as written, never decoded.
- **Signing out is respected and sessions are unchanged.** A window that signs out sees the sign-in page and is not signed straight back in (a marker cookie; the launch cookie would otherwise do it at the next page load); sessions keep their lifetime (24 hours, 8 idle by default) and, when one ends, the next page load signs the person in again through the issuer without a form.
- **The child's environment is scrubbed and the server's name is its own.** Every inherited `SCRIOBH_*` name is removed from the server's environment before the shell sets the contract's four, so a variable left in a login profile cannot move the data folder, set a base URL or switch a mode; inside the app the server is `scriobh-desktop-server`, not `scriobh`, so that it cannot collide with a server package's file (the rename is unverified in a bundle).
- **Updates are off in the strongest sense** (the v2.0 decision; since refined, see "Desktop updates: decisions"): nothing is fetched before a yes, given to one first-run question or by a click on Check for Updates… that names the host; the signature must match the key built into the app, and `requireSignedVersion` is on, so an old genuinely signed release cannot be passed off as a newer one.
- **The first person is the administrator by the existing rule,** applied without its page (desktop mode clears the administrators' group and refuses to start when one is set); a profile with no email address signs in only for the issuer string `urn:scriobh:desktop-profile`, which only the embedded issuer produces; the address is stored NULL. No migration: the profile and the provider's state are two rows of `settings`, so a backup carries them.
- **Every request must name this instance** (desktop mode only): a `Host` that is not exactly the bound address is answered 421 before any handler, which turns a DNS-rebinding page away; the authorization endpoint judges a request by its site, not by its kind; the sign-in addresses' per-address limit does not apply on the embedded issuer, where every request has one address.
- **Nothing leaves the computer** with AI off: every dial site of the program tells an observer (a source test lists them), a whole session is run with every outbound connection observed, and `desktop.info` reports the count on a running production build.
- **Edition files that are gone are said on the page, and the download answers 404** (above), and **the upgrade rehearsal takes "no migration" as a case of its own**, so a later release that adds none is rehearsed the same way.

## Cover layouts: decisions

The design is [COVERS.md](COVERS.md). Decided by the owner on 17 November 2026, each as the design recommended (see its "Decisions"):

1. The layout as project data with a fixed Cover row in the tree (recommended), or cover pages as a new document kind.
2. "A standard page" as a picture placed on a page (designed), and whether a text side (a typeset back-cover blurb) is wanted too.
3. One layout per project (designed), or a separate ebook front.
4. `golang.org/x/image/draw` for scaling (recommended), or a hand-written filter.
5. Which printers' profiles to carry beyond KDP and IngramSpark paperback.

## Release notes for v2.2.0

The gate for the coming `v2.2.0` tag, run on `main` at `9e5261fc` ("Merge version-2.2") plus the commits of the branch `release-2.2-gate` (a worktree at `/private/tmp/scriobh-rel22`; nothing is pushed or tagged). `main` at that point had the cover layouts (migrations `0810_cover_layouts` and `1837_git_remote_cover`), the app version 2.2.0, the landing page and the release-workflow changes. Build machine: darwin/arm64, 16 cpus (the bench's machine class `darwin/arm64/16cpu`), Go 1.27.4, with the short `PATH` of the task. Everything below ran with `GOMAXPROCS=2` (and `-p 2` where `go` takes it) so that the gate did not take the machine over; that includes the bench (see below). This section covers the four steps of the gate (the upgrade rehearsal, the restore rehearsal, the bench, these notes) and `go vet`; it is not the whole of `make ci`.

What was run, on which tree:

- **`scripts/upgrade-rehearsal.sh --all`**, ten tags (v0.1, v0.2, v0.3, v0.3.5, v0.4, v0.5, v1.0, v1.1, v1.2, v2.0): all passed. First on a working tree with the rehearsal changes uncommitted (45 s), then again on the clean commit `5443f614` (43 s, 2 to 4 s a tag). v2.0 is the last forge release; the forge's tags are `v0.1` to `v1.2` and `v2.0`, and GitHub's `v2.1.1` is the same code as the forge's `main` at the time and is not a tag here. v2.0 to this build applies exactly two migrations (85 before, 87 after: `0810` and `1837`), takes one pre-migration copy, ends with a schema equal to a fresh install's (349 tables, indexes and triggers compared), answers all 69 rich fields unchanged, and rolls back by copying the pre-migration file over `scriobh.db`. The default list (no argument) was v0.1, v0.5, v1.0, v1.1 and v1.2, and did not name v2.0; it now does (`scripts/upgrade-rehearsal.sh`, `docs/OPERATIONS.md`).
- **The cover tables, in the upgrade rehearsal.** The rich dataset had no attachment and no reference, and one Git remote. It now writes through the old release a PNG uploaded to the book and named in a chapter and in a research note (so two real `attachment_refs` rows, one `doc` and one `research`, in the table `0810` rebuilds), and, from v1.1 (the first release with an audience), a second remote with audience `private` on the other book beside the first (which takes the old release's default, public). After the upgrade the rehearsal reads the database and requires that: every `attachment_refs` row and every attachment is exactly as the old release left it, the picture's two references among them; no attachment is `cover_clean`; `cover_layouts` is empty; and each remote has `include_cover` 1 if it is private and 0 if it is public. Result for each tag from v0.5 (the first with attachments): 2 rows before and after the rebuild; v0.5 and v1.0 (no audience, so every remote reads as private by `1830`): one private remote with `include_cover` 1; v1.1, v1.2 and v2.0: one private (1) and one public (0). The unit tests of the same migrations (`internal/db/cover_layouts_upgrade_test.go`) use synthetic rows on the v1.0 fixture; these are the real ones. v0.1 to v0.4 have no operation table and are rehearsed with the small dataset only (no pictures through the old API, no remotes), so for them the check covers an empty `attachment_refs`, no layout and no remote.
- **`scripts/restore-rehearsal.sh`**: passed, 99 s (first, tree with the edits uncommitted) and 94 s (clean `5443f614`). The populated instance had no cover; it now has a layout of sides with a front and a back picture (through the cover upload and `project.cover_layout.set`; the derived front, thumbnail and back were made and their bytes noted).
